Shock formation in 1D conservation laws II: Vanishing viscosity
John Anderson, Sanchit Chaturvedi, Cole Graham

Abstract
. We study the effects of weak viscosity on shock formation in 1D hyperbolic conservation laws. Given an inviscid solution that forms a nondegenerate shock, we add a small viscous regularization and study the limit as the viscosity vanishes. Using a matched asymptotic expansion, we determine the sharp rate of convergence in strong norms up to the time of inviscid shock formation, and we identify universal viscous behavior near the first singularity. To treat the complex interactions between multiple characteristics and the viscosity, we develop an approximation scheme that exploits a certain decoupling between shocking and nonshocking characteristics. Our analysis makes minimal assumptions on the equation, and in particular applies to the compressible Navier–Stokes equations with degenerate physical viscosity.



1.
Introduction

We study systems of viscous conservation laws in one spatial dimension:

(1.1)

\partial_{t}\psi^{(\nu)}+A(\psi^{(\nu)})\partial_{x}\psi^{(\nu)}=\nu\partial_{x}[B(\psi^{(\nu)})\partial_{x}\psi^{(\nu)}],\quad t,x\in\mathbb{R}.

The solution
\psi^{(\nu)}
is vector-valued with
N
components, and
A
and
B
are
N\times N
advection and diffusion matrices, respectively.
We index the solution by the viscosity
\nu
, which is small and nonnegative.

Conservation laws arise widely in continuum mechanics, modeling phenomena ranging from the motion of gases to the dynamics of elastic materials
[
32
]
.
For example, if
(\rho,u,E)
denotes the mass density, velocity, and energy density of a gas, the compressible Navier–Stokes(–Fourier) equations can be written in the form

(1.2)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\rho+\partial_{x}(\rho u)

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}(\rho u)+\partial_{x}(\rho u^{2}+P)

\displaystyle=\nu\partial_{x}(\mu\partial_{x}u),

\displaystyle\partial_{t}E+\partial_{x}(Eu+Pu)

\displaystyle=\nu\partial_{x}(\mu u\partial_{x}u+\kappa\partial_{x}\theta).

The pressure
P
and temperature
\theta
are functions of
(\rho,u,E)
, while the dynamic viscosity
\mu
and thermal conductivity
\kappa
depend on
\theta
.
These functions are material properties of the gas in question.
Here the small parameter
\nu>0
represents the Knudsen number.
The system (
1.2
) provides the primary motivation for this work.

In the inviscid setting (
\nu=0
), initially smooth solutions of (
1.1
) typically develop singularities in finite time.
Fast parcels of fluid can overtake slow, forming a shock singularity in which
\psi
remains bounded but
\partial_{x}\psi
blows up
[
50
,
43
]
.
In contrast, for certain viscous equations with
\nu>0
, the diffusion
B
can prevent shock formation, producing global-in-time classical solutions
[
45
,
57
]
.
Inviscid and viscous models thus differ qualitatively at the moment of shock formation.
This discrepancy raises important questions regarding the vanishing viscosity limit:

As a shock forms, does the viscous solution
\psi^{(\nu)}
converge to the inviscid solution
\psi^{(0)}
as
\nu\to 0
?
In which norms, at what rates?
What is the precise structure of the viscous regularization?

In a companion work
[
7
]
, we examine the detailed structure of inviscid shock formation.
Building on those results, we here assume
\psi^{(0)}
forms a nondegenerate shock at a time
t_{*}>0
, and study
\psi^{(\nu)}
as
\nu\to 0
on
[0,t_{*}]\times\mathbb{R}
.

Theorem 1.1
.

Under natural conditions on
A
,
B,
and
\psi^{(0)}
,
\psi^{(\nu)}
can be approximated to arbitrary precision and regularity on
[0,t_{*}]\times\mathbb{R}
using simpler building blocks.

Using this approximation, we can answer the questions raised above.
For example:

Corollary 1.2
.

Under the same conditions,
\|\psi^{(\nu)}-\psi^{(0)}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,t_{*}]\times\mathbb{R})}\leq C\nu^{1/4}
.
In Hölder spaces,
\psi^{(\nu)}\to\psi^{(0)}
in
L_{t}^{\infty}\mathcal{C}_{x}^{\alpha}
if and only if
\alpha<1/3
.
Near shock formation,
\psi^{(\nu)}
resembles a universal profile solving the viscous Burgers equation.

We make these statements precise in Theorem
1.3
and Corollaries
1.4
–
1.6
below.

Our approach flexibly handles a wide range of equations, including the compressible Navier–Stokes equations (
1.2
) with degenerate physical viscosity.
To our knowledge, this is the first study in strong norms of the vanishing viscosity limit up to shock formation for systems of conservation laws.

1.1.
Setup

We now describe our problem in greater detail.
The solution
\psi
takes values in an
N
-dimensional “state space”
\mathcal{V}\cong\mathbb{R}^{N}
, which represents a collection of physical variables such as mass, momentum, and energy density.
The advection
A\colon\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{V}\otimes\mathcal{V}^{*}
satisfies:

(H1)

A
is the derivative
\mathrm{D}f
of a smooth “flux function”
f\colon\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{V}
and has
N
distinct real eigenvalues.
Also, there exists a smooth “entropy-flux pair”
\eta,q\colon\mathcal{V}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\nabla\eta=(\nabla q)^{\top}A
and
\mathrm{D}^{2}\eta>0
as a quadratic form.

We use
\mathrm{D}
to denote the total derivative, so
\mathrm{D}^{2}\eta
is the Hessian of
\eta
.

Conservation laws arising in physical systems often admit strictly convex entropies, which play an important role in the theory of weak solutions
[
32
]
.
Here, they prove beneficial for the well-posedness of the viscous problem (
1.1
) with
\nu>0
.
To this end, we assume that the diffusion
B\colon\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{V}\otimes\mathcal{V}^{*}
satisfies:

(1)

B
has constant nullity
s\leq N-1
, and there is a fixed basis for
\mathcal{V}
in which the first
s
rows of
B
vanish.
Moreover, there is a constant
c>0
such that

(1.3)

\mathrm{D}^{2}\eta(B\xi,\xi)\geq c\left|B\xi\right|^{2}\quad\text{for all }\xi\in\mathcal{V}.

Here we interpret
\mathrm{D}^{2}\eta
as a bilinear form.

This condition ensures that (
1.1
) is locally well-posed, as shown by Serre
[
68
]
, building on seminal work of Kawashima and Shizuta
[
46
,
70
]
.
Many physically-motivated diffusions naturally satisfy
1
.
For example, in the Navier–Stokes equations, physical viscosity vanishes in the mass equation and dissipates the entropy density
\eta
in the sense of (
1.3
).

Next, we place conditions on the inviscid solution
\psi^{(0)}
.
For convenience, we use the same initial condition
\psi^{(\nu)}(0,\,\cdot\,)=\mathring{\psi}
for all
\nu>0
and assume:

(1)

\mathring{\psi}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};\mathcal{V})
,
\mathring{\psi}|_{(-\infty,-R]}\equiv c_{-}
, and
\mathring{\psi}|_{[R,\infty)}\equiv c_{+}
for some
R>0,

c_{\pm}\in\mathcal{V}
.

In fact, Corollary
1.2
only requires a large but finite degree of regularity.
In general, if
\mathring{\psi}\in\mathcal{C}^{k}
for large
k
, Theorem
1.1
will hold to some large but finite degree of approximation depending on
k
.
Tracking this dependence is cumbersome, so we assume
\mathring{\psi}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
for simplicity.
Similarly, the assumption that
\mathring{\psi}
is constant outside a compact set is a mere technical convenience.
Our analysis is local, so we could treat much more general behaviors at infinity.

We are interested in the local structure of
\psi^{(\nu)}
near shock formation, so we assume
\psi^{(0)}
forms a first isolated shock at
(t_{*},x_{*})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
.
Generically, it should form in a single characteristic, meaning
\partial_{x}\psi
diverges in one eigenspace of
A
but remains bounded in the rest.
The corresponding “shocking” eigenvalue is the spatial velocity of the incipient shock.
Using gauge freedom, we shift into a co-moving frame so this velocity becomes zero.
More broadly, using a Galilean transformation of spacetime and an affine change in state space, we can arrange:

(1)

x_{*}=0
,
\psi^{(0)}(t_{*},0)=0
, and there is a basis
\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}\}
of
\mathcal{V}
in which
A(0)
is diagonal, the shock forms in
e_{1}
, and
A_{1}^{1}(0)=0
.

Here we use
A_{i}^{j}
to denote
e_{j}^{\top}Ae_{i}
.
Because
A(0)
is diagonal,
A_{1}^{1}(0)
is the instantaneous velocity of shock formation at
(t_{*},x_{*}=0)
, set to zero.
We eventually shift time so that
t_{*}=0
, but for the sake of exposition, we keep
t_{*}>0
in the introduction.

We next require the diffusion to act nontrivially on the shocking component:

(1)

B_{1}^{1}(0)>0
.

Thus the diffusion matrix
B
may be degenerate, but it must directly influence the shocking characteristic at shock formation.
For example, in the Navier–Stokes equations (
1.2
), physical viscosity vanishes in the first equation, but acts nontrivially on the fluid characteristics that form shocks.

We now turn to the detailed structure of generic inviscid shock formation.
In the scalar case (
N=1
), shocks generally form as in Figure
1
.

Figure 1.
Shock formation in a scalar conservation law.
As time evolves, the profile progressively steepens (blue, then purple) toward a preshock (red) at time
t_{*}
.

The spatial profile
\psi^{(0)}(t,\,\cdot\,)
steepens as
t
approaches
t_{*}
and forms a cusp-like “preshock” with infinite slope at
t=t_{*}
.
A true shock discontinuity only develops after time
t_{*}
.
Solutions must then be interpreted in a weak sense, and selection principles such as entropy conditions are required for uniqueness
[
32
]
.
We will not explore this regime here, as we restrict our attention to
t\leq t_{*}
.

Near the nascent shock, scalar solutions
\psi^{(0)}
generically resemble a self-similar inverse cubic profile
\mathfrak{u}
satisfying

x=a(t_{*}-t)\mathfrak{u}+b\mathfrak{u}^{3}

for constants
a,b\neq 0
of the same sign.
(In the case of the Burgers equation, see the work
[
22
]
of the second and third authors).
Figure
1
depicts the self-similar profile
\mathfrak{u}
.
Its character becomes most evident at the preshock
t=t_{*}
, when
\mathfrak{u}
develops a cubic cusp with infinite slope at the origin.
We note that this behavior is generic but not exhaustive.
For certain initial conditions of positive codimension, the first shock can be more degenerate with higher inverse degree
[
31
]
.
Solutions can even degenerate on an entire open interval, producing a discontinuity at
t=t_{*}
.

Returning to the vector setting with arbitrary
N
, we assume the first component resembles
\mathfrak{u}
near
(t_{*},0)
, the other components are more regular, and all admit asymptotic expansions in functions that scale like
\mathfrak{u}
:

(H6
′
)

​(Informal) Near
(t_{*},0),

\psi^{(0)}
admits an expansion in terms that scale like
\mathfrak{u}
,
\mathfrak{u}^{2}
,
\mathfrak{u}^{3}
, etc.
The leading term is
\mathfrak{u}e_{1}
.

This nondegenerate formation expansion is our key hypothesis.
For a precise statement, see
(H6)
in Section
2.2
below.
Loosely,
(H6′)
provides a fractional Taylor series for
\psi^{(0)}
with terms obeying the natural inverse-cubic scaling of generic shock formation.
In fact, the cubic nature of
\psi^{(0)}
in
(H6′)
implies that the shocking eigenvalue is genuinely nonlinear in the sense of Lax
[
50
]
.
For this reason, we need not state genuine nonlinearity as a separate hypothesis.
For more details, see Section
2.2
below.

In
[
7
]
, we show that
(H6′)
is satisfied for an open set of initial data.
In particular, it holds for small perturbations of nondegenerate “simple waves,” in which
\partial_{x}\psi
occupies a single
A
-eigenspace.
We expect this scenario to be generic, in the sense that shocking solutions should typically resemble a nondegenerate simple wave shortly before shock formation.
We leave this question to future investigation.

1.2.
Matched expansions

As indicated in Theorem
1.1
, we analyze
\psi^{(\nu)}
by assembling an approximate solution
{\psi}^{\textnormal{app}}
of (
1.1
) from solutions of simpler problems.
This amounts to a matched asymptotic expansion linking an outer expansion away from shock formation with an inner expansion near
(t_{*},0)
.
To be more precise, let
\mathfrak{e}(t,x)\coloneqq[(t_{*}-t)^{2}+x^{2}]^{1/2}
denote the Euclidean distance to the preshock
(t_{*},0)
in spacetime.
We choose an exponent
\beta\in(0,1/2)
and use a conventional expansion in
\nu
where
\mathfrak{e}\geq\nu^{\beta}
:

(1.4)

\psi^{(\nu)}\approx{\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}}+\nu{\psi}_{1}^{\textnormal{out}}+\nu^{2}{\psi}_{2}^{\textnormal{out}}+\ldots

The leading term
{\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}}
is simply the inviscid solution
\psi^{(0)}
, and thus solves a hyperbolic conservation law.
We study the precise structure of
\psi^{(0)}
in our companion paper
[
7
]
.
The higher-order correctors
{\psi}_{k}^{\textnormal{out}}
solve linearizations of the conservation law about
\psi^{(0)}
, so their behavior is closely tied to that of
\psi^{(0)}
.

The outer expansion (
1.4
) is valid where
\mathfrak{e}\geq\nu^{\beta}
because the viscosity
\nu\partial_{x}(B\partial_{x}\psi)
remains perturbative in this region: it is much smaller than the other terms in (
1.1
).
This breaks down near
(t_{*},0)
, so we employ a different inner expansion where
\mathfrak{e}\leq\nu^{\beta}
based on proximity to the preshock.
Following
[
22
]
, we introduce blow-up coordinates

(1.5)

T\coloneqq\nu^{-1/2}(t-t_{*}),\quad X\coloneqq\nu^{-3/4}x,\quad\Psi=\nu^{-1/4}\psi

and propose an inner expansion of the form

(1.6)

\Psi^{(\nu)}={\Psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}+\nu^{1/4}{\Psi}_{1}^{\textnormal{in}}+\nu^{1/2}{\Psi}_{2}^{\textnormal{in}}+\ldots

The cubic shock
\mathfrak{u}
is preserved under the blow-up (
1.5
), while higher powers of
\mathfrak{u}
are suppressed.
In light of
(H6′)
, the shocking component
e_{1}
dominates the inner expansion:
{\Psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}
lies solely in the shocking component and is thus effectively scalar.
Moreover, (
1.5
) preserves the leading behavior of each term in (
1.1
), so
{\Psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}
solves the scalar viscous Burgers equation.
The higher correctors
{\Psi}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}
solve linear equations that conveniently decouple.
Nonshocking components solve constant-coefficient transport equations, and shocking components solve linearizations of viscous Burgers.

To link the two expansions, we choose data for
{\Psi}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}
in the distant
T
-past so that partial sums of the outer and inner expansions coincide.
At leading order,
{\Psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}
resembles the cubic profile
\mathfrak{u}
at infinity in
(T,X)
.
The second and third authors constructed and extensively studied this solution of viscous Burgers in
[
22
]
.
The higher correctors are simple to analyze once the leading part
{\Psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}
is well understood.

1.3.
Main results

Once we have constructed the outer (
1.4
) and inner (
1.6
) expansions in their respective domains, we truncate them to some order
K
and use a partition of unity to glue them into an approximate solution
{\psi}_{K}^{\textnormal{app}}
.
Using local well-posedness estimates, we show that
{\psi}_{K}^{\textnormal{app}}
approximates
\psi^{(\nu)}
.

Theorem 1.3
.

Assume
(H1)
–
(H6)
.
Then there exists
\overline{\nu}>0
such that for all
\nu\in(0,\overline{\nu}]
, (
1.1
) admits a solution
\psi^{(\nu)}
on
[0,t_{*}]\times\mathbb{R}
.
Moreover, for any
s,p\geq 0
, there exist
K\in\mathbb{N}
and
C>0
such that for all
\nu\in(0,\overline{\nu}]
,

\|\psi^{(\nu)}-{\psi}_{K}^{\textnormal{app}}\|_{H^{s}([0,t_{*}]\times\mathbb{R})}\leq C\nu^{p}.

We note that large-data global well-posedness is presently open for the full Navier–Stokes–Fourier system (
1.2
), so even the existence of a solution to time
t_{*}
cannot be taken for granted.

The approximate solution
{\psi}_{K}^{\textnormal{app}}
incorporates solutions to a hyperbolic conservation law, the viscous Burgers equation, linear advection equations, and linear advection-diffusion equations.
Each is considerably simpler to analyze than the original nonlinear parabolic-hyperbolic system (
1.1
).
This justifies Theorem
1.1
.

To illustrate the utility of this approximation, we present a few applications based on the leading behavior of the outer and inner expansions.
First, we determine the sharp
L^{\infty}
-rate of the vanishing viscosity limit.
For systems, this is the first result of its kind in norms stronger than
L^{1}
.

Corollary 1.4
.

Assume
(H1)
–
(H6)
.
Then there exists
C\geq 1
such that for all
\nu\in(0,\overline{\nu}]
,

C^{-1}\nu^{1/4}\leq\|\psi^{(\nu)}-\psi^{(0)}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,t_{*}]\times\mathbb{R})}\leq C\nu^{1/4}.

We can also track convergence in higher regularity, which reveals a difference between the shocking and nonshocking components.
Writing
\psi=(\psi^{1},\ldots,\psi^{N})
in the basis from
1
, let
\sigma\coloneqq\psi^{1}
and
\omega\coloneqq(\psi^{2},\ldots,\psi^{N})
denote the shocking and nonshocking components, respectively.

Corollary 1.5
.

Assume
(H1)
–
(H6)
.
Then
\sigma^{(\nu)}\to\sigma^{(0)}
in
L_{t}^{\infty}\mathcal{C}_{x}^{\alpha}([0,t_{*}]\times\mathbb{R})
as
\nu\to 0
if and only if
\alpha<1/3
, while
\omega^{(\nu)}\to\omega^{(0)}
in
L_{t}^{\infty}\mathcal{C}_{x}^{\beta}
if
\beta<2/3
.

The regularity threshold
1/3
is naturally tied to the leading inverse-cubic behavior of the shocking component
\sigma^{(0)}
from
(H6′)
.
However, the nonshocking threshold
2/3
is somewhat surprising, as there are natural hyperbolic models in which
\omega^{(0)}
is
\mathcal{C}_{x}^{1,1/3}
.
Nonetheless, we find that the viscous perturbation
\omega^{(\nu)}-\omega^{(0)}
is generally only bounded in
\mathcal{C}_{x}^{2/3}
, due to the off-diagonal influence of
\nu\partial_{x}^{2}\sigma^{(\nu)}
on
\omega^{(\nu)}
.
We detail this effect in Remark
8.1
below.
So while the convergence of
\omega^{(\nu)}
to
\omega^{(0)}
may be improved for certain special systems, Corollary
1.5
is sharp in general.

Finally, we show that
\psi^{(\nu)}
has a universal viscous character near shock formation.
Recall the blown-up solution
\Psi^{(\nu)}(T,X)
defined through (
1.5
).

Corollary 1.6
.

Assume
(H1)
–
(H6)
.
Then there exists a unique solution
U
of the viscous Burgers equation such that, modulo scaling symmetries,
\Psi^{(\nu)}\to Ue_{1}
locally uniformly in
(T,X)
as
\nu\to 0
.

That is, sufficiently near shock formation,
\psi^{(\nu)}
resembles a universal profile solving viscous Burgers.

Collectively, Corollaries
1.4
–
1.6
provide a precise description of the nature of viscous regularization near nondegenerate shock formation.
Of the hypotheses
(H1)
–
(H6)
, the first five hold for many natural dissipative conservation laws, including the Navier–Stokes–Fourier equations (
1.2
) (in the absence of vacuum) and certain regimes in elasticity and magnetohydrodynamics.
On its face, the final assumption
(H6)
of a nondegenerate formation expansion may seem rather strong.
However, in
[
7
]
we show that it holds on an open set of initial data, and as described above, we expect it to hold generically.

We also expect our results to extend to 1D reductions of higher-dimensional systems.
For example, we anticipate that Corollaries
1.4
–
1.6
hold for the Navier–Stokes regularization of the Euler equations under azimuthal symmetry considered in
[
61
]
.
This is not immediate, as radial coordinates introduce variable coefficients in (
1.1
), which we do not explicitly consider.
Nonetheless, our approach is essentially local, so (smooth) variable coefficients should pose no difficulty.

In a similar vein, while we restrict our attention to strictly hyperbolic models, this is mostly a matter of convenience.
In fact, our analysis immediately applies to systems in which the shocking eigenvalue is simple, but others are repeated.
Moreover, we believe the overall approach should extend to shocking eigenvalues with multiplicity, which arise naturally in settings such as magnetohydrodynamics.
In this situation, the leading inner expansion would involve a vector-valued form of viscous Burgers.
We believe energy estimates could be used to treat this inner equation and recover our main results.
We leave this fascinating question to future inquiry.

1.4.
Related work

Our examination of vanishing viscosity and shock formation fits within a very broad investigation of singularities and regularization in conservation laws.
Here, we discuss a modest selection of works that strike us as particularly relevant to the present effort.
Our companion paper
[
7
]
includes a similar survey of hyperbolic results, so we here place greater emphasis on the viscous setting.

1.4.1.
Well-posedness

As shock formation indicates, the well-posedness of conservation laws is itself a subtle matter.
For hyperbolic conservation laws in one dimension, Glimm famously constructed global weak solutions for small-BV data
[
37
]
.
This inspired a number of other compactness-based constructions
[
34
,
10
,
8
,
66
]
and uniqueness results
[
12
,
14
,
15
,
24
,
13
]
.
For more details, see
[
11
]
.
In contrast, there is no general global theory for inviscid conservation laws in higher dimensions, and in fact such equations need not be well-posed in BV
[
64
]
.

In the viscous setting, degenerate diffusion greatly complicates well-posedness.
The seminal work of Kawashima and Shizuta
[
46
,
70
,
47
]
identified conditions on the diffusion matrix
B
that ensure local well-posedness.
Serre has since refined these conditions
[
69
,
68
]
, and our hypothesis
1
makes use of this framework.
For long times, small
H^{2}
data yield global solutions in quite general systems
[
46
,
45
]
, while rougher data can be propagated weakly in the isentropic Navier–Stokes equations
[
53
]
.
Moreover, Mellet and Vasseur have shown that isentropic Navier–Stokes admits global-in-time strong solutions started from
large

H^{1}
data
[
57
]
.
As mentioned earlier, there is no large-data global theory for viscous conservation laws in the generality considered here, so the existence until time
t_{*}
in Theorem
1.3
is new for many systems, including Navier–Stokes–Fourier (
1.2
).

1.4.2.
Shock formation

The study of hyperbolic shock formation has a long history dating back to work of Riemann
[
65
]
on 1D scalar conservation laws.
This was extended by Lax
[
50
]
to
2\times 2
systems and John
[
43
]
to more general systems with small data.
In higher dimensions, John
[
42
]
and Sideris
[
72
]
showed that small smooth data break down, and Alinhac provided a more detailed account of nondegenerate shock formation
[
5
,
4
,
3
]
.
Christodoulou pioneered a powerful geometric approach to shock formation
[
30
]
, which has since been extended to resolve a number of challenging problems in compressible fluids
[
54
,
55
,
1
]
.

A parallel program of Buckmaster, Shkoller, and Vicol is particularly relevant to the present work.
In
[
18
,
20
,
19
]
, the authors use modulation theory to precisely describe the nature of nondegenerate shock formation in compressible Euler.
Their analysis identifies Burgers-like behavior at leading order, and makes use of a multidimensional variant of the inverse cubic profile
\mathfrak{u}
studied here.
Buckmaster and Iyer have also constructed unstable modes of shock formation in 2D compressible Euler
[
17
]
.

Beyond shock formation, there has been much recent interest in the construction of maximal developments for hyperbolic equations.
These capture the largest region in which classical solutions make sense, and their study seems to require a detailed understanding of singularity (e.g., shock) formation.
We refer the reader to
[
30
,
1
,
71
,
7
]
for more information in this direction.

1.4.3.
Vanishing viscosity for general data

The convergence of viscous conservation laws to their inviscid counterparts as
\nu\to 0
has received a great deal of attention, beginning with pivotal work of Hopf
[
41
]
, Oleinik
[
63
]
, and Kružkov
[
49
]
on 1D scalar equations.
Vanishing viscosity for systems is considerably more delicate.
In the case of isentropic Euler, equations with nondegenerate “artificial” viscosity can be used to construct
L^{\infty}
solutions as subsequential limits
[
33
,
25
,
52
]
.
In general systems, the seminal work of Bianchini and Bressan
[
9
]
established the vanishing viscosity limit for small-BV data, again with artificial viscosity.

For more delicate
physical
viscosity (which does not act on the density), G.-Q. Chen and Perepelitsa
[
26
]
used
L^{2}
-methods to prove the convergence of Navier–Stokes to isentropic Euler for finite-energy data.
To treat small-BV solutions with physical viscosity, Kang, Leger, and Vasseur pioneered the method of “
a
-contraction with shifts”
[
51
,
44
]
.
In recent breakthrough work with G. Chen, Kang and Vasseur used this approach to establish vanishing viscosity convergence and stability for barotropic Euler
[
23
]
.

1.4.4.
Vanishing viscosity for developed shocks

The above works treat vanishing viscosity limits for general data in various classes.
In parallel, there is an extensive literature on the viscous regularization of hyperbolic solutions with fully developed shocks.
This begins with Hopf
[
41
]
and Gilbarg
[
36
]
, who constructed viscous shock waves for the Burgers and Navier–Stokes–Fourier equations (
1.2
), respectively.
In the context of vanishing viscosity, such waves should appear in an inner layer near the shock, where viscosity is non-perturbative.
Goodman and Xin developed this intuition into a complete matched asymptotic expansion for a sequence of (artificially) viscous solutions converging to a solution of a general hyperbolic system with a small, isolated shock
[
38
]
.

This groundbreaking effort inspired much subsequent work, including our own use of a matched expansion.
Yu
[
78
]
explored the early time layer in which viscosity smooths an initial discontinuity, and Rousset
[
67
]
extended
[
38
]
to large shocks satisfying certain stability criteria.
In remarkable work in multiple dimensions, Guès, Métivier, Williams, and Zumbrun
[
40
,
39
]
constructed viscous expansions about shocks satisfying the Majda stability conditions
[
56
]
.
In a different direction, Tang and Teng used a matched approximation to identify the vanishing viscosity rate in
L_{x}^{1}
about piecewise-smooth solutions of scalar conservation laws
[
73
]
.
Wang subsequently showed that their bound is achieved in Burgers at shock formation and the corners of rarefaction waves
[
74
]
.

The complete expansions developed above all concern fully developed shocks.
In the context of the present paper, they would apply after time
t_{*}+\tau
for some fixed
\tau>0
, which allows a full discontinuous shock to develop out of the preshock at time
t_{*}
.
In contrast, as in
[
22
]
, we focus on the times
[0,t_{*}]
leading up to the preshock.
This leaves the period
[t_{*},t_{*}+\tau]
of
shock development
open.
It remains a compelling subject for future work.

1.4.5.
Singularities despite regularization

In equations like ours, the addition of any amount of positive viscosity suffices to prevent the onset of singularities (here, shocks).
However, weaker forms of regularization may fail to prevent singularities.
Such blow-up
despite
regularization has attracted a great deal of recent attention.

In one dimension, the fractional Burgers and KdV equations are notable examples
[
75
]
.
These involve fractional degrees of dissipation or dispersion that can be tuned to exhibit a number of behaviors, including singularity formation under weak regularization.
In fractional (or “fractal”) Burgers, the critical degree of regularization separating blow-up and well-posedness was independently established in
[
48
,
35
,
2
]
.
The precise nature of generic singularities is now understood for equations spanning fractal Burgers
[
29
]
, Burgers–Hilbert
[
76
,
77
]
, and many other dissipative and dispersive models
[
62
]
.
If diffusion acts only in an additional transverse direction, Burgers can likewise form a shock admitting a precise description
[
31
]
.

In higher dimensions, even classical diffusion may be insufficiently strong to prevent singularities.
In a remarkable effort, Merle, Raphaël, Rodnianski, and Szeftel have constructed solutions of the 3D barotropic Euler and Navier–Stokes equations exhibiting finite-time implosion
[
59
,
60
]
.
These results have spectacular applications
[
58
]
and have inspired a number of other impressive works
[
16
,
21
,
28
,
27
]
.

1.5.
Organization

In Section
2
, we present an overview of our proof strategy.
We describe the conventional outer expansion in Section
3
.
In Section
4
, we develop the more delicate inner expansion and an auxiliary “grid” expansion that aids the gluing of the outer to the inner (see Section
2
for more details).
The core of our analysis is Section
5
, where we control the terms of the grid expansion.
Using these bounds, we demonstrate close matching between the grid and both the outer (Section
6
) and inner expansions (Section
7
).
Finally, we construct an approximate solution and prove our main results in Section
8
.

2.
Proof overview

In this section, we outline our construction of an approximate solution to (
1.1
).
This yields Theorem
1.3
, and Corollaries
1.4
–
1.6
follow from the nature of the approximate solution.

2.1.
Notation

Throughout, we use
f\lesssim_{\alpha}g
to indicate that
\left|f\right|\leq C(\alpha)g
for some constant
C(\alpha)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
that may depend on a parameter
\alpha
but is independent of the variables
t,x,
and
\nu.

We emphasize the absolute value
\left|f\right|
in our convention, which allows us to treat sign-indefinite functions
f
.
We write
\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}(g)
to denote an implicit function
f
satisfying
f\lesssim_{\alpha}g
.
We write
f\asymp_{\alpha}g
if
f\lesssim_{\alpha}g
and
g\lesssim_{\alpha}f
.
Finally, we occasionally write
f\ll g
if
f/g\to 0
in a given limit.

2.2.
Homogeneous expansion

To simplify scaling relative to the location of shock formation, we shift time so that our Cauchy problem begins at a negative time
t_{0}<0
and the first singularity forms at the origin in spacetime.
That is,

(2.1)

\psi^{(\nu)}(t_{0},\,\cdot\,)=\mathring{\psi}\quad\text{and}\quad t_{*}=0.

We study our solutions
\psi^{(\nu)}
on the spacetime strip
[t_{0},0]\times\mathbb{R}
.

Let
\mathfrak{e}
denote the spacetime Euclidean distance to the origin:

(2.2)

\mathfrak{e}(t,x)\coloneqq(\left|t\right|^{2}+\left|x\right|^{2})^{1/2}.

As described in Section
1.2
, the viscosity is perturbative in the “outer region” where
\mathfrak{e}\geq\nu^{\beta}
for a certain exponent
\beta\in(0,1/2)
to be chosen in the course of the proof.
In this region, we approximate
\psi^{(\nu)}
with the conventional outer expansion (
1.4
) in integer powers of
\nu
.
The leading term
{\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}}
coincides with the inviscid solution
\psi^{(0)}
, and the higher correctors
{\psi}_{k}^{\textnormal{out}}
solve linearizations of the the inviscid equation about
{\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}}
(Proposition
3.1
).
Thus, the nature of the expansion is determined by the behavior of its leading part.

In
(H6′)
, we assume
{\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}}
is dominated near the origin by the inverse cubic

(2.3)

x=a\left|t\right|\mathfrak{u}+b\mathfrak{u}^{3}.

We now define a “cubic distance”

(2.4)

\mathfrak{d}\coloneqq(\left|t\right|+3a^{-1}b\mathfrak{u}^{2})^{1/2},

which measures proximity to the origin in the intrinsic scaling of the nondegenerate cubic preshock
\mathfrak{u}
.
The coefficients are chosen so that
\mathfrak{d}=\mathfrak{m}^{-1/2}
for

(2.5)

\mathfrak{m}\coloneqq a\partial_{x}\mathfrak{u}=\frac{1}{\left|t\right|+3a^{-1}b\mathfrak{u}^{2}}.

For future use, we record some relationships between these functions.

Lemma 2.1
.

We have
t=-\mathfrak{m}^{-1}+3a^{-1}b\mathfrak{u}^{2}
,
\partial_{x}\mathfrak{u}=a^{-1}\mathfrak{m}
, and
\partial_{t}\mathfrak{u}=a\mathfrak{u}\partial_{x}\mathfrak{u}=\mathfrak{u}\mathfrak{m}
.
Moreover, in
\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}\times\mathbb{R}
,

(i)

|\mathfrak{u}|\asymp|x|^{\frac{1}{3}}
for
\left|t\right|\lesssim|x|^{\frac{2}{3}}
and
|\mathfrak{u}|\asymp|x||t|^{-1}
for
|x|^{\frac{2}{3}}\lesssim\left|t\right|
;

(ii)

\mathfrak{m}\lesssim|\mathfrak{u}||x|^{-1}
;

(iii)

\mathfrak{d}\asymp|t|^{\frac{1}{2}}
for
|x|^{\frac{2}{3}}\lesssim\left|t\right|
and
\mathfrak{d}\asymp|x|^{\frac{1}{3}}
for
|x|^{\frac{2}{3}}\gtrsim\left|t\right|
.

This consolidates Lemmas 8.4 and 8.5 in
[
7
]
.

The functions
\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{m},
and
\mathfrak{d}
are self-similar under an anisotropic scaling of time and space.
We introduce a nonstandard notion of homogeneity to capture this structure:

Definition 2.1
.

Given
r\in\mathbb{R}
, a function
f\colon\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is
r
-homogeneous
if

f(\lambda^{2}t,\lambda^{3}x)=\lambda^{r}f(t,x)\quad\text{for all }\lambda>0,t\leq 0,\text{ and }x\in\mathbb{R}.

Under this definition,
\mathfrak{u}
and
\mathfrak{d}
are 1-homogeneous while
\mathfrak{m}
is
-2
-homogeneous.

If an
r
-homogeneous function
f
is continuous away from the origin, it satisfies
\left|f\right|\leq C(f)\mathfrak{d}^{r}
for
C(f)\coloneqq\sup_{\{\mathfrak{d}=1\}}\left|f\right|
.
Thus
\mathfrak{d}
and its powers conveniently control homogeneous expressions.
With this in mind, we state the precise form of
(H6′)
:

(H6)

There exists a sequence
(\overline{\psi}_{\ell})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
of
\mathcal{V}
-valued
(\ell+1)
-homogeneous functions such that
\overline{\psi}_{\ell}
is a polynomial in
(t,\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{m})
,
\psi_{0}=\mathfrak{u}e_{1}
, and for all
L,m,n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

\partial_{t}^{m}\partial_{x}^{n}\psi^{(0)}=\partial_{t}^{m}\partial_{x}^{n}\sum_{\ell=0}^{L}\overline{\psi}_{\ell}+\mathcal{O}_{L,m,n}(\mathfrak{d}^{L-2m-3n+2}).

Note that
\partial_{t}
and
\partial_{x}
reduce homogeneity by
2
and
3
, respectively.
This explains the exponent in the error term above.

We now consider some consequences of
(H6)
.
Recalling the notation
\psi=(\sigma,\omega)
for the shocking and nonshocking components,
(H6)
asserts that
\sigma
is much larger that
\omega
near the origin.
We therefore project (
1.1
) onto
e_{1}
and extract the leading part of each term.
Because
A_{1}^{1}(0)=0
, we linearize and keep only the shocking portion:
A_{1}^{1}(\psi)\approx\partial_{1}A_{1}^{1}(0)\sigma
, where
\partial_{1}
denotes the derivative in
\mathcal{V}
in the direction
e_{1}
.
Taking
\nu=0,
(
1.1
) approximately reduces to the scalar Burgers equation:

(2.6)

\partial_{t}\sigma+\partial_{1}A_{1}^{1}(0)\sigma\partial_{x}\sigma\approx 0.

Substituting the leading part
\mathfrak{u}
of
\sigma
, we find

(2.7)

\partial_{t}\mathfrak{u}+\partial_{1}A_{1}^{1}(0)\mathfrak{u}\partial_{x}\mathfrak{u}=0.

Substituting (
2.3
), we find
a=-\partial_{1}A_{1}^{1}(0)
.
The former is nonzero by hypothesis, so
\partial_{1}A_{1}^{1}(0)\neq 0
.

Now if
\lambda_{(1)}
denotes the eigenvalue of
A
with eigenvector
e_{1}
at
0\in\mathcal{V}
, we can check that

(2.8)

\partial_{1}A_{1}^{1}(0)=\partial_{1}\lambda_{(1)}(0)=-a.

That is,
\partial_{1}A_{1}^{1}(0)
is the derivative of the shocking eigenvalue in the direction of the shocking eigenvector.
Thus
\partial_{1}A_{1}^{1}(0)\neq 0
is equivalent to Lax’s
genuine nonlinearity

[
50
]
for
\lambda_{(1)}
.
Hence the genuine nonlinearity of
\lambda_{(1)}
follows from the leading cubic behavior in
(H6)
.

2.3.
Inner scaling

To determine where the viscosity is non-perturbative, we reduce (
1.1
) to its shocking component as in (
2.6
), but now keep
\nu>0
to obtain the
viscous
Burgers equation:

(2.9)

\partial_{t}\sigma+\partial_{1}A_{1}^{1}(0)\sigma\partial_{x}\sigma\approx\nu B_{1}^{1}(0)\partial_{x}^{2}\sigma.

We observe that
B_{1}^{1}
is the only entry of
B
appearing at leading order, and we have assumed
B_{1}^{1}(0)>0
in
1
.
Thus at leading order we have a scalar conservation law with nondegenerate viscosity.
Using
\mathfrak{u}
as an ansatz for
\sigma
, we can formally check that the diffusion
\nu\partial_{x}^{2}\sigma
begins to dominate the advection
\sigma\partial_{x}\sigma
where
\left|t\right|\lesssim\nu^{1/2}
and
\left|x\right|\lesssim\nu^{3/4}
.
There
\left|\mathfrak{u}\right|\lesssim\nu^{1/4}
, which motivates our definition of the inner coordinates

(2.10)

T\coloneqq\nu^{-1/2}t,\quad X\coloneqq\nu^{-3/4}x,\quad\text{and}\quad\Psi\coloneqq\nu^{-1/4}\psi.

Writing
\Psi=(\Sigma,\Omega)
for the scaled shocking a nonshocking components, (
2.9
) becomes

(2.11)

\partial_{T}\Sigma+\partial_{1}A_{1}^{1}(0)\Sigma\partial_{X}\Sigma\approx B_{1}^{1}(0)\partial_{X}^{2}\Sigma.

This illustrates the utility of the inner coordinates: in these variables, the non-perturbative nature of the diffusion becomes evident.

Precisely, the diffusion is leading order in the “diffusive region” where
\left|t\right|\lesssim\nu^{1/2}
and
\left|x\right|\lesssim\nu^{3/4}
, or equivalently,
\mathfrak{d}\lesssim\nu^{1/4}
.
However, the system (
1.1
) presents an important complication relative to a scalar conservation law like Burgers.
The diffusion acts primarily in the anisotropic region
\{\left|t\right|\lesssim\nu^{1/2},\left|x\right|\lesssim\nu^{3/4}\}=\{\mathfrak{d}\lesssim\nu^{1/4}\}
, but transverse nonshocking characteristics sweep its effects to the larger isotropic region
\{\left|t\right|,\left|x\right|\lesssim\nu^{1/2}\}=\{\mathfrak{e}\lesssim\nu^{1/2}\}
, as in Figure
2
.
In hyperbolic terminology, the diffusive zone
\{\mathfrak{d}\lesssim\nu^{1/4}\}
is not causally closed, so its effects are felt throughout its much larger causal closure.
In particular, the outer expansion (
1.4
) is invalid where
\mathfrak{e}\lesssim\nu^{1/2}
, although the diffusion itself remains relatively small in much of this region.
For this reason, we use the outer expansion where
\mathfrak{e}\geq\nu^{\beta}
and the inner where
\mathfrak{e}\leq\nu^{\beta}
for some
\beta<1/2.

Figure 2.
Due to transverse advection, the anisotropic diffusive zone
\{\mathfrak{d}\lesssim\nu^{1/4}\}
in green influences a much larger region, indicated here as two blue triangular “shadows” cast to either side.

The interplay between anisotropic diffusive activity and the nonshocking characteristics poses a consistent technical challenge throughout this work.

Motivated by (
2.11
), we let the leading part
{\Sigma}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}
of
\Sigma
in the inner expansion (
1.6
) satisfy the viscous Burgers equation.
The nonshocking components are of lower order, so we set the corresponding nonshocking component
{\Omega}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}
to zero.
The higher-order correctors
{\Psi}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}=({\Sigma}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}},{\Omega}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}})
satisfy constant-coefficient transport equations and linearizations of viscous Burgers (Propositions
4.1
and
4.2
):

\displaystyle\nu^{1/4}\partial_{T}{\Omega}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}+A(0)\partial_{X}{\Omega}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}

\displaystyle=\cdots

\displaystyle\partial_{T}{\Sigma}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}+\partial_{1}A_{1}^{1}(0)\partial_{X}({\Sigma}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}{\Sigma}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}})

\displaystyle=B_{1}^{1}(0)\partial_{X}^{2}{\Sigma}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}+\cdots

Ellipses denote forcing that depends on earlier terms in the inner expansion.
These equations largely decouple, so the inner analysis is effectively scalar.

2.4.
Matching

The most delicate task in our approximation scheme is the matching between the inner and outer expansions.
We need to choose data for the inner equations so that partial sums of the two expansions agree to high order, and we need a method to prove this agreement.
As a direct approach, we might attempt to analyze an equation satisfied by the difference of the outer and inner partial sums.
This proves challenging, however.
Each expansions has a strength and a drawback: the outer is hyperbolic but fully coupled, while the inner is decoupled but parabolic.
The difference between the two expansions would suffer the drawbacks of both, solving a fully coupled degenerate parabolic equation.

To circumvent this difficulty, we introduce an auxiliary doubly-indexed expansion
(\psi_{k,\ell})_{k,\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
that we term the “grid.”
Let
{\psi}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}
denote a blow-down of
{\Psi}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}
to the original coordinates
(t,x)
.
Inspired by the homogeneous expansion
(\overline{\psi}_{\ell})
for
{\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}},
we design
(\psi_{k,\ell})
so that in the “matching zone”
\mathfrak{e}\asymp\nu^{\beta}
,

(2.12)

{\psi}_{k}^{\textnormal{out}}\approx\psi_{k,0}+\psi_{k,1}+\ldots\quad\text{and}\quad{\psi}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}\approx\psi_{0,\ell}+\nu\psi_{1,\ell}+\ldots

(See Propositions
6.1
and
7.1
.)
As a result, the outer and inner expansions are composed of the same underlying building blocks, and they therefore match to a high degree (Proposition
7.11
).
Thus the grid reduces the fine cancelation between the outer and inner expansions to the increasing smallness of terms in (
2.12
).
We can accomplish this while letting
\psi_{k,\ell}
satisfy simple
decoupled hyperbolic
equations (Propositions
4.3
and
4.4
).
The grid thus enjoys the advantages of both expansions and the disadvantages of neither.

Bounding the grid terms
(\psi_{k,\ell})
proves to be the heart of our analysis (Proposition
5.10
).
We carefully track the interplay between homogeneous and isotropic effects, and employ elaborate bookkeeping to treat the double index.
Once we have controlled the grid, matching between the outer and inner expansions follows from (
2.12
).
This allows us to smoothly switch from one to the other in the intermediate region
\{\mathfrak{e}\asymp\nu^{\beta}\}
in our approximate solution
{\psi}_{K}^{\textnormal{app}}
.
If we include enough terms
(K\gg 1)
, this approximate solution is highly accurate, in the sense that it solves (
1.1
) with minuscule residue (Proposition
8.3
).

2.5.
Closure

To complete our analysis, we show that our approximate solution does in fact approximate the true solution.
This is similar to Cauchy stability from standard well-posedness theory, with the main difficulty coming from the degenerate parabolic nature of the equations.
We draw on energy estimates from Serre’s approach to local well-posedness
[
68
]
to close (
1.1
) in arbitrarily high Sobolev norms (Theorem
8.7
).
Theorem
1.3
follows.

Corollaries
1.4
–
1.6
reflect the behavior of
\psi^{(\nu)}
in the diffusive zone
\{\mathfrak{d}\lesssim\nu^{1/4}\}
.
After the scaling (
2.10
),
\Psi^{(\nu)}(T,X)\sim{\Psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}(T,X)
while
\Psi^{(0)}(T,X)\sim\mathfrak{u}(T,X)
.
The difference
{\Psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}-\mathfrak{u}
is order 1, which corresponds to
\psi^{(\nu)}-\psi^{(0)}\asymp\nu^{1/4}
.
Thus the
L_{x}^{\infty}
-rate in Corollary
1.4
is sharp.

By (
2.10
), this
\nu^{1/4}
difference persists over a spatial region of scale
\nu^{3/4}
.
As a result, the difference is small in
\mathcal{C}_{x}^{\alpha}
if and only if
\alpha<1/3
.
The nonshocking component
\omega^{(0)}
has leading homogeneity 2 rather than 1, so similar reasoning yields a Hölder threshold of
2/3
for
\omega
, as in Corollary
1.5
.

Finally,
\Psi^{(\nu)}\sim{\Psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}
.
As noted above, we let
{\Psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}
solve viscous Burgers.
In fact, we choose the unique solution of viscous Burgers constructed in
[
22
]
that matches
\mathfrak{u}
to a high degree at infinity.
Thus, the limit of
\Psi^{(\nu)}
is unique, modulo scaling symmetries corresponding to the constants
a
and
b
in (
2.3
) and
B_{1}^{1}(0)
in (
2.11
).
This explains Corollary
1.6
.

3.
The outer expansion

In this section, we begin to study the inviscid limit
\nu\to 0
in (
1.1
).

3.1.
A standard expansion

As described in Section
2
, we expect
\psi^{(\nu)}
to admit a standard asymptotic expansion in
\nu
sufficiently far from shock formation:

(3.1)

\psi^{(\nu)}\sim\sum_{k\geq 0}\nu^{k}{\psi}_{k}^{\textnormal{out}}.

We term this the outer expansion.
Because the data
\mathring{\psi}
is independent of
\nu
, we take

{\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}}(t_{0},\,\cdot\,)=\mathring{\psi}\quad\text{and}\quad{\psi}_{k}^{\textnormal{out}}(t_{0},\,\cdot\,)=0\quad\text{for all }k\geq 1.

We can derive evolution equations for
{\psi}_{k}^{\textnormal{out}}
by substituting (
3.1
) in (
1.1
) and formally collecting terms of like order in
\nu
.
The first outer term
{\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}}
coincides with the inviscid solution
\psi^{(0)}
:

(3.2)

\partial_{t}{\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}}+A({\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}})\partial_{x}{\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}}=0,\quad{\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}}(t_{0},\,\cdot\,)=\mathring{\psi}.

By
1
and (
2.1
), the earliest shock forms at the spacetime origin,
{\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}}(0,0)=0
, and
\lambda_{(I_{0})}(0)=0
.

To express the evolution equations for
k\geq 1
, we introduce some convenient notation.
Let
\mathrm{D}A
denote the 3-tensor field defined by

[(\mathrm{D}A)(\psi)vw]^{I}\coloneqq(\partial_{K}A_{J}^{I})(\psi)v^{K}w^{J}.

We extend this in a natural fashion to higher derivatives
\mathrm{D}^{r}A
.
Given functions
g_{1},\ldots,g_{k}
, let
\texttt{f}[g_{1},\ldots,g_{k}]
denote a function of
g_{1},\ldots,g_{k}
and derivatives thereof.
We use brackets to emphasize the presence of derivatives, and we allow the function to change from instance to instance.
We write
{\psi}_{<k}^{\textnormal{out}}
for
({\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}},\ldots,{\psi}_{k-1}^{\textnormal{out}})
and trust that similar expressions will be clear from context.
Then we can write

\partial_{t}{\psi}_{k}^{\textnormal{out}}+A({\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}})\partial_{x}{\psi}_{k}^{\textnormal{out}}+(\mathrm{D}A)({\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}}){\psi}_{k}^{\textnormal{out}}\partial_{x}{\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}}=\texttt{f}[{\psi}_{<k}^{\textnormal{out}}],\quad{\psi}_{k}^{\textnormal{out}}(t_{0},\,\cdot\,)=0.

In full:

Proposition 3.1
.

For all
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
{\psi}_{k}^{\textnormal{out}}
satisfies

(3.3)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}{\psi}_{k}^{\textnormal{out}}+\partial_{x}[A({\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}}){\psi}_{k}^{\textnormal{out}}]=-\partial_{x}

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}r\geq 1,\,\kappa_{j}\geq 1\\
\kappa_{0}+\ldots+\kappa_{r}=k\end{subarray}}}\,\frac{\mathrm{D}^{r}A({\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}})}{(r+1)!}\prod_{j=0}^{r}{\psi}_{\kappa_{j}}^{\textnormal{out}}

\displaystyle+\partial_{x}\sum_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}r\geq 0,\,\kappa_{\geq 1}\hskip 0.5pt\geq 1\\
\kappa_{0}+\ldots+\kappa_{r}=k-1\end{subarray}}}\,\frac{\mathrm{D}^{r}B({\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}})}{r!}(\partial_{x}{\psi}_{\kappa_{0}}^{\textnormal{out}})\prod_{j=1}^{r}{\psi}_{\kappa_{j}}^{\textnormal{out}}.

Precisely, we contract the
(r+2)
-tensor
\mathrm{D}^{r}A
against the
r+1
tensor
\prod_{j=0}^{r}{\psi}_{\kappa_{j}}^{\textnormal{out}}
to obtain a vector (and similarly in the
B
term).
We de-emphasize this tensorial structure in the sequel.

Proof.

Recalling that
A(\psi)\partial_{x}\psi=\partial_{x}[f(\psi)]
for the flux function
f,
we formally assume (
3.1
) and expand a Taylor series for
f
:

\displaystyle\partial_{x}\Big[f\Big({\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}}+\sum_{k\geq 1}\nu^{k}{\psi}_{k}^{\textnormal{out}}\Big)-f({\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}})\Big]

\displaystyle=\partial_{x}\sum_{r\geq 0}\frac{\mathrm{D}^{r+1}f({\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}})}{(r+1)!}\Big(\sum_{k\geq 1}\nu^{k}{\psi}_{k}^{\textnormal{out}}\Big)^{r+1}

\displaystyle=\partial_{x}\sum_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}r\geq 0\\
\kappa_{0},\ldots,\kappa_{r}\geq 1\end{subarray}}}\,\nu^{\kappa_{0}+\ldots+\kappa_{r}}\frac{\mathrm{D}^{r+1}f({\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}})}{(r+1)!}\prod_{j=0}^{r}{\psi}_{\kappa_{j}}^{\textnormal{out}}.

Using
\mathrm{D}^{r+1}f=\mathrm{D}^{r}A
and extracting the coefficient of
\nu^{k}
, we obtain the expression

\partial_{x}\sum_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}r\geq 0,\,\kappa_{j}\geq 1\\
\kappa_{0}+\ldots+\kappa_{r}=k\end{subarray}}}\,\frac{\mathrm{D}^{r}A({\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}})}{(r+1)!}\prod_{j=0}^{r}{\psi}_{\kappa_{j}}^{\textnormal{out}}=\partial_{x}[A({\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}}){\psi}_{k}^{\textnormal{out}}]+\partial_{x}\sum_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}r\geq 1,\,\kappa_{j}\geq 1\\
\kappa_{0}+\ldots+\kappa_{r}=k\end{subarray}}}\,\frac{\mathrm{D}^{r}A({\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}})}{(r+1)!}\prod_{j=0}^{r}{\psi}_{\kappa_{j}}^{\textnormal{out}}.

Recalling (
1.1
), this yields all but the last term in (
3.3
).
We obtain the final term from the viscous action
\nu\partial[B(\psi)\partial_{x}\psi]
in a similar fashion, and omit the details.
∎

The system (
3.3
) is a linear heterogeneous transport equation and is well-posed on
[t_{0},0)\times\mathbb{R}
.
Hence the outer terms
{\psi}_{k}^{\textnormal{out}}
are defined unambiguously.

3.2.
Shocking and nonshocking subspaces

In subsequent work, it will be important to distinguish between shocking and nonshocking components.
For simplicity, we perform a linear change of basis in state space
\mathcal{V}
so that
r_{I}(0)=e_{I}
.
That is, the right eigenvectors of
A(0)
coincide with the standard basis.
We also relabel our indices so that
I_{0}=1
.
That is,
e_{1}
corresponds to the shocking characteristic.
(Hence in general, the eigenvalues are no longer ordered.)
As a consequence,
A(0)
is diagonal and
A_{1}^{1}(0)=\lambda_{(1)}(0)=0
.
We now introduce distinct notation for the shocking and nonshocking components:

(3.4)

\sigma\coloneqq\psi^{1}\quad\text{and}\quad\omega\coloneqq(\psi^{2},\ldots,\psi^{N}).

In particular,
\psi=(\sigma,\omega)^{\top}
.
For convenience, we let
\perp
denote restriction to nonshocking indices, so
\omega=\psi^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}
.
We have decomposed the range
\mathcal{V}
into the one-dimensional shocking eigenspace
\mathbb{R}e_{1}
, denoted by
\mathcal{V}^{1}
, and the complementary nonshocking subspace
\mathcal{V}^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}\coloneqq\operatorname{span}(e_{2},\ldots,e_{N})
.
We highlight that we “freeze coefficients” by decomposing relative to the matrix
A(0)
rather than
A(\psi)
.
This choice prevents complications when derivatives land on the eigenbasis
(\lambda_{(I)},r_{I})_{I\in[N]}
.

We extend this notation to mappings from
\mathcal{V}
.

Notation 1
.

Let
\pi\colon\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{V}^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}
and
\iota\colon\mathcal{V}^{\perp}\to\mathcal{V}
denote orthogonal projection and inclusion.
If
f\colon\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{V}
, we write
f^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}\coloneqq\pi f
.
If
M\colon\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{V}\otimes\mathcal{V}^{*}
, we write
M_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}\coloneqq M\iota
, meaning
M_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}(p)v=M(p)\iota\,v
for
p\in\mathcal{V}
,
v\in\mathcal{V}^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}
.
Similarly,
M^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}\coloneqq\pi A
and
M_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}\coloneqq\pi M\iota
.
If
g\colon\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{W}
for some vector space
\mathcal{W}
, we interpret
\mathrm{d}f\colon\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{W}\otimes\mathcal{V}^{*}
and write
\partial_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}g\coloneqq(\mathrm{d}g)_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}
.
We use analogous notation with
1
in place of
\perp
.

In our basis, projections to
\mathcal{V}^{1}
and
\mathcal{V}^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}
represent projections onto the first component and remaining components, respectively.
Hence for a matrix
M\in\mathcal{V}\otimes\mathcal{V}^{*}
acting on
\mathcal{V}
, the notation
M_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}
and
M^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}
restricts to nonshocking indices of columns and rows, respectively (and similarly for
M_{1},M^{1}
).

3.3.
Outer estimates

The quality of the approximation (
3.1
) is determined by the size of
{\psi}_{k}^{\textnormal{out}}
and its derivatives, particularly near the preshock.
By hypothesis
(H6)
, the first term
{\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}}
is well-approximated by
\mathfrak{u}
near the origin.
We therefore bound
{\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}}
and its derivatives by suitable powers of the distance
\mathfrak{d}
from (
2.4
).

We take a similar approach to the higher-order outer correctors
{\psi}_{k}^{\textnormal{out}}
.
Each appears homogeneous at leading order, but the remainder takes a more complex form due to the interplay between the viscosity and the nonshocking characteristics.
The homogeneous distance function
\mathfrak{d}
is highly anisotropic due to the different scaling exponents in
t
and
x
.
Ultimately, this reflects our convention that
\lambda(0)=0
, which lends the shocking dynamics a distinguished
t
direction.
In contrast, nonshocking characteristics scale similarly in
t
and
x
.
To capture their effects, we use the isotropic Euclidean distance
\mathfrak{e}
from (
2.2
).
The two distance functions are related in the following manner:

(3.5)

\mathfrak{d}^{-2}\lesssim\mathfrak{e}^{-1}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathfrak{d}\lesssim\mathfrak{e}^{1/3}.

We express most of the coming estimates in term of these distinct measures of proximity to the origin.
For example,

Proposition 3.2
.

For all
k,m,n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, where
\mathfrak{e}\leq 1/2
we have

(3.6)

|\partial_{t}^{m}\partial_{x}^{n}{\psi}_{k}^{\textnormal{out}}|\lesssim\mathfrak{d}^{-4k-2m-3n+1}+\left|\log\mathfrak{e}\right|\mathfrak{e}^{-2k-m+1}\mathfrak{d}^{-3n}.

As our problem is fundamentally local, we always restrict statements such as this to a fixed neighborhood of the origin.
We take this as a convention in the sequel: all estimates apply in the region
\{\mathfrak{e}\leq 1/2\}
, as the solution is uniformly smooth outside this region.

The first term in Proposition
3.2
represents the leading homogeneous behavior of
{\psi}_{k}^{\textnormal{out}}
.
However, in some regimes this term is overwhelmed by nonshocking effects, which produce the second term in (
3.6
).
We defer the proof to Section
5.2
, which clarifies this decomposition through Proposition
5.6
.
Using Proposition
3.2
, we show that the partial sum

{\psi}_{[K]}^{\textnormal{out}}\coloneqq\sum_{k=0}^{K}\nu^{k}{\psi}_{k}^{\textnormal{out}}

is a good approximate solution of (
1.1
) provided
K\gg 1
and we are not too close to the singularity at the origin.

Proposition 3.3
.

For all
K\in\mathbb{N}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, and
\beta\in(3/8,1/2)
, we can write

\partial_{t}{\psi}_{[K]}^{\textnormal{out}}+A({\psi}_{[K]}^{\textnormal{out}})\partial_{x}{\psi}_{[K]}^{\textnormal{out}}=\nu\partial_{x}\big[B({\psi}_{[K]}^{\textnormal{out}}\partial_{x}{\psi}_{[K]}^{\textnormal{out}})\big]+{F}_{K}^{\textnormal{out}}

for forcing
{F}_{K}^{\textnormal{out}}
satisfying
\partial_{x}^{n}{F}_{K}^{\textnormal{out}}\lesssim_{K,n}\nu^{(1-2\beta)K-3n/4-3/4}
where
\mathfrak{e}\geq\nu^{\beta}
.

To express the proof concisely, we introduce some multi-index notation.
Given
r\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
{\kappa=(\kappa_{0},\ldots,\kappa_{r})\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{r+1}}
, we let
\left|\kappa\right|\coloneqq\kappa_{0}+\ldots+\kappa_{r}
and
\#\kappa\coloneqq r
.

Proof.

Let
{\psi}_{[1,K]}^{\textnormal{out}}\coloneqq{\psi}_{[K]}^{\textnormal{out}}-{\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}}
and suppose
\mathfrak{e}\geq\nu^{\beta}
.
Proposition
3.2
implies that
{\psi}_{[1,K]}^{\textnormal{out}}\lesssim_{K}\nu\mathfrak{d}^{-3}\lesssim_{K}\nu^{1/4}
in this region.
After all, (
3.5
) yields

(3.7)

\mathfrak{d}^{-1}\lesssim\mathfrak{e}^{-1/2}\leq\nu^{-\beta/2}<\nu^{-1/4}.

Recall that
A
is the derivative of the flux function
f
.
Using Taylor’s theorem and Proposition
3.2
, we write

f({\psi}_{[K]}^{\textnormal{out}})-f({\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}})=\sum_{r=0}^{K}\frac{\mathrm{D}^{r}A({\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}})}{(r+1)!}\big({\psi}_{[1,K]}^{\textnormal{out}})^{r+1}+\mathcal{O}_{K}\big(\nu^{(K+2)/4}\big).

Moreover,

\displaystyle\sum_{r=0}^{K}\frac{\mathrm{D}^{r}A({\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}})}{(r+1)!}\big({\psi}_{[1,K]}^{\textnormal{out}})^{r+1}=\sum_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq\kappa_{j}\leq K\\
\left|\kappa\right|\leq K\end{subarray}}}\,\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\#\kappa}A({\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}})}{(\#\kappa+1)!}

\displaystyle\nu^{\left|\kappa\right|}\prod_{j}{\psi}_{\kappa_{j}}^{\textnormal{out}}

\displaystyle+\sum_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq\kappa_{j}\leq K\\
\#\kappa\leq K\\
\left|\kappa\right|>K\end{subarray}}}\,\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\#\kappa}A({\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}})}{(\#\kappa+1)!}\nu^{\left|\kappa\right|}\prod_{j}{\psi}_{\kappa_{j}}^{\textnormal{out}}.

The first sum on the right agrees with the sum of the
A
-terms in (
3.3
) over
1\leq k\leq K
, provided we weight
{\psi}_{k}^{\textnormal{out}}
by
\nu^{k}
.
For the second sum on the right, Proposition
3.2
implies that

\nu^{\left|\kappa\right|}\prod_{j}{\psi}_{\kappa_{j}}^{\textnormal{out}}\lesssim_{\kappa}(\nu\mathfrak{e}^{-2})^{\left|\kappa\right|}\mathfrak{d}^{\#\kappa+1}\lesssim_{\kappa}\nu^{(1-2\beta)\left|\kappa\right|}\mathfrak{d}^{\#\kappa+1}.

In this sum we have
\#\kappa\geq 1
and
\left|\kappa\right|\geq K+2
, so the sum is bounded by
\nu^{(1-2\beta)(K+2)}\mathfrak{d}^{2}
.
When we differentiate the flux in
x
, we pick up a factor of
\mathfrak{d}^{-3}
(see Proposition
3.2
) and obtain error bounded by
\nu^{(1-2\beta)K-1/4}
, due to (
3.7
).
Noting that
1-2\beta<1/4
, this is larger than the
\nu^{(K+2)/4}
Taylor-error from earlier.
Performing similar computations for the diffusive terms involving
B
, we find slightly smaller error of order
\nu^{(1-2\beta)K}
.
Thus
{F}_{K}^{\textnormal{out}}\lesssim_{K}\nu^{(1-2\beta)K-1/4}
where
\mathfrak{e}\geq\nu^{\beta}
.
Estimates on derivatives follow in a similar manner, using
\mathfrak{d}^{-3}\lesssim\nu^{-3/4}
.
∎

4.
Inner and grid expansions

We have now constructed an outer expansion that should closely approximate the full solution
\psi^{(\nu)}
a certain distance from the preshock.
In this section, we show how to treat
\psi^{(\nu)}
near the preshock, where viscous effects are nonperturbative.

By
(H6)
,
{\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}}\approx(\mathfrak{u},0)
near the origin, where
\mathfrak{u}
satisfies the cubic equation (
2.3
).
We thus expect viscous effects to matter where
\nu|\partial_{x}^{2}\mathfrak{u}|\gtrsim|\mathfrak{u}\partial_{x}\mathfrak{u}|
and to primarily act on the shocking component.
Unraveling the structure of
\mathfrak{u}
from (
2.3
), we can check that viscosity matters when
\left|t\right|\lesssim\nu^{1/2}
and
\left|x\right|\lesssim\nu^{3/4}
, and there
|\mathfrak{u}|\lesssim\nu^{1/4}
.
To study
\psi
in this small region, we perform the blow-up from (
2.10
):

T\coloneqq\nu^{-1/2}t,\quad X\coloneqq\nu^{-3/4}x,\quad\text{and}\quad\Psi\coloneqq\nu^{-1/4}\psi.

In these inner coordinates, (
1.1
) becomes

(4.1)

\partial_{T}\Psi+\nu^{-1/4}A(\nu^{1/4}\Psi)\partial_{X}\Psi=\partial_{X}\big[B(\nu^{1/4}\Psi)\partial_{X}\Psi\big].

Due to the powers of
\nu^{1/4}
appearing in (
4.1
), we posit the inner ansatz

(4.2)

\Psi\sim\sum_{\ell\geq 0}\nu^{\ell/4}{\Psi}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}.

Unlike the outer expansion (
3.1
), our ansatz will be “criminal”
[
6
]
in the sense that the terms
{\Psi}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}
themselves depend on
\nu
.
Thus (
4.2
) is not a true expansion in
\nu
.
This concession allows us to overcome a difficulty with the nonshocking components that we explain below.

4.1.
Inner equations

Following (
3.4
), we set

\Sigma\coloneqq\Psi^{1}\quad\text{and}\quad\Omega\coloneqq\Psi^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}=(\Psi^{2},\ldots,\Psi^{N}).

As in (
4.1
), we develop inner ansatzes for
\Sigma
and
\Omega
of the form

(4.3)

\Sigma\sim\sum_{\ell\geq 0}\nu^{\ell/4}{\Sigma}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}\quad\text{and}\quad\Omega\sim\sum_{\ell\geq 0}\nu^{\ell/4}{\Omega}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}.

Due to our choice
\lambda_{({1})}(0)=0
in
1
, the shocking component lies in the nullspace of
A(0)
.
Thus formally

(4.4)

\nu^{-1/4}A^{1}(\nu^{1/4}\Psi)\partial_{X}\Psi=(\mathrm{D}A^{1})(0)\Psi\partial_{X}\Psi+\mathcal{O}(\nu^{1/4}),

while

(4.5)

\nu^{-1/4}A^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}(\nu^{1/4}\Psi)\partial_{X}\Psi=\nu^{-1/4}A_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}(0)\partial_{X}\Omega+\mathcal{O}(1).

Strict hyperbolicity implies that the restriction
A^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}
of
A
to the nonshocking subspace is invertible.
It follows that the advection term in (
4.1
) is of a different order in the shocking and nonshocking components.
This term dominates all others in the nonshocking direction, while it balances others in the shocking equation.
This asymmetry leads to a triangular structure:
{\Omega}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}
does not depend on
{\Sigma}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}
, while
{\Sigma}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}
does depend on
{\Omega}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}
.
We thus first estimate
{\Omega}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}
and then
{\Sigma}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}
.
We use this structure heavily.

We take a “lawful” approach to the evolution equations for the terms
{\Sigma}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}
in (
4.3
).
We project (
4.1
) onto the span of
e_{1}
, Taylor expand the nonlinearities, and collect terms of order
\nu^{\ell/4}
.
Our criminality manifests in our treatment of the nonshocking components.
To account for the
\nu^{-1/4}
prefactor on the right side of (
4.5
), we project onto the nonshocking subspace and multiply (
4.1
) by
\nu^{1/4}
:

(4.6)

\nu^{1/4}\partial_{T}\Omega+A^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}(\nu^{1/4}\Psi)\partial_{X}\Psi=\nu^{1/4}\partial_{X}\big[B^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}(\nu^{1/4}\Psi)\partial_{X}\Psi\big].

The evolution equation for
{\Omega}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}
consists of the order-
\nu^{\ell/4}
terms in (
4.6
)
and
the time derivative
\nu^{(\ell+1)/4}\partial_{T}{\Omega}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}
.
It is our inclusion of this “off-order” derivative that makes the inner ansatz criminal.
It amounts to a slight tilt of the characteristics in the
{\Omega}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}
-equation, which maintains a causal link between the inner and outer expansions.
This avoids technical difficulties that otherwise appear.

Because the nonshocking component of
{\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}}
is much smaller than the shocking component near the origin, it is natural to take
{\Omega}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}=0
.
So in fact the
\Omega
-sum in (
4.3
) begins from
\ell=1
.
For
\Sigma
, we use (
4.4
) to extract the leading terms in (
4.1
):

(4.7)

\partial_{T}{\Sigma}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}+(\mathrm{D}A^{1})(0){\Psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}\partial_{X}{\Psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}=\partial_{X}[B^{1}(0)\partial_{X}{\Psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}].

Because
{\Omega}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}=0
, we have
{\Psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}={\Sigma}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}e_{1}
and (
4.7
) simplifies to the viscous Burgers equation:

(4.8)

\partial_{T}{\Sigma}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}+(\partial_{1}A_{1}^{1})(0){\Sigma}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}\partial_{X}{\Sigma}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}=B_{1}^{1}(0)\partial_{X}^{2}{\Sigma}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}.

As noted after (
2.7
),
\partial_{1}A_{1}^{1}(0)\neq 0
.
Moreover, by
1
,
B_{1}^{1}(0)>0
.
This is the only nondegeneracy we require of
B
.

Due to these hypotheses, (
4.8
) is equivalent modulo scaling to the viscous Burgers equation satisfied by the leading inner term in
[
22
]
.
There, we constructed a unique solution of (
4.8
) that agrees with the cubic (
2.3
), and thus
{\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}
, sufficiently well at infinity.
We use the same solution here, which is precisely constructed in
[
22
, Proposition 4.2]
.

We next describe the evolution equations for
{\Omega}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}
and
{\Sigma}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}
with
\ell\geq 1
.
We first write the nonshocking equation.
Because the viscous term in (
4.1
) is smaller than the nonshocking advection by a factor of
\nu^{1/4}
, the
{\Omega}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}
equation does not directly involve
\partial_{X}^{2}{\Omega}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}
.
Indeed, we can write

(4.9)

\nu^{1/4}\partial_{T}{\Omega}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}+A_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}(0)\partial_{X}{\Omega}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}=\texttt{f}[{\Psi}_{<\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}].

We recall that
\texttt{f}[{\Psi}_{<\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}]
denotes a function of
{\Psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}},\ldots,{\Psi}_{\ell-1}^{\textnormal{in}}
and their derivatives.
Assuming we have already determined the terms
{\Psi}_{\ell^{\prime}}^{\textnormal{in}}
for
\ell^{\prime}<\ell
, we can treat the expression on the right as forcing.
So (
4.9
) is a linear constant-coefficient transport equation.
For example,

\nu^{1/4}\partial_{T}{\Omega}_{1}^{\textnormal{in}}+A_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}(0)\partial_{X}{\Omega}_{1}^{\textnormal{in}}=-(\partial_{1}A_{1}^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}})(0){\Sigma}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}\partial_{X}{\Sigma}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}+B_{1}^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}(0)\partial_{X}^{2}{\Sigma}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}=\texttt{f}[{\Psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}].

We now turn to
{\Sigma}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}
.
Its equation is somewhat more complicated, as it includes contributions from
{\Omega}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}
and involves the viscosity directly.

(4.10)

\displaystyle\hskip-3.0pt\partial_{T}{\Sigma}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}+(\partial_{1}A_{1}^{1})(0)\partial_{X}({\Sigma}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}{\Sigma}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}})+(\partial_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}

\displaystyle A_{1}^{1})(0){\Omega}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}\partial_{X}{\Sigma}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}+(\partial_{1}A_{1}^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}})(0){\Sigma}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}\partial_{X}{\Omega}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}

\displaystyle=B_{1}^{1}(0)\partial_{X}^{2}{\Sigma}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}+B_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}^{1}(0)\partial_{X}^{2}{\Omega}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}+\texttt{f}[{\Psi}_{<\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}].

Provided we determine the nonshocking component
{\Omega}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}
first, we can fold its terms into our known forcing.
So (
4.10
) becomes a linear advection-diffusion equation:

(4.11)

\partial_{T}{\Sigma}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}+(\partial_{1}A_{1}^{1})(0)\partial_{X}({\Sigma}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}{\Sigma}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}})-B_{1}^{1}(0)\partial_{X}^{2}{\Sigma}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}=\texttt{f}[{\Psi}_{<\ell}^{\textnormal{in}},{\Omega}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}].

Comparing (
4.9
) and (
4.11
), we see that
{\Omega}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}
does not depend on
{\Sigma}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}
, while the latter depends on the former.
This is the triangular structure mentioned above.

In the following propositions, we record the precise nature of the forcing in (
4.9
) and (
4.11
).
Given a collection of functions
G_{\alpha}\coloneqq G_{\alpha_{0}},\ldots,G_{\alpha_{r}}
taking values in
\mathcal{V}
, we frequently treat Taylor terms of
A
and
B
of the form

(4.12)

\mathcal{A}G_{\alpha}\coloneqq\frac{\mathrm{D}^{r}A(0)}{(r+1)!}\prod_{j=0}^{r}G_{\alpha_{j}}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{B}G_{\alpha}\coloneqq\frac{\mathrm{D}^{r}B(0)}{r!}\partial_{X}G_{\alpha_{0}}\prod_{j=1}^{r}G_{\alpha_{j}}.

The product in the first expression denotes contractions of the vectors
G_{\alpha_{j}}
with derivatives of
A
.
We let
\mathcal{A}^{1}
and
\mathcal{A}^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}
denote the projections of the image of the multilinear operator
\mathcal{A}
onto the shocking and nonshocking subspaces, respectively.
These involve derivatives of
A^{1}
or
A^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}
rather than
A
.
We treat
\mathcal{B}
similarly.

Proposition 4.1
.

For all
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,
{\Omega}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}
satisfies

(4.13)

[\nu^{1/4}\partial_{T}+A_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}(0)]\partial_{X}{\Omega}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}=-\partial_{X}\sum_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq r\leq\ell\\
\alpha_{0}+\ldots+\alpha_{r}=\ell-r\end{subarray}}}\,\mathcal{A}^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}{\Psi}_{\alpha}^{\textnormal{in}}+\partial_{X}\sum_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq r\leq\ell-1\\
\alpha_{0}+\ldots+\alpha_{r}=\ell-r-1\end{subarray}}}\,\mathcal{B}^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}{\Psi}_{\alpha}^{\textnormal{in}}.

Proof.

We multiply (
4.1
) by
\nu^{1/4}
and collect terms of order
\nu^{\ell/4}
, omitting the time derivative.
Using the fact that
A(\Psi)\partial_{X}\Psi=\partial_{X}[f(\Psi)]
for the flux
f
, we can write

\displaystyle A(\nu^{\frac{1}{4}}\Psi)\partial_{X}\Psi

\displaystyle=\nu^{-\frac{1}{4}}\partial_{X}[f(\nu^{\frac{1}{4}}\Psi)]=\nu^{-\frac{1}{4}}\partial_{X}f\Big(\sum_{\ell}\nu^{\frac{\ell+1}{4}}{\Psi}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}\Big)

\displaystyle=\partial_{X}\sum_{\alpha}\nu^{\frac{\left|\alpha\right|+\#\alpha}{4}}\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\#\alpha+1}f(0)}{(\#\alpha+1)!}\prod_{j}{\Psi}_{\alpha_{j}}^{\textnormal{in}}=\partial_{X}\sum_{\alpha}\nu^{\frac{\left|\alpha\right|+\#\alpha}{4}}\frac{\mathrm{D}^{\#\alpha}A(0)}{(\#\alpha+1)!}\prod_{j}{\Psi}_{\alpha_{j}}^{\textnormal{in}}.

Grouping by
r=\#\alpha
, we see that terms of order
\nu^{\ell/4}
satisfy
\alpha_{0}+\ldots+\alpha_{r}=\ell-r
.
After projection onto
\mathcal{V}^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}
, the singleton index
\alpha=(\ell)
yields
A_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}(0)\partial_{X}{\Omega}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}
in (
4.13
), and the higher-order terms take the form
\mathcal{A}^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}
from (
4.12
).
The computations for
B
are similar, save that they include an extra factor of
\nu^{1/4}
, so their indices are shifted by one.
Finally, we insert
\nu^{1/4}\partial_{T}{\Omega}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}
as in the left side of (
4.13
) to match our criminal convention for
{\Omega}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}
.
∎

In the remainder of the paper, the sum notation
\sum\limits^{*}
indicates the omission of terms that directly involve the function of interest (
{\Sigma}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}
below).

Proposition 4.2
.

For all
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,
{\Sigma}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}
satisfies

(4.14)

\partial_{T}{\Sigma}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}+\partial_{1}A_{1}^{1}(0)\partial_{x}({\Sigma}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}{\Sigma}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}})-B_{1}^{1}(0)\partial_{X}^{2}{\Sigma}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}=-\partial_{X}\sum^{*}_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq r\leq\ell+1\\
\alpha_{0}+\ldots+\alpha_{r}=\ell-r+1\end{subarray}}}\,\mathcal{A}^{1}{\Psi}_{\alpha}^{\textnormal{in}}+\partial_{X}\sum^{*}_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq r\leq\ell\\
\alpha_{0}+\ldots+\alpha_{r}=\ell-r\end{subarray}}}\,\mathcal{B}^{1}{\Psi}_{\alpha}^{\textnormal{in}}.

Proof.

We collect all terms of order
\nu^{\ell/4}
in (
4.1
) and project onto
\mathcal{V}^{1}
, observing that
A_{1}^{1}(0)=0
.
The computations are analogous to the proof of Proposition
4.1
.
∎

We again emphasize again that at level
\ell
in the inner expansion, the nonshocking component
{\Omega}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}
is decoupled from the shocking component
{\Sigma}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}
.
In contrast, the right side of (
4.14
) includes terms of the form
\partial_{x}({\Sigma}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}{\Omega}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}})
and
\partial_{X}^{2}{\Omega}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}
, so
{\Omega}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}
influences
{\Sigma}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}
.
We can thus construct
{\Omega}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}
knowing only
{\Psi}_{<\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}
, and then construct
{\Sigma}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}
from both
{\Psi}_{<\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}
and
{\Omega}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}.

This triangular structure means we do not have to study potentially complicated hyperbolic-parabolic systems.

4.2.
Grid equations

To control the size of
{\Psi}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}
, we must explore the interplay between the outer expansion (
3.1
) and the inner ansatz (
4.2
).
For this purpose, we return to the original coordinates.
Set

{\psi}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}(t,x)\coloneqq\nu^{(\ell+1)/4}{\Psi}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}\big(\nu^{-1/2}t,\nu^{-3/4}x\big).

We use the evolution equations for
{\Psi}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}
to derive analogous equations for
{\psi}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}
.
At leading order, we have weakly-viscous Burgers

\partial_{t}{\sigma}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}+(\partial_{1}A_{1}^{1})(0){\sigma}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}\partial_{x}{\sigma}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}=\nu B_{1}^{1}(0)\partial_{x}^{2}{\sigma}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}},

while
{\omega}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}
vanishes.
Using (
4.9
) and (
4.11
), the equations for
\ell\geq 1
assume the following form:

\begin{split}\partial_{t}{\omega}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}+A_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}(0)\partial_{x}{\omega}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}&=\texttt{f}[{\psi}_{<\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}],\\
\partial_{t}{\sigma}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}+(\partial_{1}A_{1}^{1})(0)\partial_{x}({\sigma}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}{\sigma}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}})-\nu B_{1}^{1}(0)\partial_{x}^{2}{\sigma}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}&=\texttt{f}[{\psi}_{<\ell}^{\textnormal{in}},{\omega}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}].\end{split}

The forces
f
may depend on
\nu
as well.

To relate the outer and inner expansions, we examine the contributions to
{\psi}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}
from terms in the outer expansion.
The outer expansion arises from a Taylor expansion in
\nu
, so we expand
{\psi}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}
in integer powers of
\nu
:

(4.15)

{\psi}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}\sim\sum_{k\geq 0}\nu^{k}\psi_{k,\ell},

with similar decompositions for
\sigma
and
\omega
.
Scaling the inner ansatz (
4.2
), we should have
\psi\sim\sum_{\ell\geq 0}{\psi}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}
near the origin.
Comparing with the outer expansion (
3.1
), we formally conclude that

{\psi}_{k}^{\textnormal{out}}\sim\sum_{\ell\geq 0}\psi_{k,\ell}.

If we arrange
(\psi_{k,\ell})_{k,\ell\geq 0}
in a grid, then the outer and inner ansatzes amount to horizontal or vertical summation, respectively; see Figure
3
.
We therefore refer to
\psi_{k,\ell}
as a “grid term.”
The second and third authors employed a similar structure in an analysis of the Burgers equation
[
22
]
.
In the present setup, the criminal nature of the inner ansatz implies that the grid terms
\psi_{k,\ell}
do not enjoy the homogeneity of their brethren in
[
22
]
.

\psi_{0,0}
\nu\psi_{1,0}
\nu^{2}\psi_{2,0}
\nu^{3}\psi_{3,0}
\vdots
\psi_{0,1}
\nu\psi_{1,1}
\nu^{2}\psi_{2,1}
\nu^{3}\psi_{3,1}
\vdots
\psi_{0,2}
\nu\psi_{1,2}
\nu^{2}\psi_{2,2}
\nu^{3}\psi_{3,2}
\vdots
\psi_{0,3}
\nu\psi_{1,3}
\nu^{2}\psi_{2,3}
\nu^{3}\psi_{3,3}
\vdots
\cdots
\cdots
\cdots
\cdots
\ddots
\nu^{2}{\psi}_{2}^{\textnormal{out}}
{\psi}_{1}^{\textnormal{in}}

Figure 3.

Asymptotic structure of the inner and outer expansions:

\nu^{2}{\psi}_{2}^{\textnormal{out}}
is asymptotically equivalent to the sum of the green row, while
{\psi}_{1}^{\textnormal{in}}
is equivalent to the sum of the blue column.

We find an evolution equation for
\psi_{k,\ell}
by substituting (
4.15
) into the equation for
{\psi}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}
and collecting terms of order
\nu^{k}
.
For example, the first two contributions to
{\sigma}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}
solve

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\sigma_{0,0}+(\partial_{1}A_{1}^{1})(0)\sigma_{0,0}\partial_{x}\sigma_{0,0}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\sigma_{1,0}+(\partial_{1}A_{1}^{1})(0)\partial_{x}(\sigma_{0,0}\sigma_{1,0})

\displaystyle=B_{1}^{1}(0)\partial_{x}^{2}\sigma_{0,0}.

The first equation is inviscid Burgers, and in fact the entire sequence of equations for
(\sigma_{k,0})_{k\geq 0}
coincides with that in
[
22
]
for
(u_{k,0})_{k}
.
In accordance with
(H6)
, we naturally take
\sigma_{0,0}=\mathfrak{u}
.

When
\ell\geq 1
, we make one modification: the leading row
(\psi_{0,\ell})_{\ell}
only approximately solves the expected equations.
This leaves a residue
F_{\ell}
