that we cancel in the next row
(\psi_{1,\ell})
, where it acts with strength
\nu^{-1}
.
We describe this twist in Section
5.1
.

In a minor abuse of notation, we use the
x
-derivative
\partial_{x}
in
\mathcal{B}
when it acts on expressions in the outer variables.
Extending the notation from (
4.12
) to doubly-indexed functions, we thus define

\mathcal{A}g_{\kappa,\alpha}\coloneqq\frac{\mathrm{D}^{r}A(0)}{(r+1)!}\prod_{j=0}^{r}g_{\kappa_{j},\alpha_{j}}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{B}g_{\kappa,\alpha}\coloneqq\frac{\mathrm{D}^{r}B(0)}{r!}\partial_{x}g_{\kappa_{0},\alpha_{0}}\prod_{j=1}^{r}g_{\kappa_{j},\alpha_{j}}.

Whenever multi-indices
\kappa
and
\alpha
appear together in this fashion, we implicitly assume they have the same length.
With these conventions, we have:

Proposition 4.3
.

For all
k,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, there is a force
F_{\ell}^{\omega}
defined in Proposition
5.1
below such that

(4.16)

[\partial_{t}+A_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}(0)\partial_{x}]\omega_{k,\ell}=-\partial_{x}\sum_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq r\leq\ell\\
\kappa_{0}+\ldots+\kappa_{r}=k\\
\alpha_{0}+\ldots+\alpha_{r}=\ell-r\end{subarray}}}\,\mathcal{A}^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}\psi_{\kappa,\alpha}+\partial_{x}\sum_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq r\leq\ell-1\\
\kappa_{0}+\ldots+\kappa_{r}=k-1\\
\alpha_{0}+\ldots+\alpha_{r}=\ell-r-1\end{subarray}}}\,\mathcal{B}^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}\psi_{\kappa,\alpha}-\nu^{-1}\delta_{k,1}F_{\ell}^{\omega}.

Proposition 4.4
.

For
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, there is a force
F_{\ell}^{\sigma}
defined in Proposition
5.1
such that

(4.17)

\partial_{t}\sigma_{k,\ell}+\partial_{1}A_{1}^{1}(0)\partial_{x}(\sigma_{0,0}\sigma_{k,\ell})=-\partial_{x}\sum^{*}_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq r\leq\ell+1\\
\kappa_{0}+\ldots+\kappa_{r}=k\\
\alpha_{0}+\ldots+\alpha_{r}=\ell-r+1\end{subarray}}}\,\mathcal{A}^{1}\psi_{\kappa,\alpha}+\partial_{x}\sum_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq r\leq\ell\\
\kappa_{0}+\ldots+\kappa_{r}=k-1\\
\alpha_{0}+\ldots+\alpha_{r}=\ell-r\end{subarray}}}\,\mathcal{B}^{1}\psi_{\kappa,\alpha}-\nu^{-1}\delta_{k,1}F_{\ell}^{\sigma}.

We recall that
\sum\limits^{*}
indicates the omission of terms involving the function
\sigma_{k,\ell}
of interest.
The Kronecker delta
\delta_{k,1}
confines the force
-F_{\ell}
to the row
k=1
.
The proofs of these results are similar to those of Propositions
4.1
and
4.2
.

4.3.
Data

We now select a domain and data for the grid equations.
Let

\mathsf{S}\coloneqq\max_{I}|\lambda_{(I)}(0)|+1,

which exceeds the maximal characteristic speed at the origin.
Then we define a spacetime contour
\Gamma
and region
\mathcal{I}
by

(4.18)

\displaystyle\Gamma

\displaystyle\coloneqq\big\{(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}_{-}\times(-\pi/2,\pi/2)\mid t=-\mathsf{S}^{-1}\cos x\big\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\big\{(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}_{-}\times(-\pi/2,\pi/2)\mid t>-\mathsf{S}^{-1}\cos x\big\}.

For future use, we also define a constant
\mathsf{R}\asymp\mathsf{S}^{-1}\vee 1
by

(4.19)

\mathsf{R}\coloneqq\sup\{R>0\colon(t,x)\in\mathcal{I}\text{ if }\mathfrak{e}(t,x)<R\}.

By the definition of
\mathsf{S}
,
\Gamma
is uniformly spacelike with respect to
A(0)
: the characteristics
\partial_{t}+\lambda_{(I)}(0)\partial_{x}
flow through
\Gamma
in the same direction and are uniformly nontangential.

Given
p>0
, we write
\Gamma_{p}\coloneqq\nu^{p}\Gamma
and
\mathcal{I}_{p}\coloneqq\nu^{p}\mathcal{I}
.
We select
\beta\in(0,1/2)
satisfying certain conditions and solve the inner and grid equations in
\mathcal{I}_{\beta}
.
The contour
\Gamma_{\beta}
represents the lower part of
\partial\mathcal{I}_{\beta}
, and the spacelike nature of
\Gamma
ensures that
\mathcal{I}_{\beta}
is causally closed.
To solve our transport equations in
\mathcal{I}_{\beta}
, it thus suffices to pose data on
\Gamma_{\beta}
.
The curve
\Gamma_{\beta}
roughly denotes the transition region between the outer and inner expansions.
The grid is defined near this curve, and the joint decomposition for the inner and outer expansions in its vicinity allow us to compare the two expansions and show that they match.

We construct the zeroth row
(\psi_{0,\ell})_{\ell\geq 0}
and column
(\psi_{k,0})_{k\geq 0}
separately in Sections
5.1
and
5.2
below.
For the remaining grid terms with
k\geq 1
, we set

(4.20)

\psi_{k,1}={\psi}_{k}^{\textnormal{out}}-\psi_{k,0}\quad\text{and}\quad\psi_{k,\ell}=0\kern 5.0pt\text{on }\Gamma_{\beta}\text{ for }\ell\geq 2.

Informally, we put all data on the first row
(\psi_{1,\ell})
and initialize the remaining rows from zero.
The data for
\psi_{k,1}
links the grid to the outer expansion.

To relate the inner expansion to the grid, we scale it to the inner coordinates:

(4.21)

\Psi_{k,\ell}(T,X)\coloneqq\nu^{k-(\ell+1)/4}\psi_{k,\ell}\big(\nu^{1/2}T,\nu^{3/4}X\big).

Given
K\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, let

\Psi_{[K],\ell}\coloneqq\sum_{k\leq K}\Psi_{k,\ell}

denote the corresponding partial sum up to row
K
.
Then we construct solutions of the inner equations (
4.13
) and (
4.14
) such that
{\Psi}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}\approx\Psi_{[K],\ell}
in a precise sense where
\mathfrak{e}\asymp\nu^{\beta}
.
We defer the details to Section
7
.

5.
Grid estimates

We now use the equations and data for the grid terms
\psi_{k,\ell}
to control their size in
\mathcal{I}_{\beta}
.
We begin with bespoke constructions of the zeroth row
(\psi_{0,\ell})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
and zeroth column
(\psi_{0,k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
.

5.1.
The zeroth row

The zeroth row
(\psi_{0,\ell})
satisfies a hierarchy of equations decomposing the inviscid conservation law (
3.2
).
To state this hierarchy, we use
\mathcal{V}^{\ell}
to denote the Cartesian
\ell
-power of
\mathcal{V}
and define polynomials
\mathsf{p}_{\ell}^{\omega}\colon\mathcal{V}^{\ell}\to\mathcal{V}^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}
and
\mathsf{p}_{\ell}^{\sigma}\colon\mathcal{V}^{\ell}\times\mathcal{V}^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}\to\mathcal{V}^{{1}}
by

(5.1)

\mathsf{p}_{\ell}^{\omega}(g_{<\ell})\coloneqq-\sum_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq r\leq\ell\\
\alpha_{0}+\ldots+\alpha_{r}=\ell-r\end{subarray}}}\,\mathcal{A}^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}g_{\alpha}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathsf{p}_{\ell}^{\sigma}(g_{<\ell},(g_{\ell})_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}})\coloneqq-\sum^{*}_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq r\leq\ell+1\\
\alpha_{0}+\ldots+\alpha_{r}=\ell-r+1\end{subarray}}}\,\mathcal{A}^{1}g_{\alpha}.\vskip-5.69054pt

Here
\sum\limits^{*}
omits terms involving the shocking component
(g_{\ell})_{1}
, which is not provided.

These polynomials represent certain terms in the Taylor series of
A
projected onto the nonshocking and shocking subspaces, respectively.
Ideally, we would like
\psi_{0,\ell}
to satisfy

(5.2)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\omega_{0,\ell}+A_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}(0)\partial_{x}\omega_{0,\ell}

\displaystyle=\partial_{x}[\mathsf{p}_{\ell}^{\omega}(\psi_{0,<\ell})],

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\sigma_{0,\ell}+\partial_{1}A_{1}^{1}(0)\partial_{x}(\sigma_{0,0}\sigma_{0,\ell})

\displaystyle=\partial_{x}[\mathsf{p}_{\ell}^{\sigma}(\psi_{0,<\ell},\,\omega_{0,\ell})].

However, we do not exactly solve these equations.
Rather, we exhibit approximate solutions that solve the equations with a residue
F_{\ell}
:

(5.3)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\omega_{0,\ell}+A_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}(0)\partial_{x}\omega_{0,\ell}

\displaystyle=\partial_{x}[\mathsf{p}_{\ell}^{\omega}(\psi_{0,<\ell})]+F_{\ell}^{\omega}

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\sigma_{0,\ell}+\partial_{1}A_{{1}}^{{1}}(0)\partial_{x}(\sigma_{0,0}\sigma_{0,\ell})

\displaystyle=\partial_{x}[\mathsf{p}_{\ell}^{\sigma}(\psi_{0,<\ell},\omega_{0,\ell})]+F_{\ell}^{\sigma}.

In the following, we use the notation
[L]
to indicate partial summation in
\ell
up to
\ell=L
.
We choose
\psi_{0,\ell}
so that the partial sum
\psi_{0,[L]}\coloneqq\sum_{\ell=0}^{L}\psi_{0,\ell}
closely approximates
{\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}}
and
\psi_{0,\ell}
and
F_{\ell}
vanish at suitable rates at the origin.

Proposition 5.1
.

There exist solutions
(\psi_{0,\ell})
of (
5.3
) with residues
(F_{\ell})
such that for all
\ell,L,m,n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

(5.4)

\begin{gathered}\big|\partial_{t}^{m}\partial_{x}^{n}\psi_{0,\ell}\big|\lesssim_{\ell,m,n}\left|\log\mathfrak{d}\right|^{(\ell-1)_{+}}\mathfrak{d}^{\ell-2m-3n+1},\\[5.69054pt]
\big|\partial_{t}^{m}\partial_{x}^{n}F_{\ell}^{\omega}\big|\lesssim_{\ell,m,n}\left|\log\mathfrak{d}\right|^{\ell}\mathfrak{d}^{\ell-2m-3n-1},\quad\big|\partial_{t}^{m}\partial_{x}^{n}F_{\ell}^{\sigma}\big|\lesssim_{\ell,m,n}\left|\log\mathfrak{d}\right|^{\ell}\mathfrak{d}^{\ell-2m-3n},\end{gathered}

and

(5.5)

\big|\partial_{t}^{m}\partial_{x}^{n}({\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}}-\psi_{0,[L]})\big|\lesssim_{L,m,n}\left|\log\mathfrak{d}\right|^{L}\mathfrak{d}^{L-2m-3n+2}.

Moreover,
\psi_{0,0}=(\sigma_{0,0},0)
and
F_{0}=0.

To construct
\psi_{0,\ell}
, we turn to the homogeneous components
\overline{\psi}_{\ell}
of
{\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}}
from
(H6)
.
These are polynomials in the functions
t
,
\mathfrak{u}
, and
\mathfrak{m}
(from (
2.3
) and (
2.5
)) that are homogeneous in the sense of Definition
2.1
.
Expanding on
(H6)
, we obtain:

Proposition 5.2
.

There exists a sequence
(\overline{\psi}_{\ell})
of
(\ell+1)
-homogeneous functions
\overline{\psi}_{\ell}
such that for all
L,m,n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

(5.6)

\big|\partial_{t}^{m}\partial_{x}^{n}({\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}}-\overline{\psi}_{[L]})\big|\lesssim_{L,m,n}\mathfrak{d}^{L-2m-3n+2}.

Moreover,
\overline{\psi}_{0,\ell}
is a polynomial in
(t,\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{m})
and
\psi_{0}=(\mathfrak{u},0)
.
It solves

(5.7)

\displaystyle A_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}(0)\partial_{x}\overline{\omega}_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\partial_{x}[\mathsf{p}_{\ell}^{\omega}(\overline{\psi}_{<\ell})]-\partial_{t}\overline{\omega}_{\ell-1},

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\overline{\sigma}_{\ell}+\partial_{1}A_{{1}}^{{1}}(0)\partial_{x}(\overline{\sigma}_{0}\overline{\sigma}_{\ell})

\displaystyle=\partial_{x}[\mathsf{p}_{\ell}^{\sigma}(\overline{\psi}_{<\ell},\overline{\omega}_{\ell})].

Proof.

The first two statements restate
(H6)
.
To find (
5.7
), we substitute the homogeneous expansion into the inviscid equation (
3.2
) and collect terms with like homogeneity.
These homogeneous equations can be compared with the criminal equations (
5.2
).
∎

The homogeneous equations (
5.7
) only differ from (
5.2
) in their treatment of the nonshocking component: it is advected horizontally by
A_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}(0)\partial_{x}
in (
5.7
) and diagonally by
\partial_{t}+A_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}(0)\partial_{x}
in (
5.2
) and (
5.3
).
The tilted characteristics of the latter are essential for our subsequent viscous analysis.

Proof of Proposition
5.1
.

To construct
\psi_{0,\ell}
, we first identify its leading part
\hat{\psi}_{\ell}
, which will be a polynomial in
(t,\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{m},\log\mathfrak{d})
of degree at most
\ell-1
in
\log\mathfrak{d}
.
We have already fixed
\psi_{0,0}=\overline{\psi}_{0}=(\mathfrak{u},0)
, so we set
\hat{\psi}_{0}=\overline{\psi}_{0}
.
Because
\overline{\psi}_{1}
is the leading part of
{\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}}-\psi_{0,0}
, we must set
\hat{\psi}_{1}=\overline{\psi}_{1}
.
However, this identity need not hold for
\ell\geq 2.

We say a polynomial in
(t,\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{m},\log\mathfrak{d})
has
polyhomogeneity

h\in\mathbb{Z}
if it has homogeneity
h
when we drop the factors of
\log\mathfrak{d}
.
Given
h\in\mathbb{Z}
and
d\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, let
\mathcal{Q}_{h;d}
denote the set of polynomials in
(t,\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{m},\log\mathfrak{d})
whose terms have polyhomogeneity at least
h
and those of polyhomogeneity exactly
h
have degree at most
d
in
\log\mathfrak{d}
.
We construct
\hat{\psi}_{\ell}
and
\psi_{0,\ell}
inductively in the class
\mathcal{Q}_{\ell+1;\ell-1}
.

For the nonshocking component, we set

\hat{\omega}_{\ell}\coloneqq\mathsf{p}_{\ell}^{\omega}(\hat{\psi}_{<\ell}).

Using the inductive hypothesis and (
5.1
), this lies in
\mathcal{Q}_{\ell+1;\ell-2}
.
For the shocking component, we wish to solve

(5.8)

\partial_{t}\hat{\sigma}_{\ell}+\partial_{1}A_{{1}}^{{1}}(0)\partial_{x}(\sigma_{0,0}\hat{\sigma}_{\ell})=\partial_{x}[\mathsf{p}_{\ell}^{\sigma}(\hat{\psi}_{<\ell},\hat{\omega}_{\ell})]\in\mathcal{Q}_{\ell-1;\ell-2}.

Let
\overline{\partial}_{t}=\partial_{t}+\partial_{1}A_{{1}}^{{1}}(0)\sigma_{0,0}\partial_{x}
denote the
(t,\mathfrak{u})
-coordinate vector field for
t
, so
\overline{\partial}_{t}t=1
and
\overline{\partial}_{t}\mathfrak{u}=0
.
Using Lemma
2.1
, we can write the left side of (
5.8
) as

\overline{\partial}_{t}\hat{\sigma}_{\ell}+\partial_{1}A_{1}^{1}(0)(\partial_{x}\sigma_{0,0})\hat{\sigma}_{\ell}=\overline{\partial}_{t}\hat{\sigma}_{\ell}-\mathfrak{m}\hat{\sigma}_{\ell}.

By the same lemma,

(5.9)

\mathfrak{m}^{-1}=\left|t\right|+3a^{-1}b\mathfrak{u}^{2},

so
\overline{\partial}_{t}\mathfrak{m}^{-1}=-1
.
Multiplying (
5.8
) by the integrating factor
\mathfrak{m}^{-1}
, the left side simplifies to
\overline{\partial}_{t}(\mathfrak{m}^{-1}\hat{\sigma}_{\ell})
, so

(5.10)

\overline{\partial}_{t}(\mathfrak{m}^{-1}\hat{\sigma}_{\ell})=\mathfrak{m}^{-1}\partial_{x}[\mathsf{p}_{\ell}^{\sigma}(\hat{\psi}_{<\ell},\hat{\omega}_{\ell})]\in\mathcal{Q}_{\ell+1;\ell-2}.

By (
5.9
),
\mathcal{Q}_{\ell+1;\ell-2}
is spanned by terms of the form
\mathfrak{m}^{p}\mathfrak{u}^{q}\log^{r}\mathfrak{d}
for
p\in\mathbb{Z}
and
q,r\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
satisfying
q-2p\geq\ell+1
and
r\leq\ell-2
if
q-2p=\ell+1
.

We wish to find a
\overline{\partial}_{t}
-antiderivative of
\mathfrak{m}^{p}\mathfrak{u}^{q}\log^{r}\mathfrak{d}
.
Factoring out constants, it suffices to antidifferentiate
(\left|t\right|+c\mathfrak{u}^{2})^{-p}\mathfrak{u}^{q}\log^{r}(\left|t\right|+c\mathfrak{u}^{2})
in the class
\mathcal{Q}_{\ell+3;\ell-1}
.
When we integrate along
\overline{\partial}_{t}
,
\mathfrak{u}
is constant.
It follows that

\int_{-\infty}^{t}\mathfrak{u}^{q}\frac{\log^{r}(\left|s\right|+c\mathfrak{u}^{2})}{(\left|s\right|+c\mathfrak{u}^{2})^{p}}\,\overline{\text{d}}s\in\mathcal{Q}_{\ell+3;\ell-2}\quad\text{when }p\geq 2.

Similarly,

-\int_{t}^{0}\mathfrak{u}^{q}\frac{\log^{r}(\left|s\right|+c\mathfrak{u}^{2})}{(\left|s\right|+c\mathfrak{u}^{2})^{p}}\,\overline{\text{d}}s\in\mathcal{Q}_{\ell+3;\ell-2}\quad\text{when }p\leq 0.

Finally, when
p=1
,

\frac{\mathfrak{u}^{q}}{r+1}\log^{r+1}(\left|t\right|+c\mathfrak{u}^{2})\in\mathcal{Q}_{\ell+3,\ell-1}

is a suitable antiderivative.
This is the only case in which the logarithmic degree increases.
Given a
\overline{\partial}_{t}
-antiderivative
P\in\mathcal{Q}_{\ell+3;\ell-1}
of the right side of (
5.10
), we set
\hat{\sigma}_{\ell}=\mathfrak{m}P\in\mathcal{Q}_{\ell+1;\ell-1}
.

This completes the inductive construction of the leading parts
(\hat{\psi}_{\ell})
, which satisfy

\displaystyle A_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}(0)\partial_{x}\hat{\omega}_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\partial_{x}[\mathsf{p}_{\ell}^{\omega}(\hat{\psi}_{<\ell})]

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\hat{\sigma}_{\ell}+\partial_{1}A_{{1}}^{{1}}(0)\partial_{x}(\overline{\sigma}_{0}\hat{\sigma}_{\ell})

\displaystyle=\partial_{x}[\mathsf{p}_{\ell}^{\sigma}(\hat{\psi}_{<\ell},\hat{\omega}_{\ell})].

This is similar to (
5.7
), but the nonshocking equation is missing a
\partial_{t}
term on the right hand side.

Now define

(5.11)

\psi_{0,\ell}\coloneqq\hat{\psi}_{\ell}+(\overline{\psi}_{\ell+1}-\hat{\psi}_{\ell+1})\in\mathcal{Q}_{\ell+1;\ell-1}.

Because
\overline{\psi}_{\ell+1}-\hat{\psi}_{\ell+1}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\ell+2;\ell}
, we can readily verify (
5.4
).
Moreover, because
\overline{\psi}_{0}=\hat{\psi}_{0}
, the telescoping structure in (
5.11
) ensures that

\psi_{0,[L]}=\overline{\psi}_{[L]}-\overline{\psi}_{0}+\hat{\psi}_{0}+\overline{\psi}_{L+1}-\hat{\psi}_{L+1}=\overline{\psi}_{[L]}+(\overline{\psi}_{L+1}-\hat{\psi}_{L+1}).

Thus (
5.5
) follows from (
5.6
).
∎

Ultimately, we must account for the residues
F_{\ell}
.
We do so in the first row, where we include them in the forcing for
\psi_{1,\ell}
.
To show that
\psi_{1,[L]}
approximates
{\psi}_{1}^{\textnormal{out}}
, we must show that the cumulative residue largely cancels.

Lemma 5.3
.

For all
L,m,n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we have

\big|\partial_{t}^{m}\partial_{x}^{n}F_{[L]}^{\omega}\big|\lesssim_{L}\left|\log\mathfrak{d}\right|^{L+1}\mathfrak{d}^{L-2m-3n}\quad\text{and}\quad\big|\partial_{t}^{m}\partial_{x}^{n}F_{[L]}^{\sigma}\big|\lesssim_{L}\left|\log\mathfrak{d}\right|^{L+1}\mathfrak{d}^{L-2m-3n+1}.

Proof.

The polynomials
\mathsf{p}_{\ell}
are chosen so that the sum of the grid equations (
5.2
) closely approximates the true inviscid conservation law.
For example, if we sum the equations for
\omega_{0,\ell}
up to
\ell=L
using (
4.12
), we obtain

\partial_{t}\omega_{0,[L]}+\partial_{x}\sum_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq r\leq L\\
\alpha_{0}+\ldots+\alpha_{r}\leq L-r\end{subarray}}}\,\frac{\mathrm{D}^{r}A^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}(0)}{(r+1)!}\prod_{j=0}^{r}\psi_{0,\alpha_{j}}=F_{[L]}^{\omega}.

On the other hand, recalling the flux
f
satisfying
\mathrm{D}f=A
, we have

f(\psi_{0,[L]})-f(0)=\sum_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq r\leq L\\
\alpha_{j}\leq L\end{subarray}}}\,\frac{\mathrm{D}^{r}A(0)}{(r+1)!}\prod_{j=0}^{r}\psi_{0,\alpha_{j}}+\mathcal{O}_{L}\big(|\psi_{0,[L]}|^{L+2}\big).

Therefore Proposition
5.1
yields

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\omega_{0,[L]}+\partial_{x}f^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}(\psi_{0,[L]})

\displaystyle=F_{[L]}^{\omega}+\partial_{x}\sum_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq r\leq L\\
\alpha_{j}\leq L\\
\alpha_{0}+\ldots+\alpha_{r}>L-r\end{subarray}}}\,\frac{\mathrm{D}^{r}A^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}(0)}{(r+1)!}\prod_{j=0}^{r}\psi_{0,\alpha_{j}}+\mathcal{O}_{L}(\mathfrak{d}^{L-1})

\displaystyle=F_{[L]}^{\omega}+\mathcal{O}_{L}(\left|\log\mathfrak{d}\right|^{L}\mathfrak{d}^{L-1}).

Subtracting the conservation law (
3.2
), we find

\partial_{t}({\omega}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}}-\omega_{0,[L]})+\partial_{x}[f^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}({\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}})-f^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}(\psi_{0,[L]})]=F_{[L]}^{\omega}+\mathcal{O}_{L}\big(\left|\log\mathfrak{d}\right|^{L}\mathfrak{d}^{L-1}\big).

Now using (
5.5
) and rearranging, we see that
\big|F_{[L]}^{\omega}\big|\lesssim_{L}\left|\log\mathfrak{d}\right|^{L}\mathfrak{d}^{L-1}.

In fact, we handled the above equations somewhat sloppily (as detailed in the next subsection) and can improve the result
a posteriori
.
Indeed, Proposition
5.1
states that
F_{L+1}^{\omega}\lesssim_{L}\left|\log\mathfrak{d}\right|^{L+1}\mathfrak{d}^{L}
.
Bounding
F_{[L+1]}^{\omega}
as above, we therefore obtain the improved bound

F_{[L]}^{\omega}=F_{[L+1]}^{\omega}-F_{L+1}^{\omega}\lesssim_{L}\left|\log\mathfrak{d}\right|^{L+1}\mathfrak{d}^{L}.

The
\sigma
-equations involve an approximation of
F
that is one-degree higher order, so similar reasoning yields
\big|F_{[L]}^{\omega}\big|\lesssim_{L}\left|\log\mathfrak{d}\right|^{L+1}\mathfrak{d}^{L+1}.

Finally, these estimates behave as expected under differentiation because
F_{\ell}
is a polynomial in
(t,\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{m},\log\mathfrak{d})
, so the lemma follows.
∎

5.2.
The zeroth column

We next analyze the zeroth column
(\psi_{k,0})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
.
Because
{\Omega}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}=0
, we take
\omega_{k,0}=0
.
The resulting equations for the shocking components
\sigma_{k,0}
are identical to those encountered in the Burgers equation in
[
22
]
.
There, the second and third authors constructed solutions
\sigma_{k,0}
as polynomials in
(\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{u})
of homogeneity
-4k+1
.
This corresponds to solving the grid equation for
\sigma_{k,0}
from zero data at past infinity.
We work with the same solutions here; the following restates part of
[
22
, Proposition 3.9]
.

Proposition 5.4
.

For all
k,m,n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
\omega_{k,0}=0
and
|\partial_{t}^{m}\partial_{x}^{n}\sigma_{k,0}|\lesssim_{k,n,m}\mathfrak{d}^{-4k-2m-3n+1}.

Because the difference
{\psi}_{k}^{\textnormal{out}}-\psi_{k,0}
serves as data for the next column
\psi_{k,1}
, we must control it before constructing the remaining grid terms.
The outer expansion (
3.3
) is governed by the linear operator
\partial_{t}+\partial_{x}[A({\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}})\,\cdot\,]
.
We therefore state a general estimate for this operator, which we prove in Section
6.3
.

Proposition 5.5
.

Fix
k\in\mathbb{N}
and let
\partial_{t}p+\partial_{x}[A({\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}})p]=\partial_{x}h+F
in a causally closed domain
\mathcal{D}
with smooth Cauchy hypersurface
\Sigma
.
Let
a_{k}=\textup{{1}}(k\geq 2)
and suppose

\displaystyle|\nabla^{n}(p|_{\Sigma})|\lesssim_{n}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k+1}\mathfrak{d}^{-3n},\quad|\partial_{t}^{m}\partial_{x}^{n}h_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}|\lesssim_{m,n}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k-m+1}\mathfrak{d}^{-3n},

\displaystyle|\partial_{t}^{m}\partial_{x}^{n}F_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}|\lesssim_{m,n}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k-m}\mathfrak{d}^{-3n},\quad|\partial_{t}^{m}\partial_{x}^{n}(\partial_{x}h_{1}+F_{1})|\lesssim_{m,n}\left|\log\mathfrak{e}\right|^{a_{k}}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k-m+1}\mathfrak{d}^{-3n-2}

for all
m,n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
Then
|\partial_{t}^{m}\partial_{x}^{n}p|\lesssim_{m,n}\left|\log\mathfrak{e}\right|\mathfrak{e}^{-2k-m+1}\mathfrak{d}^{-3n}
in
\mathcal{D}
.

With this, we can control the data
{\psi}_{k}^{\textnormal{out}}-\psi_{k,0}
of
\psi_{k,1}
.

Proposition 5.6
.

For all
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
m,n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

(5.12)

|\partial_{t}^{m}\partial_{x}^{n}({\psi}_{k}^{\textnormal{out}}-\psi_{k,0})|\lesssim_{k,m,n}\left|\log\mathfrak{e}\right|\mathfrak{e}^{-2k-m+1}\mathfrak{d}^{-3n}.

Together, Propositions
5.4
and
5.6
imply Proposition
3.2
.

Proof.

Let
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
denote a variation on
\mathcal{A}
from (
4.12
) defined with
\mathrm{D}^{r}A({\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}})
in place of
\mathrm{D}^{r}A(0)
, and similarly for
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}
.
Then by (
3.3
),
p_{k}\coloneqq{\psi}_{k}^{\textnormal{out}}-\psi_{k,0}
satisfies

(5.13)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}(\psi_{k,0}+p_{k})+\partial_{x}[A({\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}})(\psi_{k,0}+p_{k})]

\displaystyle=-\partial_{x}\sum_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}\#\kappa\geq 1,\,\kappa_{j}\geq 1\\
\left|\kappa\right|=k\end{subarray}}}\,\tilde{\mathcal{A}}(\psi_{\kappa,0}+p_{\kappa})

\displaystyle+\partial_{x}\sum_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}\kappa_{\geq 1}\hskip 0.5pt\geq 1\\
\left|\kappa\right|=k-1\end{subarray}}}\,\tilde{\mathcal{B}}(\psi_{\kappa,0}+p_{\kappa}).

We expand the the multilinear terms on the right and group them into terms with no factors of
p
and terms with at least one factor of
p
, writing:

(5.14)

\partial_{t}(\psi_{k,0}+p_{k})+\partial_{x}[A({\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}})(\psi_{k,0}+p_{k})]=\partial_{x}(h_{0}+h_{\geq 1}).

Proceeding by induction, suppose we have proved the proposition for
k^{\prime}<k
.
Because
\mathring{\psi}
is smooth and
{\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}}
is smooth away from the origin, finite speed of propagation implies that the desired estimate holds on any closed set disjoint from the origin.
It thus suffices to prove the estimate in
\mathcal{D}\coloneqq c\mathcal{I}
for the region
\mathcal{I}
defined in (
4.18
) and a constant
c>0
chosen so that
c\mathcal{I}\subset\{\mathfrak{e}\leq 1/2\}
.
By the construction of
\mathcal{I}
,
\mathcal{D}
is causally closed with Cauchy hypersurface
c\Gamma
, also from (
4.18
).

First consider
h_{\geq 1}
, which only involves differences
p_{k^{\prime}}
with
k^{\prime}<k
.
If
k^{\prime}=0
, (
5.5
) from Proposition
5.1
implies that
p_{0}\lesssim\mathfrak{d}^{2}
, while
\psi_{0,0}\lesssim\mathfrak{d}
.
Note that there is no log-factor in
p_{0}
.
If
1\leq k^{\prime}<k
, (
3.5
) and Proposition
5.4
yield

|\psi_{k^{\prime},0}|\lesssim\mathfrak{d}^{-4k^{\prime}+1}\lesssim\mathfrak{e}^{-2k^{\prime}}\mathfrak{d}\gg\left|\log\mathfrak{e}\right|\mathfrak{e}^{-2k^{\prime}+1}.

In each case, our bound on
\psi_{k^{\prime},0}
exceeds that on
p_{k^{\prime}}
, so
h_{\geq 1}
is dominated by terms with a single factor of
p
.

These terms of
h_{\geq 1}
involve products of the form
p_{\kappa_{i}}\prod_{j\neq i}\psi_{\kappa_{j},0}
.
Using (
3.5
), the inductive hypothesis, and Proposition
5.4
, we find

\displaystyle p_{\kappa_{i}}\prod_{j\neq i}\psi_{\kappa_{j},0}\lesssim\left|\log\mathfrak{d}\right|\mathfrak{e}^{-2\kappa_{i}+1}\mathfrak{d}^{\sum_{j\neq i}(-4\kappa_{j}+1)}

\displaystyle=\left|\log\mathfrak{d}\right|\mathfrak{e}^{-2\kappa_{i}+1}\mathfrak{d}^{-4(\left|\kappa\right|-\kappa_{i})+\#\kappa}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left|\log\mathfrak{d}\right|\mathfrak{e}^{-2\left|\kappa\right|+1}\mathfrak{d}^{\#\kappa}.

The
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
-terms in (
5.13
) satisfy
\left|\kappa\right|=k
and
\#\kappa\geq 1
, so we conclude that these contribute no more than
\left|\log\mathfrak{e}\right|\mathfrak{e}^{-2k+1}\mathfrak{d}
to
h_{\geq 1}
.

For
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}
, we note that
\left|\kappa\right|=k-1
and
\#\kappa\geq 0
, while the additional
\partial_{x}
causes a loss of
\mathfrak{d}^{-3}
by the inductive hypothesis.
Hence a similar calculation yields

\max\Big\{p_{\kappa_{i}}\partial_{x}\psi_{\kappa_{0},0}\prod_{j\neq i,0}\psi_{\kappa_{j},0},\,\partial_{x}p_{\kappa_{0}}\prod_{j>0}\psi_{\kappa_{j},0}\Big\}\lesssim\left|\log\mathfrak{e}\right|\mathfrak{e}^{-2k+3}\mathfrak{d}^{-3}\lesssim\left|\log\mathfrak{e}\right|\mathfrak{e}^{-2k+1}\mathfrak{d}.

Therefore
\left|h_{\geq 1}\right|\lesssim_{k}\left|\log\mathfrak{e}\right|\mathfrak{e}^{-2k+1}\mathfrak{d}.

Moreover, as noted earlier,
p_{0}
has no log-loss.
It follows that when
k=1
, we in fact have
\left|h_{\geq 1}\right|\lesssim\mathfrak{e}^{-2k+1}\mathfrak{d}.

Recalling the indicator function
a_{k}=\textup{{1}}(k\geq 2)
, we can thus write

\left|h_{\geq 1}\right|\lesssim\left|\log\mathfrak{e}\right|^{a_{k}}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k+1}\mathfrak{d}\quad\text{and}\quad\left|\partial_{x}h_{\geq 1}\right|\lesssim\left|\log\mathfrak{e}\right|^{a_{k}}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k+1}\mathfrak{d}^{-2}.

Next, we show that
h_{0}
nearly balances the terms on the left of (
5.14
) that do not involve
p_{k}
.
We wish to define
h^{\textnormal{res}}
by

\partial_{x}h^{\textnormal{res}}=\partial_{x}h_{0}-\partial_{t}\psi_{k,0}-\partial_{x}[A({\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}})\psi_{k,0}]=h_{0}-\partial_{t}\sigma_{k,0}e_{1}-\partial_{x}[A_{1}({\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}})\sigma_{k,0}].

To select a solution of this equation, we note that (
4.17
) yields

\partial_{t}\sigma_{k,0}=-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{1}A_{1}^{1}(0)\partial_{x}\sum_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq k^{\prime}\leq k\end{subarray}}}\,\sigma_{k^{\prime},0}\sigma_{k-k^{\prime},0}+B_{1}^{1}(0)\partial_{x}^{2}\sigma_{k-1,0}.

We therefore set

h^{\textnormal{res}}\coloneqq h_{0}+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{1}A_{1}^{1}(0)\sum_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq k^{\prime}\leq k\end{subarray}}}\,\sigma_{k^{\prime},0}\sigma_{k-k^{\prime},0}e_{1}-B_{1}^{1}(0)\partial_{x}\sigma_{k-1,0}e_{1}-A_{1}({\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}})\sigma_{k,0}.

Projecting on the nonshocking directions, we find
h_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}^{\textnormal{res}}=(h_{0})_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}-A_{1}^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}({\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}})\sigma_{k,0}.

Note that (
5.6
) yields
{\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}}\lesssim\mathfrak{d}
.
Because
A_{1}^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}(0)=0
, the mean value theorem implies that
A_{1}^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}({\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}})\lesssim\mathfrak{d}
, so
A_{1}^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}({\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}})\sigma_{k,0}\lesssim\mathfrak{d}^{-4k+2}
.
Next, (
5.13
) shows that the
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
-terms in
h_{0}
are controlled by

\prod_{j}\psi_{\kappa_{j},0}\lesssim\mathfrak{d}^{-4\left|\kappa\right|+\#\kappa+1}\lesssim\mathfrak{d}^{-4k+2}.

One can readily check the same bound for the
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}
-terms, so in total (
3.5
) yields

|h_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}^{\textnormal{res}}|\lesssim\mathfrak{d}^{-4k+2}\lesssim\mathfrak{e}^{-2k+1}.

Now consider the shocking component.
We view
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
-terms with
\#\kappa\geq 2
and
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}
-terms with
\#\kappa\geq 1
as error.
Using Proposition
5.4
, these are bounded by
\mathfrak{d}^{-4k+3}
.
For the low-order terms, we exploit cancellation with
\partial_{t}\sigma_{k,0}
, writing

\displaystyle h_{1}^{\textnormal{res}}=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{0\leq k^{\prime}\leq k}[\partial_{1}A_{1}^{1}({\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}})-\partial_{1}A_{1}^{1}(0)]\sigma_{k^{\prime},0}\sigma_{k-k^{\prime},0}+[B_{1}^{1}({\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}})-B_{1}^{1}

\displaystyle(0)]\partial_{x}\sigma_{k-1,0}

\displaystyle+\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{d}^{-4k+3}).

Because
{\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}}\lesssim\mathfrak{d}
, the differences involving
A
and
B
are order
\mathfrak{d}
, so Proposition
5.4
yields
h_{1}^{\textnormal{res}}\lesssim\mathfrak{d}^{-4k+3}
.
Taking a derivative, we find

|\partial_{x}h_{1}^{\textnormal{res}}|\lesssim\mathfrak{d}^{-4k}=\mathfrak{d}^{-4k+2}\mathfrak{d}^{-2}\lesssim\mathfrak{e}^{-2k+1}\mathfrak{d}^{-2}.

Now let
h\coloneqq h^{\textnormal{res}}+h_{\geq 1}
, so
\partial_{t}p_{k}+\partial_{x}[A({\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}})p_{k}]=\partial_{x}h
.
Gathering the above results, we have

\left|h_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}\right|\lesssim\mathfrak{e}^{-2k+1}\quad\text{and}\quad\left|\partial_{x}h_{1}\right|\lesssim\left|\log\mathfrak{e}\right|^{a_{k}}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k+1}\mathfrak{d}^{-2}.

We therefore satisfy the hypotheses of Proposition
5.5
with
F\equiv 0
and
m=n=0
.
Throughout our estimates,
\partial_{t}
and
\partial_{x}
cause
\mathfrak{d}^{-2}\lesssim\mathfrak{e}^{-1}
and
\mathfrak{d}^{-3}
losses, respectively, so the hypotheses in fact hold for all
m,n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
Thus (
5.12
) follows from Proposition
5.5
.
∎

5.3.
Renormalization

To treat the remaining grid terms, we require a few more technical tools.
We will analyze hyperbolic equations of the form

(5.15)

\partial_{t}\phi+M\partial_{x}\phi=\partial_{x}F,

where
M
is a constant full-rank matrix and
\phi
is a vector-valued unknown.
For example, from (
4.16
), we might have
\phi=\omega_{k,\ell}
,
M=A_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}(0)
, and
F
a spatial derivative of a function of
\psi_{\leq k,<\ell}
.

Near the origin, dynamics are largely driven by the shocking component
\sigma
.
Its characteristic vector field is parallel to
\partial_{t}
but transverse to
\partial_{x}
at the origin, so
\partial_{t}\sigma
is better behaved than
\partial_{x}\sigma
.
For this region, it is often advantageous to rearrange or “renormalize” (
5.15
) so that it is driven by time derivatives rather than space derivatives.
We formalize this procedure in the following lemma.

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
M
be a constant full-rank matrix, and
F
and
G
be sufficiently smooth.
If
\phi
satisfies
\partial_{t}\phi+M\partial_{x}\phi=\partial_{x}^{n+1}F+G,
then

(5.16)

(\partial_{t}+M\partial_{x})(\phi-\mathbf{R}_{n}[M,F])=(-1)^{n+1}M^{-(n+1)}\partial_{t}^{n+1}F+G,

where
\mathbf{R}_{n}[M,\,\cdot\,]\colon\mathcal{C}^{k}\to\mathcal{C}^{k-n}
is the linear map

\mathbf{R}_{n}[M,F]\coloneqq\sum_{j=0}^{n}(-1)^{j}M^{-(j+1)}\partial_{t}^{j}\partial_{x}^{n-j}F.

Proof.

Proceeding by induction, we first consider
n=0
.
Direct computation yields

(\partial_{t}+M\partial_{x})(\phi-M^{-1}F)=-M^{-1}\partial_{t}F+G.

Next, assuming we have shown (
5.16
) for some
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we show it for
n+1
.
Using
\partial_{x}^{n+2}F=\partial_{x}^{n+1}(\partial_{x}F)
, the inductive hypothesis yields

(\partial_{t}+M\partial_{x})(\phi-\mathbf{R}_{n}[M,\partial_{x}F])=\partial_{x}[(-1)^{n+1}M^{-(n+1)}\partial_{t}^{n+1}F]+G.

Applying the
n=0
case, we find

(\partial_{t}+M\partial_{x})(\phi-\mathbf{R}_{n}[M,\partial_{x}F]-(-1)^{n+1}M^{-(n+2)}\partial_{t}^{n+1}F)=(-1)^{n}M^{-(n+2)}\partial_{t}^{n+2}F+G.

Noting that
\mathbf{R}_{n+1}[M,F]=\mathbf{R}_{n}[M,\partial_{x}F]+(-1)^{n+1}M^{-(n+2)}\partial_{t}^{n+1}F
, the result follows from induction.
∎

5.4.
Integral bounds

After renormalization, we project (
5.15
) onto an eigenvector of
A
and integrate along its characteristic.
Here, we record bounds on the resulting integrals.
In the following, let
\mathcal{D}\subset\{\mathfrak{e}\leq 1/2\}
be a causally closed domain with Cauchy hypersurface
\Sigma
.
Let
\tilde{\gamma}_{I}
denote the characteristic of
\partial_{t}+\lambda_{(I)}({\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}})\partial_{x}
beginning at
\Sigma
and ending at some
(t,x)\in\mathcal{D}
.
Let
\gamma_{I^{\prime}}
and
\gamma_{1}
denote “frozen” characteristics corresponding to
\partial_{t}+\lambda_{(I^{\prime})}(0)\partial_{x}
and
\partial_{t}+\partial_{1}A_{1}^{1}(0)\sigma_{0,0}\partial_{x}
, respectively, with the same initial and terminal behavior.
All are affinely parameterized, meaning the parameter increases at rate one along the defining vector field.

Lemma 5.8
.

Fix
2\leq I^{\prime}\leq N
and
(t,x)\in\mathcal{D}
.
For all
r>1
, and
a,q\geq 0
,

(5.17)

\displaystyle\int\mathfrak{e}^{-r}\circ\tilde{\gamma}_{I^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{r}\mathfrak{e}^{-r+1}(t,x),

(5.18)

\displaystyle\int\mathfrak{d}^{-2}\mathfrak{e}^{-r}\circ\tilde{\gamma}_{I^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{r}\mathfrak{e}^{-r+1/3}(t,x),

(5.19)

\displaystyle\int\left|\log\mathfrak{e}\right|^{a}\mathfrak{e}^{-r}\mathfrak{d}^{-q}\circ\tilde{\gamma}_{1}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{r,a,q}\left|\log\mathfrak{e}(t,x)\right|^{a}\mathfrak{e}^{-r+1}(t,x)\mathfrak{d}^{-q}(t,x),

(5.20)

\displaystyle\int\mathfrak{e}^{-1}\mathfrak{d}^{-q}\circ\tilde{\gamma}_{1}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{q}\left|\log\mathfrak{e}(t,x)\right|\mathfrak{d}^{-q}(t,x).

Moreover, the same estimates hold with
\gamma
in place of
\tilde{\gamma}
.

To treat the true shocking characteristic
\tilde{\gamma}_{1}
, it is helpful to introduce a corresponding Lagrangian coordinate
u
, termed the eikonal function.
It satisfies

(5.21)

\partial_{t}u+\lambda_{(1)}({\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}})\partial_{x}u=0,\quad u(t_{0},x)=x-c,

where
c\in\mathbb{R}
is chosen so that
u(0,0)=0
.
We expect
u
to resemble a multiple of
\mathfrak{u}
near the origin.
We only require a weaker result, that the “cubic distance” associated to
u
is similar to
\mathfrak{d}
.

Lemma 5.9
.

In
\mathcal{D}
,
\mathfrak{d}^{2}\asymp\left|t\right|+u^{2}
.

Proof.

We first control
\mathfrak{d}^{2}\partial_{x}u
.
By
(H6)
, (
2.5
), and (
2.8
),

(5.22)

\lambda_{(1)}({\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}})=-a\mathfrak{u}+\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{d}^{2})\quad\text{and}\quad\partial_{x}[\lambda_{(1)}({\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}})]=-\mathfrak{m}+\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{d}^{-1}).

Also, (
2.7
) yields
(\partial_{x}-a\mathfrak{u}\partial_{x})\mathfrak{u}=0
.
Writing
L\coloneqq\partial_{t}+\lambda_{(1)}({\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}})\partial_{x}
, (
2.4
) thus implies

L(\mathfrak{d}^{2})=[\partial_{t}-a\mathfrak{u}\partial_{x}+\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{d}^{2})\partial_{x}](\mathfrak{d}^{2})=-1+\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{d}).

Using these observations, we commute (
5.21
) with
\mathfrak{d}^{2}\partial_{x}
to find

L(\mathfrak{d}^{2}\partial_{x}u)=\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{d}^{-1})\mathfrak{d}^{2}\partial_{x}u.

Now
\mathfrak{d}^{-1}
is integrable along the characteristic
\tilde{\gamma}_{1}
, which resembles
\partial_{t}
near the origin.
It follows from Grönwall that
\mathfrak{d}^{2}\partial_{x}u
is continuous.
Since it is initially positive, it must remain so.
Hence

(5.23)

\partial_{x}u\sim\texttt{c}\mathfrak{d}^{-2}=\texttt{c}\partial_{x}\mathfrak{u}

near the origin, for some constant
\texttt{c}>0
.

Next, let
\gamma(t)
denote the
x
-coordinate of the unique root of
u
at time
t
, so
u\big(t,\gamma(t)\big)=0
.
By (
5.21
), this satisfies
\dot{\gamma}=\lambda_{(1)}\circ{\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}}\big(t,\gamma(t)\big)
with
\gamma(0)=0
.
Now (
2.3
) implies
\left|a\mathfrak{u}(t,x)\right|\leq\left|x\right|\left|t\right|^{-1}
.
Thus (
5.22
) yields

\left|\dot{\gamma}\right|\leq\left|t\right|^{-1}\left|\gamma\right|+F

with
F\lesssim\mathfrak{d}^{2}
.
By Lemma
2.1
, there exists
C>0
such that
F\leq C\left|t\right|
where
\left|\gamma\right|\leq\left|t\right|^{3/2}
.
For the moment, cut off
F
by
0
where
\left|t\right|>\left|t\right|^{3/2}
and let
\hat{\gamma}
denote the corresponding solution.
Then Grönwall yields
\hat{\gamma}\lesssim\left|t\right|^{2}
.
Because
\left|t\right|\leq\left|t\right|^{3/2}
for
\left|t\right|
small,
\hat{\gamma}
does not experience the
F
-cutoff.
It follows that
\hat{\gamma}=\gamma
near the origin and
\gamma\lesssim\left|t\right|^{2}
.

We now fix
t<0
and integrate (
5.23
) in
x
from
\gamma(t)
to find

u\lesssim\mathfrak{u}+\mathfrak{u}(t,\gamma)\lesssim\mathfrak{u}+\left|t\right|.

The second bound follows from Lemma
2.1
.
Then

\left|t\right|+u^{2}\lesssim\left|t\right|+(\mathfrak{u}+\left|t\right|)^{2}\lesssim\mathfrak{d}^{2}.

To show the other direction, we invert (
5.23
) to find

\partial_{u}x\lesssim\mathfrak{d}^{2}\lesssim\max\{\left|t\right|,\left|x\right|^{2/3}\}.

Holding
t
constant and integrating in
u
from
u=0
, this yields

x-\gamma(t)\lesssim\left|tu\right|+\left|u\right|^{3}.

By Young’s inequality,

x\lesssim\left|tu\right|+\left|u\right|^{3}+\left|t\right|^{2}\lesssim\left|t\right|^{3/2}+\left|u\right|^{3}.

Therefore Lemma
2.1
yields

\mathfrak{d}^{2}\lesssim\left|t\right|+\left|x\right|^{2/3}\lesssim\left|t\right|+u^{2}.\qed

With this bound in place, we can control the integrals (
5.17
)–(
5.20
).

Proof of Lemma
5.8
.

We only treat the unfrozen characteristics
\tilde{\gamma}
, as the proofs in the frozen case
\gamma
are similar but simpler.
Let
s\in[s_{0},s_{1}]
denote the affine parameter, which is only determined up to a shift, and let the upright
\big(\mathsf{t}(s),\mathsf{x}(s)\big)
denote the coordinates of
\tilde{\gamma}
.

We first consider the nonshocking bounds.
Set
s_{1}=-\mathfrak{e}(t,x)
.
Modulo a constant factor, the time component of
\partial_{t}+\lambda_{(I^{\prime})}\partial_{x}
is no smaller than its space component.
One can thus readily check that
\left|s\right|\asymp\mathfrak{e}\circ\tilde{\gamma}_{I^{\prime}}(s)
for all
s\in[s_{0},s_{1}]
.
This immediately implies (
5.17
), as

\int\mathfrak{e}^{-r}\circ\tilde{\gamma}_{I^{\prime}}\asymp\int_{s_{0}}^{s_{1}}\left|s\right|^{-r}\;\mathrm{d}s\lesssim\left|s_{1}\right|^{-r+1}\asymp\mathfrak{e}^{-r+1}(t,x).

Next, it suffices to focus on a neighborhood of the origin on which
\lambda_{(I^{\prime})}({\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}})
is nonzero.
By symmetry, we may assume
\lambda_{(I^{\prime})}({\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}})\geq c>0
for some
c>0
that may change from line to line.
We then claim it suffices to treat
x\geq 0
.
Indeed, suppose we parametrize by time instead, which changes our integrals by no more than a constant factor.
Given
x>0
, let
\tilde{\gamma}_{I^{\prime}}^{\pm}
denote the characteristics ending at
(t,\pm x)
.
Then
\mathsf{t}^{-}=\mathsf{t}^{+}
, so by ODE uniqueness,
\mathsf{x}^{-}
can never coincide with
\mathsf{x}^{+}
.
It follows that
\left|\mathsf{x}^{-}\right|\geq\left|\mathsf{x}^{+}\right|
, so the integrand of (
5.18
) is larger for
\tilde{\gamma}_{I^{\prime}}^{+}
than for
\tilde{\gamma}_{I^{\prime}}^{-}
.
We are thus free to assume that
x\geq 0
in
(t,x)
.

To show (
5.18
), we return to an affine parametrization ending at
s_{1}=-\mathfrak{e}(t,x)
, so
\mathfrak{e}\asymp\left|s\right|
in the integral.
We thus need to control
\mathfrak{d}
.
It suffices to work within a small ball
B
about the origin, as contributions from outside
B
are order
1
.
Assume
(t,x)\in B
with
x\geq 0
.
Within
B
,
\tilde{\gamma}_{I^{\prime}}
is roughly a straight line with positive slope, while the curves
\mathsf{x}=\pm\left|\mathsf{t}\right|^{3/2}
are nearly vertical.
The characteristic thus crosses these at two locations, with parameters
s_{-}<s_{+}
.
We can easily check that

(5.24)

\left|\mathsf{t}\right|\big|_{[s_{-},s_{+}]}\asymp\mathfrak{e}(t,x)\quad\text{and}\quad s_{+}-s_{-}\asymp\left|\mathsf{t}\right|^{3/2}\asymp\mathfrak{e}(t,x)^{3/2}.

If
x<\left|t\right|^{3/2}
, then
s_{+}>s_{1}
, and we instead set
s_{+}=s_{1}
.
The characteristic leaves
B
at some
s_{B}<s_{-}
.
Let
I_{1}
,
I_{2}
, and
I_{3}
denote the contributions to the integral in (
5.18
) from
s
in
[s_{B},s_{-}],[s_{-},s_{+}],
and
[s_{+},s_{1}]
, respectively.
In
I_{1}
,
\mathfrak{d}\asymp\left|\mathsf{x}\right|^{1/3}
and
\left|\mathsf{x}\right|\asymp s_{+}-s
.
Using Lemma
2.1
, we find

I_{1}=\int_{s_{B}}^{s_{-}}\mathfrak{d}^{-2}\mathfrak{e}^{-r}\circ\tilde{\gamma}_{I^{\prime}}\lesssim\int_{-\infty}^{s_{-}}\left|s\right|^{-r}(s_{+}-s)^{-2/3}\;\mathrm{d}s\asymp\left|s_{-}\right|^{-r+1/3}\asymp\mathfrak{e}(t,x)^{-r+1/3}.

For
I_{2}
,
\mathfrak{d}\asymp\left|\mathsf{t}\right|^{1/2}
, so (
5.24
) yields

I_{2}=\int_{s_{-}}^{s_{+}}\mathfrak{d}^{-2}\mathfrak{e}^{-r}\circ\tilde{\gamma}_{I^{\prime}}\asymp\mathfrak{e}(t,x)^{-r-1}(s_{+}-s_{-})\asymp\mathfrak{e}(t,x)^{-r+1/2}.

Finally, in
I_{3}
,
\mathfrak{d}\asymp\left|\mathsf{x}\right|^{1/3}
and
\left|\mathsf{x}\right|\asymp s-s_{-}
, so mimicking
I_{1}
,

I_{3}=\int_{s_{+}}^{s_{1}}\mathfrak{d}^{-2}\mathfrak{e}^{-r}\circ\tilde{\gamma}_{I^{\prime}}\lesssim\int_{s_{+}}^{s_{1}}\left|s\right|^{-r}(s-s_{-})^{-2/3}\;\mathrm{d}s\lesssim\mathfrak{e}(t,x)^{-r+1/3}.

In combination, we obtain (
5.18
).

Turning to the shocking characteristic
\tilde{\gamma}_{1}
, we recall the eikonal function
u
defined in (
5.21
) and switch to coordinates
(\mathsf{t},u)
.
Then
\tilde{\gamma}_{1}
is constant in
u
, so throughout we let
u=u(t,x)
be the value of the eikonal function at the endpoint.
Because
\tilde{\gamma}_{1}
is constant in
u
, we can use
s=\mathsf{t}
as our parameter.

For simplicity, we focus on (
5.19
) with
a=0
.
When
a>0
, the result will follow from analogous computations.
Combining Lemmas
2.1
and
5.9
, we can check that

\mathfrak{e}\asymp\left|t\right|+\mathfrak{d}^{3}\asymp\left|t\right|+\left|u\right|^{3}.

It thus suffices to control

\int\mathfrak{e}^{-r}\mathfrak{d}^{-q}\circ\tilde{\gamma_{1}}\asymp\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{(\left|s\right|^{r}+\left|u\right|^{3r})(\left|s\right|^{q/2}+\left|u\right|^{q})},

where
t_{0}
is the earliest time in our integral.
We generally assume
r>1
, which corresponds to (
5.19
); we make brief asides to treat the
r=1
case (
5.20
).

Case 1
: Suppose
\left|t\right|<\left|u\right|^{3}
, in which case
\mathfrak{d}(t,x)\asymp\left|u\right|
and
\mathfrak{e}(t,x)\asymp\left|u\right|^{3}
.
We break the integral into
I_{1},I_{2}
, and
I_{3}
over the intervals
[-\left|u\right|^{3},t]
,
[-\left|u\right|^{2},-\left|u\right|^{3}],

[t_{0},-\left|u\right|^{2}]
, respectively.
We may assume that these three intervals are nontrivial: otherwise we set their contribution to zero, and the calculation simplifies.

In
I_{1}
, we have
\mathfrak{d}(s,u)\asymp\left|u\right|
and
\mathfrak{e}(s,u)\asymp\left|u\right|^{3}
, so

I_{1}\asymp\left|u\right|^{-3r-q}\int_{-\left|u\right|^{3}}^{t}\;\mathrm{d}s\lesssim\left|u\right|^{-3(r-1)-q}\asymp\mathfrak{e}(t,x)^{-r+1}\mathfrak{d}(t,x)^{-q}.

In
I_{2}
,
\mathfrak{d}\asymp\left|u\right|
but
\mathfrak{e}\asymp\left|s\right|
.
Hence

I_{2}\asymp\left|u\right|^{-q}\int_{-\left|u\right|^{2}}^{-\left|u\right|^{3}}\left|s\right|^{-r}\;\mathrm{d}s\lesssim u^{-3(r-1)-q}\asymp\mathfrak{e}(t,x)^{-r+1}\mathfrak{d}(t,x)^{-q}.

Additionally, if
r=1
, we pick up an additional factor of
\left|\log\left|u\right|\right|\asymp\left|\log\mathfrak{e}(t,x)\right|
, as in (
5.20
).
Finally, in
I_{3}
,
\mathfrak{d}\asymp\left|s\right|^{1/2}
and
\mathfrak{e}\asymp\left|s\right|
, so

I_{3}\asymp\int_{t_{0}}^{-\left|u\right|^{2}}\left|s\right|^{-r-\frac{q}{2}}\;\mathrm{d}s\lesssim u^{-2(r-1)-q}\lesssim\mathfrak{e}(t,x)^{-\frac{2(r-1)}{3}}\mathfrak{d}(t,x)^{-q}.

Similarly, we generate a log if
r=1
and
q=0
.
Collecting these bounds, we find (
5.19
) and (
5.20
).

Case 2
: Next suppose
\left|u\right|^{3}\leq\left|t\right|<\left|u\right|^{2}
, so
\mathfrak{d}(t,x)\asymp\left|u\right|
but
\mathfrak{e}(t,x)\asymp\left|t\right|
.
We break the integral into
I_{2}
and
I_{3}
over
[-\left|u\right|^{2},t]
and
[t_{0},-\left|u\right|^{2}]
.
By the bounds used in Case 1,

I_{2}\asymp\left|u\right|^{-q}\int_{-\left|u\right|^{2}}^{t}\left|s\right|^{-r}\;\mathrm{d}s\lesssim u^{-q}\left|t\right|^{-r+1}\asymp\mathfrak{e}(t,x)^{-r+1}\mathfrak{d}(t,x)^{-q}.

Again, we pick up
\left|\log\left|t\right|\right|\asymp\left|\log\mathfrak{e}(t,x)\right|
if
r=1
.
Next,

I_{3}\asymp\int_{t_{0}}^{-\left|u\right|^{2}}\left|s\right|^{-r-\frac{q}{2}}\;\mathrm{d}s\lesssim u^{-2(r-1)-q}\lesssim\mathfrak{e}(t,x)^{-r+1}\mathfrak{d}(t,x)^{-q}.

And again,
r=1
and
q=0
produces a log.
Together, these yield (
5.19
) and (
5.20
).

Case 3
: Finally, suppose
\left|t\right|\geq\left|u\right|^{2}
, so
\mathfrak{d}(t,x)\asymp\left|t\right|^{1/2}
and
\mathfrak{e}(t,x)\asymp\left|t\right|
.
Let
I_{3}
denote the entire integral.
Using the bounds above,

I_{3}\lesssim\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\left|s\right|^{-r-\frac{q}{2}}\;\mathrm{d}s\lesssim\left|t\right|^{-r-\frac{q}{2}+1}\asymp\mathfrak{e}(t,x)^{-r+1}\mathfrak{d}(t,x)^{-q}.

If
r=1
and
q=0
, we accrue
\left|\log\mathfrak{e}\right|
.
This completes the proof of (
5.19
) and (
5.20
), and the lemma.
∎

5.5.
Bulk grid estimates

We now control the “bulk” grid terms
\psi_{k,\ell}=(\sigma_{k,\ell},\omega_{k,\ell})^{\top}
with
k,\ell\geq 1
.
Recall that
\omega_{k,\ell}
and
\sigma_{k,\ell}
satisfy the linear advection equations (
4.16
) and (
4.17
) in
\mathcal{I}_{\beta}
with data (
4.20
) on
\Gamma_{\beta}
.

Proposition 5.10
.

Fix
\varepsilon\in\big(0,\tfrac{1}{6}\big]
and
\beta\in\big(\tfrac{1}{2+\varepsilon},\tfrac{1}{2}\big)
.
Then for all
k,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,
m,n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

(5.25)

\big|\partial_{t}^{m}\partial_{x}^{n}\psi_{k,\ell}\big|\lesssim_{\varepsilon,\beta,k,\ell,m,n}\nu^{(\ell-1-3\varepsilon)\beta/3}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k-m+1}\mathfrak{d}^{-3n}\quad\text{in }\mathcal{I}_{\beta}\setminus\mathcal{I}_{1/2}.

We often take
\varepsilon=1/6
and suppress the dependence of the implied constant on
\beta\in(6/13,1/2)
.

This estimate differs from its predecessors, Propositions
5.1
and
5.4
, in important respects.
First, as
\ell
increases, we do not show a higher order of vanishing at the origin
per se
.
Rather, we show that we gain a positive power of
\nu
.
In other words, we do not describe the germ of
{u}_{k}^{\textnormal{out}}
at the origin, but content ourselves with uniform improvement in the region
\mathcal{I}_{\beta}\setminus\{\mathfrak{e}\leq\nu^{1/2}\}
.
The latter is simpler to show and suffices for our purposes.
Note that we cut out the region
\mathcal{I}_{1/2}
where the viscosity is nonperturbative.
The approximations underlying the grid break down in
\mathcal{I}_{1/2}
, and we make no use of bulk grid terms there.

Second, the estimate (
5.25
) involves the Euclidean distance
\mathfrak{e}
in addition to the cubic distance
\mathfrak{d}
.
This reflects the transverse non-shocking characteristics, which are not adapted to
\mathfrak{d}
.
Integrating in non-shocking directions naturally leads to expressions controlled by
\mathfrak{e}
(
Cf.
Lemma
5.8
), and much of the coming analysis involves the interplay between
\mathfrak{d}
and
\mathfrak{e}
along various characteristics.

Proof.

Given
\varepsilon\in\big(0,\frac{1}{6}\big]
and
\beta\in\big(\tfrac{1}{2+\varepsilon},\tfrac{1}{2}\big)
, let
\varepsilon^{\prime}
be the mean of
\varepsilon
and
\beta^{-1}-2<\varepsilon
, so that
\varepsilon^{\prime}<\varepsilon
but
\beta>\frac{1}{2+\varepsilon^{\prime}}
.
Let
b\coloneqq\frac{2}{3}+\varepsilon^{\prime}
, so
b\in\big(\frac{2}{3},\frac{5}{6}\big)
.
We show

(5.26)

\big|\partial_{t}^{m}\partial_{x}^{n}\psi_{k,\ell}\big|\lesssim_{b,\beta,k,\ell,m,n}\nu^{(\ell+1)\beta/3-b\beta-o_{\ell}(1)}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k-m+1}\mathfrak{d}^{-3n}\quad\text{in }\mathcal{I}_{\beta}\setminus\mathcal{I}_{1/2}\,.

The proposition follows after relaxing
b
to
\frac{2}{3}+\varepsilon
.

We induct on
(k,\ell)
according to the lexicographic ordering on
\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}
, so
(k^{\prime},\ell^{\prime})<(k,\ell)
if
k^{\prime}<k
or
k^{\prime}=k
and
\ell^{\prime}<\ell
.
Fix
(k,\ell)\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}
and suppose we have shown (
5.26
) for all
(k^{\prime},\ell^{\prime})\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}
such that
(k^{\prime},\ell^{\prime})<(k,\ell)
and all
m,n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
We show (
5.26
) for
(k,\ell)
with
m=n=0
.
Derivative estimates with
m>0
or
n>0
follow from nearly identical calculations, so we only sketch their proofs.
Recall from Propositions
4.3
and
4.4
that
\omega_{k,\ell}
depends only on
\psi_{<(k,\ell)}
while
\sigma_{k,\ell}
depends also on
\omega_{k,\ell}
.
We therefore first analyze
\omega
and then
\sigma
.

Step 1: Nonshocking estimates.

Recalling (
4.16
), define
h=h_{A}+h_{B}
for

(5.27)

h_{A}\coloneqq-\sum_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq r\leq\ell\\
\kappa_{0}+\ldots+\kappa_{r}=k\\
\alpha_{0}+\ldots+\alpha_{r}=\ell-r\end{subarray}}}\,\mathcal{A}_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}\psi_{\kappa,\alpha}\quad\text{and}\quad h_{B}\coloneqq\sum_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq r\leq\ell-1\\
\kappa_{0}+\ldots+\kappa_{r}=k-1\\
\alpha_{0}+\ldots+\alpha_{r}=\ell-r-1\end{subarray}}}\,\mathcal{B}_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}\psi_{\kappa,\alpha}.

Using Lemma
5.7
, let
R\coloneqq\textbf{R}_{0}[A_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}(0),h]
and
F\coloneqq-A_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}(0)^{-1}\partial_{t}h-\nu^{-1}\delta_{k,1}F_{\ell}^{\omega},
so

(5.28)

[\partial_{t}\omega_{k,\ell}+A_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}(0)\partial_{x}](\omega_{k,\ell}-R)=F.

We first control the renormalization term
R
, which is simply a linear transformation of
h
.
We want to show that
\left|h\right|\lesssim_{k,\ell}\nu^{(\ell+1)\beta/3-b\beta-o_{\ell}(1)}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k+1}.

We begin with
h_{A}
.

Our approach depends on the number of terms in
\psi_{\kappa_{j},\alpha_{i}}
in the zeroth column or zeroth row, as we use different estimates in these cases.
Let
{C}
denote the indices
j
such that
\alpha_{j}=0
(the zeroth column),
{R}
those such that
\alpha_{j}\geq 1
and
\kappa_{j}=0
(the inviscid row), and
{B}
the remainder (the bulk).
Using Propositions
5.1
and
5.4
as well as the inductive hypothesis, we can estimate the product in
\mathcal{A}\psi_{\kappa,\alpha}
by

(5.29)

\displaystyle\prod_{j=0}^{r}

\displaystyle\psi_{\kappa_{j},\alpha_{j}}=\prod_{{C}}\psi_{\kappa_{j},0}\cdot\prod_{{R}}\psi_{0,\alpha_{j}}\cdot\prod_{{B}}\psi_{\kappa_{j},\alpha_{j}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{k,\ell}\mathfrak{d}^{-4\sum_{{C}}\kappa_{j}}\mathfrak{d}^{\left|{C}\right|}(\left|\log\mathfrak{d}\right|\mathfrak{d})^{\sum_{{R}}(\alpha_{j}+1)}\nu^{\frac{\beta}{3}\sum_{{B}}(\alpha_{j}+1)}\nu^{-b\beta\left|{B}\right|}\mathfrak{e}^{-2\sum_{{B}}\kappa_{j}}\mathfrak{e}^{\left|{B}\right|}.

On
\mathcal{I}_{\beta}\setminus\mathcal{I}_{1/2}
, we have
\left|\log\mathfrak{d}\right|\asymp\left|\log\nu\right|=\nu^{-o(1)}
.
We can thus absorb logarithmic terms in the factor
\nu^{-o_{\ell}(1)}
in (
5.26
), and we ignore them in the remainder of the proof.

To simplify (
5.29
), we extract
2k-1
factors of
\mathfrak{e}^{-1}
and show that the remaining terms involve only nonnegative powers of
\mathfrak{d}
or
\mathfrak{e}
, which we bound by suitable powers of
\nu
on
\mathcal{I}_{\beta}
.
We sometimes convert factors of
\mathfrak{d}^{-2}
to
\mathfrak{e}^{-1}
, so the precise calculation depends on the relative powers of
\mathfrak{d}
and
\mathfrak{e}
in (
5.29
).
We therefore modulate our argument depending on the balance between
{C}\cup{R}
to
{B}
.
This theme runs throughout the proof.

The terms in
h_{A}
satisfy
\left|{C}\cup{R}\cup{B}\right|=r+1
,
\sum_{j}(\alpha_{j}+1)=\ell+1
, and
\sum_{j}\kappa_{j}=k
.
If
{B}=\emptyset
, (
5.29
) simplifies.
Noting that
\alpha_{j}=0
for
j\in{C}
and
\kappa_{j}=0
for
j\in{R}
, (
3.5
) and
\mathfrak{d}\lesssim\nu^{\beta/3}
yield

(5.30)

\displaystyle\prod_{j=0}^{r}\psi_{\kappa_{j},\alpha_{j}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{k,\ell}\nu^{-o_{\ell}(1)}\mathfrak{d}^{-4k}\mathfrak{d}^{\ell+1}=\nu^{-o_{\ell}(1)}\mathfrak{d}^{\ell-1}\mathfrak{d}^{-4k+2}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{k,\ell}\nu^{(\ell-1)\beta/3-o_{\ell}(1)}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k+1}.

This is sufficiently small because
b>2/3
.
Now suppose
\left|{B}\right|\geq 1
.
Using
\mathfrak{e}\lesssim\nu^{\beta}
and
\beta<1
, we find

\displaystyle\prod_{j=0}^{r}\psi_{\kappa_{j},\alpha_{j}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{k,\ell}\nu^{-o_{\ell}(1)}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k+1}\mathfrak{d}^{\sum_{{C}\cup{R}}(\alpha_{j}+1)}\nu^{\frac{\beta}{3}\sum_{j}(\alpha_{j}+1)}\nu^{-b\beta\left|{B}\right|}\mathfrak{e}^{\left|{B}\right|-1}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{k,\ell}\nu^{-o_{\ell}(1)}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k+1}\nu^{(\ell+1)\beta/3}\nu^{(1-b)\beta\left|{B}\right|-\beta}\lesssim\nu^{(\ell+1)\beta/3-b\beta-o_{\ell}(1)}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k+1}.

We can treat
h_{B}
in (
5.27
) similarly, but we get an additional factor of
\mathfrak{d}^{-3}
from
\partial_{x}
, while
\sum_{j}(\alpha_{j}+1)=\ell
and
\sum_{j}\kappa_{j}=k-1
.
When
{B}=\emptyset
, these changes cancel and we obtain an identical bound to (
5.30
).
If
\left|{B}\right|\geq 1
, we find

\displaystyle(\partial_{x}\psi_{\kappa_{0},\alpha_{0}})\prod_{j=1}^{r}\psi_{\kappa_{j},\alpha_{j}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{k,\ell}\mathfrak{d}^{-3}\nu^{-o_{\ell}(1)}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k+3}\mathfrak{d}^{\sum_{{C}\cup{R}}(\alpha_{j}+1)}\nu^{\frac{\beta}{3}\sum_{j}(\alpha_{j}+1)}\nu^{-b\beta\left|{B}\right|}\mathfrak{e}^{\left|{B}\right|-1}

(5.31)

\displaystyle\lesssim_{k,\ell}\mathfrak{d}^{-3}\mathfrak{e}^{2}\nu^{\beta\ell/3}\nu^{-b\beta-o_{\ell}(1)}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k+1}.

Now (
3.5
) implies
\mathfrak{d}^{-3}\mathfrak{e}^{2}\lesssim\mathfrak{e}^{1/2}\lesssim\nu^{\beta/2}<\nu^{\beta/3}
, so the same bound holds.
That is:

(5.32)

\left|R\right|\lesssim\left|h\right|\lesssim_{k,\ell}\nu^{(\ell+1)\beta/3-b\beta-o_{\ell}(1)}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k+1}.

Now consider
F\coloneqq-A_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}(0)^{-1}\partial_{t}h-\nu^{-1}\delta_{k,1}F_{\ell}^{\omega}
in (
5.28
).
Throughout Propositions
5.1
and
5.4
and the inductive hypothesis,
\partial_{t}
contributes
\mathfrak{e}^{-1}
, so

\left|\partial_{t}h\right|\lesssim_{k,\ell}\nu^{(\ell+1)\beta/3-b\beta-o_{\ell}(1)}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k}.

Meanwhile, when
k=1
, Proposition
5.1
states that the residue
\nu^{-1}F_{\ell}^{\omega}
contributes
\nu^{-1-o(1)}\mathfrak{d}^{\ell-1}
.
Using
1\leq(2+b-2/3)\beta
, we find

\nu^{-1-o(1)}\mathfrak{d}^{\ell-1}\lesssim_{\ell}\nu^{-(2+b-2/3)\beta+(\ell-1)\beta/3-o(1)}\lesssim\nu^{(\ell+1)\beta/3-b\beta-o(1)}\mathfrak{e}^{-2}.

So this contribution is no worse, and we obtain

(5.33)

\left|F\right|\lesssim_{k,\ell}\nu^{(\ell+1)\beta/3-b\beta-o_{\ell}(1)}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k}.

The above control of the inviscid residue
F_{\ell}
is the only step requiring
\beta\geq\frac{1}{2+\varepsilon}
.
It therefore determines the lower bound on
\beta
throughout the paper.

Fix
2\leq I^{\prime}\leq N
.
Using the transport equation (
5.28
), we can write

(5.34)

\omega_{k,\ell}^{I^{\prime}}=\omega_{k,\ell}^{I^{\prime}}|_{\Gamma_{\beta}}+R^{I^{\prime}}-R^{I^{\prime}}|_{\Gamma_{\beta}}+\int_{\Gamma_{\beta}}^{\cdot}F^{I^{\prime}}\circ\gamma_{I^{\prime}},

where
\gamma_{I^{\prime}}
denotes the characteristic of
\partial_{t}+\lambda_{(I^{\prime})}(0)\partial_{x}
and the integral limits indicate we integrate from
\Gamma_{\beta}
to the implied argument
(t,x)
of
\omega_{k,\ell}
.
Using (
4.20
), Proposition
5.6
, and
b>2/3
,

\left|\omega_{k,\ell}|_{\Gamma_{\beta}}\right|\lesssim_{k}\nu^{-(2k-1)\beta}\lesssim\nu^{(1+1)\beta/3-b\beta-o(1)}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k+1}\quad\text{if }\ell=1

and
\omega_{k,\ell}|_{\Gamma_{\beta}}=0
otherwise.
Next, (
5.32
) yields

\left|R-R|_{\Gamma_{\beta}}\right|\lesssim_{k,\ell}\nu^{(\ell+1)\beta/3-b\beta-o_{\ell}(1)}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k+1}.

Finally, we apply (
5.17
) from Lemma
5.8
to (
5.33
) to find

\Big|\int_{\Gamma_{\beta}}^{\cdot}F^{I^{\prime}}\circ\gamma_{I^{\prime}}\Big|\lesssim_{k,\ell}\nu^{(\ell+1)\beta/3-b\beta-o_{\ell}(1)}\int_{\Gamma_{\beta}}^{\cdot}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k}\circ\gamma_{I^{\prime}}\lesssim_{k,\ell}\nu^{(\ell+1)\beta/3-b\beta-o_{\ell}(1)}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k+1}.

Now (
5.34
) yields
|\omega_{k,\ell}|\lesssim_{k,\ell}\nu^{(\ell+1)\beta/3-b\beta-o_{\ell}(1)}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k+1}.

We briefly discuss the corresponding derivative bounds.
Fix
m,n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and apply
\partial_{t}^{m}\partial_{n}^{x}
to (
4.16
).
Using Lemma
5.7
, we can write

[\partial_{t}+A_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}(0)](\partial_{t}^{m}\partial_{x}^{n}\omega_{k,\ell}-R_{m,n})=F_{m,n}

for

\displaystyle R_{m,n}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\textbf{R}_{n}[A_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}(0),\partial_{t}^{m}h]-\textbf{R}_{n-1}[A_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}(0),\nu^{-1}\delta_{k,1}\partial_{t}^{m}F_{\ell}^{\omega}],

\displaystyle F_{m,n}

\displaystyle\coloneqq(-1)^{n+1}A_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}(0)^{-(n+1)}\partial_{t}^{m+n+1}h+(-1)^{n+1}\nu^{-1}\delta_{k,1}A_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}(0)^{-n}\partial_{t}^{m+n}F_{\ell}^{\omega}.

By Propositions
5.1
and
5.4
and the inductive hypothesis,
\partial_{t}
and
\partial_{x}
contribute factors of
\mathfrak{e}^{-1}
and
\mathfrak{d}^{-3}
, respectively.
Repeating our analysis with these additional factors, we find

\displaystyle|R_{m,n}|

\displaystyle\lesssim_{k,\ell,m,n}\nu^{(\ell+1)\beta/3-b\beta-o_{\ell}(1)}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k-m+1}\mathfrak{d}^{-3n},

\displaystyle|F_{m,n}|

\displaystyle\lesssim_{k,\ell,m,n}\nu^{(\ell+1)\beta/3-b\beta-o_{\ell}(1)}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k-m-n}.

To complete the picture, we must control the derivative data, namely
\partial_{t}^{m}\partial_{x}^{n}\omega_{k,\ell}|_{\Gamma_{\beta}}
.
We illustrate the procedure for
\mathrm{D}\omega_{k,\ell}
; higher derivatives follow from induction.
The equation (
5.28
) provides a linear combination of
\partial_{x}(\omega_{k,\ell}-R)
and
\partial_{t}(\omega_{k,\ell}-R)
.
Moreover, we can differentiate
\omega_{k,\ell}-R
along
\Gamma_{\beta}
to obtain a second, linearly independent combination of the same quantities.
By construction,
\Gamma_{\beta}
is uniformly timelike with respect to the characteristics of
A
.
This ensures that we can invert the above linear relations to uniformly express
\mathrm{D}(\omega_{k,\ell}-R)
in terms of
F
and the derivative of
\omega_{k,\ell}-R
along
\Gamma_{\beta}
.
Using (
4.20
), (
5.12
), and (
5.33
), we obtain

(5.35)

\mathrm{D}(\omega_{k,\ell}-R)|_{\Gamma_{\beta}}\lesssim_{k,\ell}\nu^{(\ell+1)\beta/3-b\beta-o_{\ell}(1)}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k}.

Differentiating
R
, we obtain the desired bounds on
\partial_{t}\omega_{k,\ell}|_{\Gamma_{\beta}}
and
\partial_{x}\omega_{k,\ell}|_{\Gamma_{\beta}}
.
Integrating as in (
5.34
), we extend these bounds to the interior
\mathcal{I}_{\beta}
, and to higher derivatives by induction.
This completes the analysis of the nonshocking component (Step 1).

Step 2: Estimates on the shocking component.

We recycle notation from Step 1.
Recalling (
4.17
), define
F\coloneqq F_{\omega}+F_{A}+F_{B}-\nu^{-1}\delta_{k,1}F_{\ell}^{\sigma}
for
F_{\omega}\coloneqq-\partial_{1}A_{1}^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}(0)\partial_{X}(\sigma_{0,0}\omega_{k,\ell})
,

F_{A}\coloneqq-\partial_{x}\sum_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq r\leq\ell+1\\
\kappa_{0}+\ldots+\kappa_{r}=k\\
\alpha_{0}+\ldots+\alpha_{r}=\ell-r+1\\
(\kappa_{j},\alpha_{j})\neq(k,\ell)\end{subarray}}}\,\mathcal{A}_{1}\psi_{\kappa,\alpha}\quad\text{and}\quad F_{B}\coloneqq\partial_{x}\sum_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq r\leq\ell\\
\kappa_{0}+\ldots+\kappa_{r}=k-1\\
\alpha_{0}+\ldots+\alpha_{r}=\ell-r\end{subarray}}}\,\mathcal{B}_{1}\psi_{\kappa,\alpha}.

Then we can write (
4.17
) as

(5.36)

\partial_{t}\sigma_{k,\ell}+\partial_{1}A_{1}^{1}(0)\partial_{x}(\sigma_{0,0}\sigma_{k,\ell})=F.

Recall that each
\partial_{x}
contributes a factor of
\mathfrak{d}^{-3}
.
Using Step 1, the first term
F_{\omega}
in
h
is simple to bound:
F_{\omega}\lesssim_{k,\ell}\mathfrak{d}^{-2}\nu^{(\ell-2)\beta/3-b\beta-o_{\ell}(1)}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k+1}.

To treat
F_{A}
, we follow Step 1 and write

(5.37)

\displaystyle\hskip-7.0pt\prod_{j=0}^{r}\psi_{\alpha_{j},\kappa_{j}}\!\lesssim_{\ell,k}\nu^{-o_{\ell}(1)}\mathfrak{d}^{-4\sum_{{C}}\kappa_{j}}\mathfrak{d}^{\sum_{{C}\cup{R}}(\alpha_{j}+1)}\nu^{\frac{\beta}{3}\sum_{{B}}(\alpha_{j}+1)}\nu^{-b\beta\left|{B}\right|}\mathfrak{e}^{-2\sum_{{B}}\kappa_{j}}\mathfrak{e}^{\left|{B}\right|}.

In
h_{A}
,
\left|{C}\cup{R}\cup{B}\right|=r+1\geq 2
,
\sum_{j}\kappa_{j}=k
, and
\sum_{j}(\alpha_{j}+1)=\ell+2
.
We analyze (
5.37
) by cases depending on
\left|{B}\right|
.
If
{B}=\emptyset
, we follow (
5.30
) and find

\displaystyle\prod_{j=0}^{r}\psi_{\alpha_{j},\kappa_{j}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\ell,k}\nu^{-o_{\ell}(1)}\mathfrak{d}^{-4k}\mathfrak{d}^{\ell+2}=\mathfrak{d}\nu^{-o_{\ell}(1)}\mathfrak{d}^{\ell-1}\mathfrak{d}^{-4k+2}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{k,\ell}\mathfrak{d}\nu^{(\ell+1)\beta/3-b\beta-o_{\ell}(1)}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k+1}.

If
\left|{B}\right|=1
, then
\left|{C}\cup{R}\right|=r+1-\left|{B}\right|\geq 1
, so
\sum_{{C}\cup{R}}(\alpha_{j}+1)\geq 1
and

\displaystyle\prod_{j=0}^{r}\psi_{\alpha_{j},\kappa_{j}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\ell,k}\mathfrak{d}\nu^{-o_{\ell}(1)}\mathfrak{d}^{\sum_{{C}\cup{R}}(\alpha_{j}+1)-1}\nu^{\frac{\beta}{3}\sum_{{B}}(\alpha_{j}+1)}\nu^{-b\beta}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k+1}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{k,\ell}\mathfrak{d}\nu^{(\ell+1)\beta/3-b\beta-o_{\ell}(1)}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k+1}.

Finally, if
\left|{B}\right|\geq 2
, we have

\displaystyle\prod_{j=0}^{r}\psi_{\alpha_{j},\kappa_{j}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\ell,k}\mathfrak{e}^{1/2}\nu^{(\ell+2)\beta/3-b\beta\left|{B}\right|-o_{\ell}(1)}\mathfrak{e}^{\left|{B}\right|-3/2}\!\mathfrak{e}^{-2k+1}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{k,\ell}\mathfrak{d}\nu^{(\ell+1)\beta/3-o_{\ell}(1)}\nu^{[1/3+(1-b)\left|{B}\right|-3/2]\beta}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k+1}.

Now
b<5/6
and
\left|{B}\right|\geq 2
, so
(1-b)\left|{B}\right|\geq 2(1-b)
and

b+\frac{1}{3}+2(1-b)-\frac{3}{2}=5/6-b>0.

It follows that

(5.38)

\prod_{j=0}^{r}\psi_{\alpha_{j},\kappa_{j}}\lesssim_{k,\ell}\mathfrak{d}\nu^{(\ell+1)\beta/3-b\beta-o_{\ell}(1)}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k+1}.

Collecting these bounds and applying
\partial_{x}
, we have shown that

F_{A}\lesssim_{k,\ell}\mathfrak{d}^{-2}\nu^{(\ell+1)\beta/3-b\beta-o_{\ell}(1)}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k+1}.

We note that
\left|{B}\right|\geq 2
is the only case that demands
b\leq 5/6
, and thus
\varepsilon\leq 1/6
.
We cannot be too lax with
\varepsilon
lest repeated products of (
5.25
) become unmanageable.

Now consider
F_{B}
, for which
r\geq 0
,
\sum_{j}\kappa_{j}=k-1
,
\sum_{j}(\alpha_{j}+1)=\ell+1
, and we pick up an additional factor of
\mathfrak{d}^{-3}
from
\partial_{x}
.
We have

\displaystyle(\partial_{x}\psi_{\kappa_{0},\alpha_{0}})\prod_{j=1}^{r}

\displaystyle\psi_{\kappa_{j},\alpha_{j}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{k,\ell}\nu^{-o(1)}\mathfrak{d}^{-3}\mathfrak{d}^{-4\sum_{{C}}\kappa_{j}}\mathfrak{d}^{\left|{C}\cup{R}\right|}\mathfrak{d}^{\sum_{{R}}\alpha_{j}}\nu^{\frac{\beta}{4}\sum_{{B}}\alpha_{j}}\nu^{-\beta\left|{B}\right|/2}\mathfrak{e}^{-2\sum_{{B}}\kappa_{j}}\mathfrak{e}^{\left|{B}\right|}.

When
\left|B\right|=\emptyset
, this simplifies to powers of
\mathfrak{d}
alone (and
\nu^{-o(1)}
).
The result is identical to the corresponding case in
F_{A}
.
When
\left|{B}\right|\geq 1
, we write

\displaystyle(\partial_{x}\psi_{\kappa_{0},\alpha_{0}})\prod_{j=1}^{r}

\displaystyle\psi_{\kappa_{j},\alpha_{j}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{k,\ell}\mathfrak{d}(\mathfrak{d}^{-4}\mathfrak{e}^{2})\nu^{-o_{\ell}(1)}\mathfrak{d}^{\sum_{{C}\cup{R}}(\alpha_{j}+1)}\nu^{\frac{\beta}{3}\sum_{{B}}(\alpha_{j}+1)}\nu^{-b\beta\left|{B}\right|}\mathfrak{e}^{\left|{B}\right|-1}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k+1}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{k,\ell}\mathfrak{d}\nu^{(\ell+1)\beta/3-b\beta-o_{\ell}(1)}\nu^{(1-b)(\left|{B}\right|-1)\beta}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k+1}

(5.39)

\displaystyle\lesssim_{k,\ell}\mathfrak{d}\nu^{(\ell+1)\beta/3-b\beta-o_{\ell}(1)}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k+1}.

Applying
\partial_{x}
, we see that
F_{\omega}+F_{A}+F_{B}\lesssim_{k,\ell}\mathfrak{d}^{-2}\nu^{(\ell+1)\beta/3-b\beta-o_{\ell}(1)}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k+1}.

In Step 1, Proposition
5.1
provided
\nu^{-1}\delta_{k,1}F_{\ell}^{\omega}\lesssim\nu^{(\ell+1)\beta/3-b\beta-o_{\ell}(1)}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k}.

Because
F_{\ell}^{\sigma}
is smaller than
F_{\ell}^{\omega}
by a factor of
\mathfrak{d}
, (
3.5
) yields

\nu^{-1}\delta_{k,1}F_{\ell}^{\sigma}\lesssim_{\ell}\mathfrak{d}\nu^{(\ell+1)\beta/3-b\beta-o_{\ell}(1)}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k}\lesssim_{\ell}\mathfrak{d}^{-2}\nu^{(\ell+1)\beta/3-b\beta-o_{\ell}(1)}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k+1}.

Hence (
5.36
) yields

\partial_{t}\sigma_{k,\ell}+\partial_{1}A_{1}^{1}(0)\partial_{x}(\sigma_{0,0}\sigma_{k,\ell})=F\lesssim_{k,\ell}\mathfrak{d}^{-2}\nu^{(\ell+1)\beta/3-b\beta-o_{\ell}(1)}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k+1}.

We now follow the derivation of (
5.10
) to write this as

(5.40)

\overline{\partial}_{t}(\mathfrak{d}^{2}\sigma_{k,\ell})=\mathfrak{d}^{2}F\lesssim_{k,\ell}\nu^{(\ell+1)\beta/3-b\beta-o_{\ell}(1)}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k+1},

where
\overline{\partial}_{t}=\partial_{t}+\partial_{1}A_{1}^{1}(0)\sigma_{0,0}\partial_{x}
and
\mathfrak{d}^{2}=\mathfrak{m}^{-1}
.
Integrating along the corresponding shocking characteristic
\gamma_{1}
, we find

\sigma_{k,\ell}=\sigma_{k,\ell}|_{\Gamma_{\beta}}+\mathfrak{d}^{-2}\int_{\Gamma_{\beta}}^{\cdot}(\mathfrak{d}^{2}F)\circ\gamma_{1}.

Using (
4.20
), Lemma
5.8
, and (
5.40
), we obtain
|\sigma_{k,\ell}|\lesssim_{k,\ell}\nu^{(\ell+1)\beta/3-b\beta-o_{\ell}(1)}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k+1}
, as desired.
We note from (
5.20
) that we pick up a factor of
\left|\log\mathfrak{e}\right|\lesssim\left|\log\nu\right|
when
k=1
.
We absorb this in
\nu^{-o_{\ell}(1)}
.

To control derivatives of
\sigma_{k,\ell}
, we commute (
5.36
) with
\partial_{t}^{m}\partial_{x}^{n}
.
Inducting on
(m,n)
and recalling (
2.5
), we can check that

\overline{\partial}_{t}(\partial_{t}^{m}\partial_{x}^{n}\sigma_{k,\ell})-(n+1)\mathfrak{d}^{-2}\partial_{t}^{m}\partial_{x}^{n}\sigma_{k,\ell}=F_{n,m}\lesssim_{k,\ell,n,m}\nu^{(\ell+1)\beta/3-b\beta-o_{\ell}(1)}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k-m}\mathfrak{d}^{-3n}.

Multiplying by the integrating factor
\mathfrak{d}^{2(n+1)}
, this yields

\overline{\partial}_{t}(\mathfrak{d}^{2(n+1)}\partial_{t}^{m}\partial_{x}^{n}\sigma_{k,\ell})\lesssim_{k,\ell,n,m}\nu^{(\ell+1)\beta/3-b\beta-o_{\ell}(1)}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k-m}\mathfrak{d}^{2-n}.

We control the data
\sigma_{k,\ell}|_{\Gamma_{\beta}}
as described for the nonshocking components above.
By Lemma
5.8
,
|\partial_{t}^{m}\partial_{x}^{n}\sigma_{k,\ell}|\lesssim_{k,\ell,m,n}\nu^{(\ell+1)\beta/3-b\beta-o_{\ell}(1)}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k-m+1}\mathfrak{d}^{-3n}.

∎

For future convenience, we record bounds on
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
evaluated on the grid.

Lemma 5.11
.

Fix
\varepsilon\in(0,1/6]
,
\beta\in\big(\tfrac{1}{2+\varepsilon},\tfrac{1}{2}\big)
,
k,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, and
m,n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

(i)

If
\left|\alpha\right|+\#\alpha=\ell
and
\left|\kappa\right|=k
,

|\partial_{t}^{m}\partial_{x}^{n}\mathcal{A}_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}\psi_{\kappa,\alpha}|\lesssim_{\varepsilon,\beta,k,\ell,m,n}\nu^{(\ell-1-3\varepsilon)\beta/3}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k-m+1}\mathfrak{d}^{-3n.}

(ii)

If
\left|\alpha\right|+\#\alpha=\ell-1
and
\left|\kappa\right|=k-1
,

|\partial_{t}^{m}\partial_{x}^{n}\mathcal{B}_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}\psi_{\kappa,\alpha}|\lesssim_{\varepsilon,\beta,k,\ell,m,n}\nu^{(\ell-1-3\varepsilon)\beta/3}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k-m+1}\mathfrak{d}^{-3n}.

(iii)

If
\left|\alpha\right|+\#\alpha=\ell+1
and
\left|\kappa\right|=k
,

|\partial_{t}^{m}\partial_{x}^{n}\mathcal{A}_{1}\psi_{\kappa,\alpha}|\lesssim_{\varepsilon,\beta,k,\ell,m,n}\mathfrak{d}\nu^{(\ell-1-3\varepsilon)\beta/3}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k-m+1}\mathfrak{d}^{-3n}.

(iv)

If
\left|\alpha\right|+\#\alpha=\ell
and
\left|\kappa\right|=k-1
,

|\partial_{t}^{m}\partial_{x}^{n}\mathcal{B}_{1}\psi_{\kappa,\alpha}|\lesssim_{\varepsilon,\beta,k,\ell,m,n}\mathfrak{d}\nu^{(\ell-1-3\varepsilon)\beta/3}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k-m+1}\mathfrak{d}^{-3n}.

Proof.

These essentially follow from (
5.29
), (
5.31
), (
5.38
), and (
5.39
).
There are two small cases to fill in.
First, we assumed in (
5.29
) that
\#\alpha\geq 1
.
When
\#\alpha=0
, the bound follows directly from Proposition
5.10
.
Similarly, when estimating
\mathcal{A}_{1}
in (
5.38
), we omitted terms involving
\sigma_{k,\ell}
.
Now with Proposition
5.10
we can include these terms without changing the bound.
∎

6.
Outer-grid matching

We designed the grid
(\psi_{k,\ell})
so that summation in
\ell
yields
{\psi}_{k}^{\textnormal{out}}
and summation in
k
yields
{\psi}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}
.
In this section we prove the first fact: summation in
\ell
closely matches the outer expansion in the matching zone
\mathcal{I}_{\beta}
.
Given
L\in\mathbb{N}
, we study the difference
p_{k,L}
between
{\psi}_{k}^{\textnormal{out}}
and the partial sum

(6.1)

\psi_{k,[L]}\coloneqq\sum_{\ell=0}^{L}\psi_{k,\ell}.

In the inviscid
k=0
case, we already bounded
p_{0,L}
in (
5.5
) of Proposition
5.1
.
Thus we need only consider
k\geq 1
.
If
{\psi}_{k}^{\textnormal{out}}\sim\sum_{\ell\geq 0}\psi_{k,\ell}
, then we should expect
p_{k,L}
to be commensurate with the largest term omitted from (
6.1
), namely
\psi_{k,L+1}
.

Proposition 6.1
.

Fix
\varepsilon\in\big(0,\tfrac{1}{6}\big]
and
\beta\in\big(\tfrac{1}{2+\varepsilon},\tfrac{1}{2}\big)
.
For all
k,L\in\mathbb{N}
and
m,n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, the difference
p_{k,L}\coloneqq{\psi}_{k}^{\textnormal{out}}-\psi_{k,[L]}
satisfies

(6.2)

\big|\partial_{t}^{m}\partial_{x}^{n}p_{k,L}\big|\lesssim_{k,L,m,n}\nu^{(L-3\varepsilon)\beta/3}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k-m+1}\mathfrak{d}^{-3n}\quad\text{in }\mathcal{I}_{\beta}\setminus\mathcal{I}_{1/2}.

Our approach is similar to the proof of Proposition
5.6
.
Recalling the outer equation (
3.3
), we write

(6.3)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}(\psi_{k,[L]}+

\displaystyle p_{k,L})+\partial_{x}[A({\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}})(\psi_{k,[L]}+p_{k,L})]

\displaystyle=-\partial_{x}\sum_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}r\geq 1,\,\kappa_{j}\geq 1\\
\kappa_{0}+\ldots+\kappa_{r}=k\end{subarray}}}\,\frac{\mathrm{D}^{r}A({\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}})}{(r+1)!}\prod_{j=0}^{r}(\psi_{\kappa_{j},[L]}+p_{\kappa_{j},L})

\displaystyle+\partial_{x}\sum_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}r\geq 0,\,\kappa_{\geq 1}\hskip 0.5pt\geq 1\\
\kappa_{0}+\ldots+\kappa_{r}=k-1\end{subarray}}}\,\frac{\mathrm{D}^{r}B({\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}})}{r!}\partial_{x}(\psi_{\kappa_{0},[L]}+p_{\kappa_{0},L})\prod_{j=1}^{r}(\psi_{\kappa_{j},[L]}+p_{\kappa_{j},L})

\displaystyle\eqqcolon\partial_{x}(h_{0}+h_{\geq 1}).

To define
h_{0}
and
h_{\geq 1}
, we expand products on the right side and obtain terms mixing factors of
\psi_{<k,[L]}
and
p_{<k,L}
.
Let
h_{0}
denote the collection of terms with no factors of
p
and
h_{\geq 1}
the remaining terms with at least one
p
-factor.

Each difference
p
is relatively small, and we can readily bound
h_{\geq 1}
through the inductive hypothesis.
In contrast,
h_{0}
has no reason to be small.
However, it is nearly balanced by the terms involving
\psi_{k,[L]}
on the left.
Equivalently,
\psi_{k,[L]}
approximately solves (
3.3
).

Together, these observations imply that
\partial_{t}p_{k,L}+\partial_{x}[A({\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}})p_{k,L}]\approx 0
.
We chose grid data in (
4.20
) so that
p_{k,[L]}=0
on
\Gamma_{\beta}
.
Integrating, we conclude that
p_{k,L}
is small.
Throughout, we focus on the case
m=n=0
in Proposition
6.1
.
Derivative estimates follow from very similar calculations.

6.1.
Difference estimates

We first control the terms
h_{\geq 1}
in the right side of (
6.3
) containing at least one factor of
p
.
We begin with a simple bound on the partial sums
\psi_{k,[L]}
.

Lemma 6.2
.

For all
L\in\mathbb{N}
and
k,m,n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

(6.4)

\big|\partial_{t}^{m}\partial_{x}^{n}\psi_{k,[L]}\big|\lesssim_{k,L,m,n}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k-m}\mathfrak{d}^{-3n+1}.

Proof.

We show that the bound (
6.4
) holds for each
\psi_{k,\ell}
.
Using (
3.5
) and Propositions
5.1
and
5.4
, it is immediate when
k
or
\ell
vanishes.
For the remaining cases, we observe that (
6.1
) and
\mathfrak{e}\lesssim\nu^{\beta}
imply that
\nu^{-\beta/6}\mathfrak{e}\lesssim\mathfrak{e}^{5/6}\lesssim\mathfrak{e}^{1/2}\lesssim\mathfrak{d}
.
Hence when
k,\ell\geq 1
, Proposition
5.10
yields

\begin{aligned} \big|\partial_{t}^{m}\partial_{x}^{n}\psi_{k,\ell}\big|&\lesssim_{k,\ell,m,n}\nu^{(\ell-3/2)\beta 3}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k-m+1}\mathfrak{d}^{-3n}\lesssim_{k,\ell,m,n}\nu^{-\beta/6}\mathfrak{e}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k-m}\mathfrak{d}^{-3n}\\
&\lesssim_{k,\ell,m,n}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k-m}\mathfrak{d}^{-3n+1}.\end{aligned}\qed

We can now bound the terms on the right side of (
6.3
) containing factors of
p
.

Lemma 6.3
.

Fix
k,L\in\mathbb{N}
.
If Proposition
6.1
holds for all
k^{\prime}<k
, then for all
m,n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

\big|\partial_{t}^{m}\partial_{x}^{n}h_{\geq 1}\big|\lesssim_{k,L,m,n}\nu^{(L-3\varepsilon)\beta/3}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k-m+1}\mathfrak{d}^{-3n+1}.

Proof.

Comparing (
6.2
) and (
6.4
), we see that the inductive bound on
p_{k^{\prime},L}
is always tighter than that on
\psi_{k^{\prime},[L]}
for
k^{\prime}<k
.
So terms in
h_{\geq 1}
with multiple factors of
p
are easier to control than those with exactly one.
We therefore only explicitly analyze terms with exactly one factor of
p_{<k,L}
.

First consider the contribution to
h_{\geq 1}
from
A
.
Given
0\leq i\leq r
, the inductive hypothesis (
6.2
