Collecting
p
-terms in the matching residue and using Proposition
7.11
, the largest is

\partial_{x}p\lesssim_{K,L}\nu^{(1-2\beta)K-3/4}+\nu^{L\beta/3-1/2}.

Derivative estimates follow as usual.
∎

For the sake of brevity, we write
S_{0}^{(\nu)}
for
S_{0}(\varphi^{(\nu)})
,
{S}_{0}^{\textnormal{app}}
for
S_{0}({\varphi}_{[K,L]}^{\textnormal{app}})
, and similarly for
S_{1}
and
Z
.
Taking the difference between (
8.3
) and (
8.5
), we obtain:

Corollary 8.4
.

Fix
\beta\in(6/13,1/2)
and let
S_{0},S_{1},Z,
and
F
be as in Propositions
8.2
and
8.3
.
Then there exist smooth 3-tensor-valued functions
Q_{0},Q_{1},
and
Q_{Z}
such that
v(t_{0},\,\cdot\,)\equiv 0
and

\displaystyle\big[S_{0}^{(\nu)}\partial_{t}+S_{1}^{(\nu)}\partial_{x}\big]v

\displaystyle+\big[Q_{0}({\varphi}_{[K,L]}^{\textnormal{app}},\varphi^{(\nu)})(\partial_{t}{\varphi}_{[K,L]}^{\textnormal{app}})+Q_{1}({\varphi}_{[K,L]}^{\textnormal{app}},\varphi^{(\nu)})(\partial_{x}{\varphi}_{[K,L]}^{\textnormal{app}})\big]v

(8.7)

\displaystyle=\nu\iota\partial_{x}\big[Z^{(\nu)}\partial_{x}\Pi v+Q_{Z}({\varphi}_{[K,L]}^{\textnormal{app}},\varphi^{(\nu)})(\partial_{x}\Pi{\varphi}_{[K,L]}^{\textnormal{app}})v\big]+F.

Here
Q_{0}
,
Q_{1}
, and
Q_{Z}
represent suitable difference quotients of
S_{0},S_{1}
, and
Z
.
By (
8.4
), these functions are uniformly smooth in our domain.

We now prove an energy estimate for the difference equation (
8.4
).

Lemma 8.5
.

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, there exists
C(n)>0
such that for all
t\in[t_{0},t_{\mathrm{b}}]
,

\|\partial_{x}^{n}v\|_{L_{x}^{2}}\!(t)\lesssim_{K,L,n}\mathrm{e}^{C\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\big(\|\mathrm{D}{\varphi}_{[K,L]}^{\textnormal{app}}\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}}+\left\|\mathrm{D}v\right\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}}+\nu\|\partial_{x}{\varphi}_{[K,L]}^{\textnormal{app}}\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}}^{2}+\nu\|\partial_{x}v\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}}^{2}\big)}\sup_{[t_{0},t]}\left\|F_{n}\right\|_{L_{x}^{2}},

for
F_{n}^{2}\lesssim(\partial_{x}^{n}F)^{2}+\sum_{n^{\prime}=0}^{n-1}[H_{n,n^{\prime}}\partial_{x}^{n^{\prime}}v]^{2}
, where
H_{n,n^{\prime}}
is a function of polynomial growth in
{\varphi}_{[K,L]}^{\textnormal{app}},\ldots,\partial_{x}^{n+2}{\varphi}_{[K,L]}^{\textnormal{app}}
and
v,\ldots,\partial_{x}^{n^{\prime}-1}v
.

In fact, given that we assume (
8.4
), there is no need for
\left\|\mathrm{D}v\right\|
in the above exponent, as it is bounded by
1
.
We include it to highlight the structure of the argument and the importance of the bootstrap.

Proof.

This essentially shown in Section 3.1 of
[
68
]
.
Here we sketch details salient to our context.
We apply
\partial_{x}^{n}
to (
8.4
), take its inner product in
\mathcal{V}
against
\partial_{x}^{n}v
, and integrate in space.
Applications of the product rule, integration by parts, and Young’s inequality produce “forcing” terms that only involve derivatives of
v
of order
n-1
or less.
We group these in
F_{n}
.
Because
v
appears in all but the last term of (
8.4
), the only term in
F_{n}
with no factor of
\partial_{x}^{n^{\prime}}v
is
\partial_{x}^{n}F
.
This explains the functional form of
F_{n}
in the lemma.

We now explain each term in the Grönwall exponent.
The difference equation (
8.4
) includes the zeroth-order term
\big(Q_{0}\partial_{t}{\varphi}_{[K,L]}^{\textnormal{app}}+Q_{1}\partial_{x}{\varphi}_{[K,L]}^{\textnormal{app}}\big)v
, which yields
\|\mathrm{D}{\varphi}_{[K,L]}^{\textnormal{app}}\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}}
in Grönwall.
When
n\geq 1
, derivatives can land on
S_{i}
, producing terms such as
\mathrm{D}S_{1}\partial_{x}v\partial_{x}^{n}v
.
These contribute
\|\mathrm{D}v\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}}
in Grönwall.

The quadratic terms in the exponent arise from the diffusion, and thus include factors of
\nu
.
To treat the diffusion in our energy estimate, we observe that it is a total derivative.
Integrating by parts, we obtain

-\nu\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{x}^{n+1}(\Pi v)\cdot\partial_{x}^{n}\big[Z^{(\nu)}\partial_{x}\Pi v+Q_{Z}\partial_{x}(\Pi{\varphi}_{[K,L]}^{\textnormal{app}})v\big]\;\mathrm{d}x.

When
\partial_{x}^{n}
lands on
\partial_{x}\Pi v
in the first term, we obtain
-\nu\int\partial_{x}^{n+1}(\Pi v)\cdot Z^{(\nu)}\partial_{x}^{n+1}(\Pi v)
.
Because
Z^{(\nu)}
is positive on the range of
\Pi
, this is equivalent to
-\nu\|\partial_{x}^{n+1}\Pi v\|_{L_{x}^{2}}^{2}
.
We use this favorable term to absorb lower-order contributions.

For example, if
\partial_{x}^{n}
lands on
v
in the second term, Young’s inequality yields

\nu\partial_{x}^{n+1}(\Pi v)Q_{Z}\partial_{x}(\Pi{\varphi}_{[K,L]}^{\textnormal{app}})\partial_{x}^{n}v\leq\nu\delta(\partial_{x}^{n+1}\Pi v)^{2}+\nu\delta^{-1}Q_{Z}^{2}(\partial_{x}\Pi{\varphi}_{[K,L]}^{\textnormal{app}})^{2}(\partial_{x}^{n}v)^{2}.

Choosing
\delta>0
small relative to the ellipticity constant of
Z^{(\nu)}
, we can absorb the former into our favorable term.
The latter is controlled by
\nu\|\partial_{x}{\varphi}_{[K,L]}^{\textnormal{app}}\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}}^{2}(\partial_{x}^{n}v)^{2}
, which contributes
\nu\|\partial_{x}{\varphi}_{[K,L]}^{\textnormal{app}}\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}}^{2}
in Grönwall.

If
\partial_{x}^{n}
instead lands on
Z^{(\nu)}
, we obtain a term of the form

\nu\partial_{x}^{n+1}(\Pi v)\mathrm{D}Z\partial_{x}(\Pi v)\partial_{x}^{n}v.

An identical computation shows that we can coercively absorb the higher-order part, and the lower-order part contributes
\nu\|\partial_{x}v\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}}^{2}
to Grönwall.

Finally, it is straightforward to check that all terms in the calculation can be controlled by one of the above approaches.
∎

To apply this energy estimate, we require bounds on
\mathrm{D}{\varphi}_{[K,L]}^{\textnormal{app}}
.

Lemma 8.6
.

For all
K,L\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
t\in[t_{0},0]
,

\|\mathrm{D}{\varphi}_{[K,L]}^{\textnormal{app}}\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}}(t)\lesssim_{K,L}(\left|t\right|\vee\nu^{1/2})^{-1}.

Proof.

First consider the outer sum
{\psi}_{[K]}^{\textnormal{out}}=\sum_{k=0}^{K}\nu^{k}{\psi}_{k}^{\textnormal{out}}
where
\mathfrak{e}\gtrsim\nu^{\beta}
.
By (
5.12
),
\mathrm{D}{\psi}_{k}^{\textnormal{out}}=\mathrm{D}\psi_{k,0}+\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{e}^{-2k+1}\mathfrak{d}^{-3})
.
Note that
\nu\mathfrak{d}^{-4}\lesssim\nu\mathfrak{e}^{-2}\lesssim\nu^{1-2\beta}\lesssim 1.

Because
\psi_{k,0}
is
(-4k+1)
-homogeneous,
\nu^{k}\mathrm{D}\psi_{k,0}\lesssim_{k}\nu^{k}\mathfrak{d}^{-4k-2}\lesssim_{k}\mathfrak{d}^{-2}
.
Treating the error,

\nu^{k}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k+1}\mathfrak{d}^{-3}\lesssim_{k}(\nu\mathfrak{e}^{-2})^{k}\mathfrak{e}\mathfrak{d}^{-3}\lesssim_{k}\mathfrak{d}^{-1}\lesssim\mathfrak{d}^{-2}.

Therefore
|{\psi}_{[K]}^{\textnormal{out}}|\lesssim_{K}\mathfrak{d}^{-2}
where
\mathfrak{e}\gtrsim\nu^{\beta}
.

Next consider the inner sum
{\psi}_{[L]}^{\textnormal{in}}=\sum_{\ell=0}^{K}{\psi}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}
where
\mathfrak{e}\lesssim\nu^{\beta}
.
Interpreting Proposition
7.2
in the outer coordinates, we find
|\mathrm{D}{\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}|\lesssim(\mathfrak{d}\vee\nu^{1/4})^{-2}
.
For higher inner terms, (
7.8
) yields

|\mathrm{D}{\psi}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}|\lesssim_{\ell}\left|\log\nu\right|^{\ell-1}\nu^{(\ell+1)\beta/3}(\mathfrak{d}\vee\nu^{1/4})^{-3}.

Now
2\beta/3>4/13>1/4
, so

|\mathrm{D}{\psi}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}|\lesssim_{\ell,\varepsilon}\nu^{(\ell+1)\beta/3-\varepsilon}(\mathfrak{d}\vee\nu^{1/4})^{-3}\lesssim_{\ell}\nu^{1/4}(\mathfrak{d}\vee\nu^{1/4})^{-3}\lesssim(\mathfrak{d}\vee\nu^{1/4})^{-2}.

We conclude that
|\mathrm{D}{\psi}_{[L]}^{\textnormal{in}}|\lesssim_{L}(\mathfrak{d}\vee\nu^{1/4})^{-2}
.

Our matching estimates imply that
{\psi}_{[K]}^{\textnormal{out}}
and
{\psi}_{[L]}^{\textnormal{in}}
agree with
\psi_{[K,L]}
to higher order where
\mathfrak{e}\asymp\nu^{\beta}
, so the cutoff in
{\psi}_{[K,L]}^{\textnormal{app}}
does not change the estimate:

|\mathrm{D}{\psi}_{[K,L]}^{\textnormal{app}}|\lesssim_{K,L}(\mathfrak{d}\vee\nu^{1/4})^{-2}\lesssim(\left|t\right|\vee\nu^{1/2})^{-1}.

Finally, the transformation
\mathsf{H}
is smooth, so the same bound holds for
\mathrm{D}{\varphi}_{[K,L]}^{\textnormal{app}}
.
∎

We now show that the difference
v
is small.

Theorem 8.7
.

Fix
\beta\in(6/13,1/2)
.
There exists a sequence of exponents
(\Lambda_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
depending only on
t_{0}
,
A,

B
, and
\mathring{\psi}
such that for all
K,L,n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
\nu\in(0,1]
,

\|v\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}H_{x}^{n}([t_{0},0]\times\mathbb{R})}\lesssim_{K,L,n}\nu^{\min\{\!(1-2\beta)K,\,L\beta/3\}-\Lambda_{n}}.

Proof.

Suppose for the moment that the bootstrap (
8.4
) holds on
[t_{0},t]
for some
t\in(t_{0},0].

Using Lemma
8.6
in Lemma
8.5
, we see that

\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\big(\|\mathrm{D}{\varphi}_{[K,L]}^{\textnormal{app}}\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}}+\left\|\mathrm{D}v\right\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}}+\nu\|\partial_{x}{\varphi}_{[K,L]}^{\textnormal{app}}\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}}^{2}+\nu\|\partial_{x}v\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}}^{2}\big)\lesssim\log\nu^{-1}.

Thus Lemma
8.5
implies that
\|\partial_{x}^{n}v\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}L_{x}^{2}}\lesssim\nu^{-C(n)}\|F_{n}\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}L_{x}^{2}}
, where the norms apply on
[t_{0},t]\times\mathbb{R}
.
Recall that
F_{n}
depends polynomially on
\partial_{x}^{n}F
, derivatives of
{\phi}_{[K,L]}^{\textnormal{app}}
, and lower derivatives of
v
.
We controlled
\partial_{x}^{n}F
in Proposition
8.3
, and derivatives of
{\phi}_{[K,L]}^{\textnormal{app}}
, like those of
{\psi}_{[K,L]}^{\textnormal{app}}
, can be bounded by powers of
\nu^{-1}
.
Thus we lose a power of
\nu^{-1}
with each additional derivative.
Using Proposition
8.3
and induction, there exists a sequence
(\Lambda_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
independent of
K
and
L
such that

(8.8)

\|v\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}H_{x}^{n}([t_{0},t]\times\mathbb{R})}\lesssim_{K,L,n}\nu^{\min\{\!(1-2\beta)K,\,L\beta/3\}-\Lambda_{n}}.

Taking
K,L\gg 1
, we can arrange
\|v\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}H_{x}^{2}}\ll 1
.
Then Sobolev embedding yields

\left\|v\right\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}}(s)+\left\|\mathrm{D}v\right\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}}(s)\ll 1\quad\text{for all }s\in[t_{0},t].

That is, we have improved on the bootstrap (
8.4
).

Because
v(t_{0},\,\cdot\,)=0
, a standard argument from local well-posedness and continuity implies that in fact (
8.4
) holds for all
t\in(t_{0},0]
, and in particular for
t=0
.
So (
8.8
) holds on
[t_{0},0]\times\mathbb{R}
for sufficiently large
K
and
L
.
For smaller
K
and
L
, the theorem follows from the triangle inequality.
∎

8.1.
Inviscid limit

We now use Theorem
8.7
to study the inviscid limit
\psi^{(\nu)}\to\psi^{(0)}
first in
L^{\infty}
, then in Hölder norms.

Proof of Corollary
1.4
.

By Theorem
8.7
and Sobolev embedding, we can choose
\beta\in(6/13,1/2)
and
K,L\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
such that
\|\psi^{(\nu)}-{\psi}_{[K,L]}^{\textnormal{app}}\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\nu^{1/3}
, where the norm applies to
[t_{0},0]\times\mathbb{R}
.
Consider the difference
{\psi}_{[K,L]}^{\textnormal{app}}-{\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}}
.
In the outer region where
\mathfrak{e}\gtrsim\nu^{\beta}
,
{\psi}_{[K]}^{\textnormal{out}}-{\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\nu^{k}{\psi}_{k}^{\textnormal{out}}
.
Using (
3.6
), this partial sum is dominated by the
k=1
term:

{\psi}_{[K]}^{\textnormal{out}}-{\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}}\lesssim\nu\mathfrak{d}^{-3}+\nu\left|\log\nu\right|\mathfrak{e}^{-1}\lesssim\nu^{1/4}.

Here we use (
3.5
) and
\mathfrak{e}\gtrsim\nu^{\beta}\geq\nu^{1/2}
.
Next, in the inner region where
\mathfrak{e}\lesssim\nu^{\beta}
, we can write

(8.9)

{\psi}_{[L]}^{\textnormal{in}}-{\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}}=\sum_{\ell=0}^{L}({\psi}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}-\psi_{0,\ell})-({\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}}-\psi_{0,[L]}).

By Proposition
5.1
,
{\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}}-\psi_{0,[L]}
is quite small once
L
is large, so we need only treat
{\psi}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}-\psi_{0,\ell}
.
At leading order, Proposition
7.2
implies that

{\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}-\psi_{0,0}\lesssim\nu(\mathfrak{d}\vee\nu^{1/4})^{-3}\lesssim\nu^{1/4}.

For
\ell\geq 1
, Proposition
5.1
, Corollary
7.3
, and the triangle inequality yield

{\psi}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}-\psi_{0,\ell}\lesssim\left|\log\nu\right|\nu^{2\beta/3}\lesssim\nu^{1/4}.

Since
{\psi}_{[K,L]}^{\textnormal{app}}
is a convex combination of
{\psi}_{[K]}^{\textnormal{out}}
and
{\psi}_{[L]}^{\textnormal{in}}
in the matching zone, the upper bound in the corollary follows.

For the lower bound, we work with the shocking component in the diffusive zone
\{\mathfrak{d}\leq\nu^{1/4}\},
which we implicitly assume in the coming bounds.
In this region
{\sigma}_{[K,L]}^{\textnormal{app}}={\sigma}_{[L]}^{\textnormal{in}}
, so by our choice of
K
and
L
,
\sigma^{(\nu)}={\sigma}_{[L]}^{\textnormal{in}}+\mathcal{O}(\nu^{1/3})
.
Using (
7.8
) from Lemma
7.3
, we see that

\sigma^{(\nu)}={\sigma}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}+\mathcal{O}(\nu^{1/3}).

On the other hand,
(H6)
yields
\sigma^{(0)}=\sigma_{0,0}+\mathcal{O}(\nu^{1/2})
, so

(8.10)

\sigma^{(\nu)}-\sigma^{(0)}={\sigma}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}-\sigma_{0,0}+\mathcal{O}(\nu^{1/3}).

It thus suffices to show that
|{\sigma}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}-\sigma_{0,0}|\gtrsim\nu^{1/4}
somewhere in the diffusive zone.

In the inner coordinates,
\partial_{X}{\Sigma}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}\lesssim 1
while
\partial_{X}\Sigma_{0,0}\gg 1
near the origin.
Hence there exists
X_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\mathfrak{D}(0,X_{1})\leq 1
and
{\Sigma}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}(0,X_{1})\neq\Sigma_{0,0}(0,X_{1})
.
Scaling to the original coordinates, it follows that

(8.11)

|{\sigma}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}(0,x_{1})-\sigma_{0,0}(0,x_{1})|\gtrsim\nu^{1/4}

for
x_{1}\coloneqq\nu^{3/4}X_{1}
.
Since
\mathfrak{d}(0,x_{1})\leq\nu^{1/4}
, the lower bound in the corollary follows from (
8.10
).
∎

For our Hölder estimates, we make the following simple observation:

Lemma 8.8
.

If
\left|\partial_{x}f\right|\lesssim\mathfrak{d}^{-a}
for some
a\in[0,3)
, then
f\in L_{t}^{\infty}\mathcal{C}_{x}^{1-a/3}
.

Proof.

Fix
t<0
and let
g(x)\coloneqq f(t,x)
, so
\partial_{x}g\lesssim\left|x\right|^{-a/3}
.
Let
b\coloneqq 1-a/3
, and first suppose
y>x\geq 0
.
Integrating, we see that
g(y)-g(x)\lesssim y^{b}-x^{b}\leq(y-x)^{b}
, where the last inequality comes from comparing the derivative of
z^{b}
on different intervals of width
y-x
.
If
y>0>x
, we instead write

g(y)-g(x)=[g(y)-g(0)]+[g(0)-g(x)]\lesssim y^{b}+\left|x\right|^{b}\leq 2(y+\left|x\right|)^{b}=(y-x)^{b}.

The remaining cases follow symmetrically.
∎

We now examine the vanishing viscosity limit in Hölder spaces.
We do not pursue sharp decay rates in
\nu
, but they can in principle be obtained from Theorem
8.7
.

Proof of Corollary
1.5
.

Fix
\delta\in(0,1/3)
and take
\beta\in(\max\{6/13,(2+\delta)^{-1}\},1/2)
.
Using this
\beta
in Theorem
8.7
, Morrey’s inequality implies that we can choose
K,L
sufficiently large that
\psi^{(\nu)}-{\psi}_{[K,L]}^{\textnormal{app}}\to 0
in
L_{t}^{\infty}\mathcal{C}_{x}^{2/3}
.
It thus suffices to consider
{\psi}^{\textnormal{app}}\coloneqq{\psi}_{[K,L]}^{\textnormal{app}}
.
Recall that
\psi^{(0)}={\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}}
.

In the outer region
\mathfrak{e}\gtrsim\nu^{\beta}
,
{\psi}_{[K]}^{\textnormal{out}}-{\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\nu^{k}{\psi}_{k}^{\textnormal{out}}
.
Note that (
3.5
) yields
\mathfrak{d}\gtrsim\nu^{\beta/2}\gg\nu^{1/4}
.
For the leading parts
\nu^{k}\psi_{k,0}
,
(-4k+1)
-homogeneity implies that

\nu^{k}\partial_{x}\psi_{k,0}\lesssim_{k}\nu^{k}\mathfrak{d}^{-4k-2}\lesssim\nu^{\eta}\mathfrak{d}^{-2}

for some
\eta(\delta,\beta)>0
that may change from line to line.
By (
3.5
) and Proposition
5.6
,

(8.12)

\nu^{k}\partial_{x}({\psi}_{k}^{\textnormal{out}}-\psi_{k,0})\lesssim_{k}\left|\log\nu\right|\nu^{k}\mathfrak{e}^{-2k+1}\mathfrak{d}^{-3}\lesssim\nu^{\eta}\mathfrak{e}\mathfrak{d}^{-3}\lesssim\nu^{\eta}\mathfrak{d}^{-1}.

So
\partial_{x}({\psi}_{[K]}^{\textnormal{out}}-{\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}})\lesssim\nu^{\eta}\mathfrak{d}^{-2}
in the outer region.

In the inner region where
\mathfrak{e}\lesssim\nu^{\beta}
, we use (
8.9
).
By Proposition
5.1
,
{\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}}-\psi_{0,[L]}
is quite regular once
L
is large, so we need only treat
{\psi}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}-\psi_{0,\ell}
.
At leading order, Proposition
7.2
implies that

\partial_{x}({\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}-\psi_{0,0})\lesssim\nu(\mathfrak{d}\vee\nu^{1/4})^{-6}\lesssim(\mathfrak{d}\vee\nu^{1/4})^{-2}\lesssim\nu^{\eta}\mathfrak{d}^{-2-3\delta}.

For
\ell\geq 1
, we take
\varepsilon=\delta
in Corollary
7.3
to find

\partial_{x}({\psi}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}-{\psi}_{0,\ell}^{\textnormal{cut}})\lesssim\nu^{(1-\delta)/2}(\mathfrak{d}\vee\nu^{1/4})^{-3},

recalling that
{\psi}_{0,\ell}^{\textnormal{cut}}
is
\psi_{0,\ell}
but cut off where
\mathfrak{d}\leq\nu^{1/4}
.
By Proposition
5.1
,

\partial_{x}({\psi}_{0,\ell}^{\textnormal{cut}}-\psi_{0,\ell})\lesssim\mathfrak{d}^{-1}\textup{{1}}_{\mathfrak{d}\leq\nu^{1/4}}.

Thus by the triangle inequality,

\partial_{x}({\psi}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}-\psi_{0,\ell})\lesssim\nu^{(1-\delta)/2}(\mathfrak{d}\vee\nu^{1/4})^{-3}+\mathfrak{d}^{-1}\textup{{1}}_{\mathfrak{d}\leq\nu^{1/4}}.

Now

\nu^{(1-\delta)/2}(\mathfrak{d}\vee\nu^{1/4})^{-3}=\nu^{\delta/4}(\nu^{1/4})^{-2-3\delta}(\mathfrak{d}\vee\nu^{1/4})^{-3}\lesssim\nu^{\eta}\mathfrak{d}^{-1-3\delta}

and

\mathfrak{d}^{-1}\textup{{1}}_{\mathfrak{d}\leq\nu^{1/4}}\leq\nu^{3\delta/4}\mathfrak{d}^{-1-3\delta}\textup{{1}}_{\mathfrak{d}\leq\nu^{1/4}}\leq\nu^{\eta}\mathfrak{d}^{-1-3\delta}.

We conclude that

(8.13)

\partial_{x}({\psi}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{in}}-\psi_{0,\ell})\lesssim\nu^{\eta}\mathfrak{d}^{-1-3\delta}.

It only remains to consider the effect of the cutoff
\zeta
in the matching zone where
\mathfrak{e}\asymp\nu^{\beta}
.
Using (
8.1
), we must bound
({\psi}_{[K]}^{\textnormal{out}}-{\psi}_{[L]}^{\textnormal{in}})\partial_{x}\zeta
.
But
\partial_{x}\zeta\asymp\nu^{-\beta}
, and Proposition
7.11
ensures that we can choose
K,L\gg 1
such that the matching error
{\psi}_{[K]}^{\textnormal{out}}-{\psi}_{[L]}^{\textnormal{in}}
is much less than
\nu^{\beta}
.
So this term is suitably small, and we see that
\partial_{x}({\psi}^{\textnormal{app}}-{\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}})\lesssim\nu^{\eta}\mathfrak{d}^{-2-3\delta}
.
By Lemma
8.8
,

\|{\psi}^{\textnormal{app}}-{\psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}}\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}\mathcal{C}_{x}^{1/3-\delta}}\lesssim\nu^{\eta}.

Since
\delta>0
can be arbitrarily small, we have shown that
\psi^{(\nu)}\to\psi^{(0)}
in
L_{t}^{\infty}\mathcal{C}_{x}^{1/3-}
as
\nu\to 0
.
In particular, the same holds for the shocking component
\sigma
.

We next consider the nonshocking components.
Recall that the entire zeroth column (
\ell=0
) of the grid and inner expansion has only a shocking component.
In the absence of
\psi_{k,0}
, (
8.12
) implies that

\partial_{x}({\omega}_{[K]}^{\textnormal{out}}-{\omega}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}})\lesssim\nu^{\eta}\mathfrak{d}^{-1}

in the outer region where
\mathfrak{e}\gtrsim\nu^{\beta}
.
In the inner region, we use (
8.9
) and ignore the pure-shocking
\ell=0
terms.
For
\ell\geq 1
, (
8.13
) yields

\partial_{x}({\omega}_{[L]}^{\textnormal{in}}-{\omega}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}})\lesssim\nu^{\eta}\mathfrak{d}^{-1-3\delta}.

Contributions from
\zeta
in the matching region can be treated as before, so we find

\partial_{x}(\omega^{(\nu)}-\omega^{(0)})\lesssim\nu^{\eta}\mathfrak{d}^{-1-3\delta}.

By Lemma
8.8
,
\omega^{(\nu)}\to\omega^{(0)}
in
L_{t}^{\infty}\mathcal{C}_{x}^{2/3-\delta}
.

To conclude, we must show that
\sigma
does not converge in
\mathcal{C}^{1/3}.

By Theorem
8.7
, we can choose
K,L
sufficiently large that
\psi^{(\nu)}-{\psi}_{[K,L]}^{\textnormal{app}}\to 0
in
L_{t}^{\infty}\mathcal{C}_{x}^{1/3}
.
Because
{\psi}_{[K,L]}^{\textnormal{app}}={\psi}_{[L]}^{\textnormal{in}}
in the diffusive zone
\{\mathfrak{d}\leq\nu^{1/4}\}
, it suffices to show that
{\sigma}_{[L]}^{\textnormal{in}}-{\sigma}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}}\not\to 0
in
L_{t}^{\infty}\mathcal{C}_{x}^{1/3}(\{\mathfrak{d}\leq\nu^{1/4}\})
.

Let
w\coloneqq{\sigma}_{[L]}^{\textnormal{in}}-{\sigma}_{0}^{\textnormal{out}}.

By (
8.10
),
w={\sigma}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}-\sigma_{0,0}+\mathcal{O}(\nu^{1/3})
.
Both
{\sigma}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}
and
\sigma_{0,0}
are odd in
x
, so
{\sigma}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}(0,0)=\sigma_{0,0}(0,0)=0
.
Combining these observations with (
8.11
), we see that

\left|w(0,x_{1})-w(0,0)\right|\gtrsim\nu^{1/4}

for some
x_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\mathfrak{d}(0,x_{1})\leq\nu^{1/4}
.
It follows that
\left|x_{1}\right|\lesssim\nu^{3/4}
, so

\frac{\left|w(0,x_{1})-w(0,0)\right|}{\left|x_{1}\right|^{1/3}}\gtrsim 1.

This implies that
\sigma^{(\nu)}\not\to\sigma^{(0)}
in
L_{t}^{\infty}\mathcal{C}_{x}^{1/3}
.
∎

Remark 8.1
.

As noted in the introduction, the
2/3
regularity threshold for
\omega
may seem somewhat lax.
Indeed, by Theorem 4.2 of
[
7
]
, the true nonshocking components
l^{I^{\prime}}(\psi^{(0)})\partial_{x}\psi^{(0)}
are smooth in the eikonal coordinates
(\tau,u)
.
Because the eikonal function
u
is
\mathcal{C}_{x}^{1/3}
, the inviscid solution has a
\mathcal{C}_{x}^{1,1/3}
-character in the nonshocking tangent directions.
We emphasize that this result requires the use of the variable left eigenvector
l^{I^{\prime}}(\psi^{(0)})
of
A(\psi^{(0)})
rather than the frozen vector
l^{I^{\prime}}(0)
used to define
\omega
.
Thus, the
2/3
threshold in Corollary
1.5
might appear to be an artifact of our frozen coefficients.
This is not the case.

In fact, the
2/3
threshold is intrinsic to the viscous problem, and cannot generally be improved through a redefinition of the nonshocking component.
Indeed, a principal source of irregularity in
\omega^{(\nu)}
is a diffusive cross term in (
1.1
) with schematic form
B_{1}^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}\partial_{x}^{2}\sigma
.
Because the diffusion has no reason to be diagonal in the eigenbasis of
A
, we generally expect
B_{1}^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}\neq 0
, so the second derivative of the shocking component forces the nonshocking component.
This force has order
\nu(\mathfrak{d}\vee\nu^{1/4})^{-5}
.
Through renormalization along the nonshocking characteristic, it contributes a term
\omega_{\text{cross}}
of order
\nu(\mathfrak{d}\vee\nu^{1/4})^{-2}
to the nonshocking component.
Differentiating and following the reasoning of the above proof, we find

\partial_{x}\omega_{\text{cross}}\lesssim\nu(\mathfrak{d}\vee\nu^{1/4})^{-5}\lesssim\nu^{\varepsilon}\mathfrak{d}^{-1-3\delta}.

So
\omega_{\text{cross}}
tends to zero in
\mathcal{C}_{x}^{2/3-}
, but not in
\mathcal{C}_{x}^{2/3}
.
This is not an artifact of our frozen definition of
\omega
.
Indeed, we can easily check that
l^{I^{\prime}}(\psi^{(\nu)})\partial_{x}\psi^{(\nu)}
also includes
\partial_{x}\omega_{\text{cross}}
.
It follows that
l^{I^{\prime}}(\psi^{(\nu)})\partial_{x}\psi^{(\nu)}\to l^{I^{\prime}}(\psi^{(0)})\partial_{x}\psi^{(0)}
in
L_{t}^{\infty}\mathcal{C}_{x}^{\alpha}
if and only if
\alpha<-1/3
(drawing on negative Hölder spaces).
This holds despite the fact that the limit itself is considerably more regular: as noted above,
l^{I^{\prime}}(\psi^{(0)})\partial_{x}\psi^{(0)}\in L_{t}^{\infty}\mathcal{C}_{x}^{1/3}
.

This limited degree of convergence is unavoidable unless a special relationship between
A
and
B
leads
B_{1}^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}(0)
to vanish.
For example, consider the simple system

\partial_{t}v+v\partial_{x}v=\nu\partial_{x}^{2}v\quad\text{and}\quad\partial_{t}w-\partial_{x}w=\nu\mathsf{B}\partial_{x}^{2}v,

in which viscous Burgers forces a linear transport equation through its second derivative.
If
\mathsf{B}=B_{1}^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[5]$\bm{\perp}$}}{\mbox{\smaller[6]$\bm{\perp}$}}}(0)\neq 0
, then
l^{I^{\prime}}(\psi^{(\nu)})\partial_{x}\psi^{(\nu)}\not\to l^{I^{\prime}}(\psi^{(0)})\partial_{x}\psi^{(0)}
in
L_{t}^{\infty}\mathcal{C}_{x}^{-1/3}
.
(The eigenspace of
A
is constant in this problem, so
l^{I^{\prime}}(\psi)\partial_{x}\psi=\partial_{x}\omega=\partial_{x}w
.)
On the other hand, if
\mathsf{B}=0
,
B
is diagonal in the eigenbasis of
A
.
Then
\omega_{\text{cross}}
does not arise, and
\partial_{x}w^{(\nu)}\to\partial_{x}\omega^{(0)}
in
L_{t}^{\infty}\mathcal{C}_{x}^{1/3-}
.
Indeed, in this simple problem we have
\partial_{x}w^{(\nu)}=\partial_{x}\omega^{(0)}
when
\mathsf{B}=0
.

Finally, we prove that
\Psi^{(\nu)}
has a universal limit as
\nu\to 0
.

Proof of Corollary
1.6
.

Let
\mathcal{K}\subset(-\infty,0]\times\mathbb{R}
be compact.
By Theorem
8.7
, we can choose
K
and
L
sufficiently large that
\Psi^{(\nu)}={\Psi}_{[L]}^{\textnormal{in}}+\mathchoice{\scalebox{.9}{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}{\scalebox{.9}{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}{\scalebox{.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}{\scalebox{.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(1)
in
\mathcal{K}
.
Moreover, Proposition
7.1
implies that
{\Psi}_{[L]}^{\textnormal{in}}={\Psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}+\mathchoice{\scalebox{.9}{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}{\scalebox{.9}{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}{\scalebox{.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}{\scalebox{.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(1)
.
Now
{\Psi}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}={\Sigma}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}e_{1}
, so
\Psi^{(\nu)}\to{\Sigma}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}e_{1}
locally uniformly as
\nu\to 0
.
By (
4.8
),
{\Sigma}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}
satisfies viscous Burgers.
Precisely, it is the unique solution of viscous Burgers that converges to the cubic
\mathfrak{u}(T,X)
at infinity with error
\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{D}^{-3})

[
22
, Proposition 4.2]
.

This solution is parametrized by three scalars:
a,b\neq 0
in (
2.3
) and the shocking viscosity
B_{1}^{1}(0)>0
in (
4.8
).
(It may appear that the advective coefficient
\partial_{1}A_{1}^{1}(0)
is a fourth parameter, but this is bound to
a
through the relation
a=-\partial_{1}A_{1}^{1}(0)
.)
Through an appropriate scaling of
T,X
, and
\Sigma
, we can set these parameters to
1
.
Thus
{\Sigma}_{0}^{\textnormal{in}}
is unique modulo scaling.
∎
